When and Why is Optimistic Multiplicative Weights Slow? The Geometry of Energy Dissipation
John Lazarsfeld 1 Anas Barakat 1 Georgios Piliouras 2 Antonios Varvitsiotis 1,3,4 Andre Wibisono 5

Abstract
This paper studies the convergence of the Optimistic Multiplicative Weights Update algorithm (OMWU) in two-player zero-sum games. Recent works have identified instances on which the last-iterate of OMWU can converge arbitrarily slowly, but understanding when and why this slow convergence occurs has remained open. In this work, we develop a new analysis framework that gives sharp, quantitative explanations for this behavior. Our analysis is based on viewing the algorithm’s dual iterates as an optimistic skew-gradient descent with respect to an energy function. We prove over the dual iterates that energy is dissipative , and by establishing tight bounds on the magnitude of dissipation, our analysis quantifies the geometric bottlenecks that arise when the corresponding primal iterates are close to the simplex boundary. This further translates into a new linear last-iterate convergence rate in KL divergence on games with a unique and interior Nash equilibrium. Compared to prior work, this new rate contains a much sharper dependence on game-specific constants, and we prove this dependence is optimal. Moreover, these geometric insights further translate into new separations on uniform convergence rates for OMWU. On the one hand, we prove constant lower bounds on the uniform best-iterate convergence rate in KL divergence and total variation distance from Nash. On the other hand, we establish for the 2 × 2 2\times 2 setting a new O ~ ( T − 1 / 2 ) \widetilde{O}(T^{-1/2}) best-iterate rate in duality gap, improving substantially over prior work. Together, this shows in general that uniform convergence rate guarantees do not transfer across different measures of distance to Nash. Figure 1: The primal and dual iterates of OMWU on two instances of a 2 × 2 2\times 2 zero-sum game. The algorithm is run for T = 1500 T=1500 iterations with stepsize η = 0.2 \eta=0.2 in both instances. The Nash equilibrium (and corresponding dual equilibrium point) is denoted by the blue star; the OMWU initializations by the green circle; the T T ’th primal and dual iterates by the red square. In the left instance, the Nash equilibrium (NE) is p ⋆ = q ⋆ = ( 0.5 , 0.5 ) p^{\star}=q^{\star}=(0.5,0.5) and the primal initialization is ( 0.25 , 0.25 ) (0.25,0.25) . In the right instance, the NE is p ⋆ = q ⋆ = ( 0.9 , 0.1 ) p^{\star}=q^{\star}=(0.9,0.1) and the primal initialization is ( 0.5 , 0.5 ) (0.5,0.5) . Note that the algorithm’s dual iterates are completely determined by the primal iterates. In both instances, the left subplot shows the trajectory of the primal iterates overlayed on the levelsets of KL divergence from NE , and the right subplot shows the trajectory of the dual iterates overlayed on the levelsets of the log-sum-exp energy function . In the right instance, the last-iterate convergence to NE is significantly slower due to the lower energy dissipation over the dual iterates.

1  Introduction

Optimistic Multiplicative Weights Update (OMWU)
is among the most prominent algorithms for online
learning in games.
While the standard Multiplicative Weights Update (MWU)
algorithm diverges to the simplex boundary
in two-player zero-sum games
(
Bailey and Piliouras, 2018
)
,
the optimistic variant
converges in last-iterate
to a Nash equilibrium

(
Daskalakis and Panageas, 2019
;
Wei et al., 2021
)
.
This desirable convergence property
has led OMWU to gain increasing relevance
in machine learning
applications ranging from adversarial training

(
Daskalakis et al., 2018
;
Mertikopoulos et al., 2019
)
,
to zero-sum Markov games
(
Cen et al., 2023
)
,
and Nash learning from human feedback

(
Munos et al., 2024
;
Tiapkin et al., 2026
)
.

However, recent work has identified instances on which
the last-iterate convergence of OMWU
can be arbitrarily slow
(
Cai et al., 2024
)
,
raising new doubts on
the algorithm’s reliability for fast learning in games.
Moreover, this finding has highlighted the fact that,
despite an extensive recent literature studying the

average-iterate
convergence behavior of
OMWU (
Syrgkanis et al., 2015
;
Daskalakis et al., 2021
;
Anagnostides et al., 2022a
),
and despite the long history of optimistic algorithms in general,
1
1

1

OMWU is the canonical instantiation
of the Optimistic Follow-the-Regularized Leader (OFTRL)
family with
entropic regularization
(
Rakhlin and Sridharan, 2013
)
,
and this family itself has its roots in
optimistic and extragradient methods
for solving monotone variational inequalities
(see, e.g.,
Korpelevich, 1976
,

Popov, 1980
,

Nemirovski, 2004
,
Nesterov, 2007
).

obtaining a fine-grained
and quantitative understanding of the

last-iterate
behavior of Optimistic MWU
has still remained surprisingly elusive,
even in the most fundamental constrained setting
of two-player bilinear zero-sum games.

By way of background,
the
asymptotic
last-iterate convergence
of OMWU to a unique Nash equilibrium
in zero-sum games was first proven
by
Daskalakis and Panageas, 2019
.
This result was later strengthened by
Wei et al., 2021
,
who proved a non-asymptotic
linear

last-iterate convergence rate
for the same setting,
but with a bound containing constant
factors that depend on the game’s smallest
non-zero equilibrium mass
\delta
.
Such bounds are called
universal

convergence rates, and they imply that the algorithm’s
performance can vary dramatically depending on
the magnitude of
\delta
in a specific game instance.
However, whether this potentially poor scaling in their analysis
accurately captures the
true iterate-to-iterate behavior of the algorithm
was left unanswered.

Towards this direction,
Cai et al., 2024

recently gave new evidence that OMWU
can indeed

converge arbitrarily slowly:
for every time horizon
T
,
they proved that the last iterate
of OMWU on a
2\times 2
instance
remains an absolute constant
distance away from equilibrium in duality gap.
While this confirms for fixed time horizons
that the last-iterate of OMWU can remain far from Nash,
it does not explain what
features
of a specific game instance
and what
algorithmic properties
of OMWU govern its
possibly slow iterate-to-iterate behavior.
Moreover, whether the analysis of
Wei et al., 2021

– which involves tracking distances between

primal
iterates –
and its dependence on
\delta
is quantitatively
sharp still remains unresolved.
Thus, the central focus of our paper is to
address the following fundamental question:

When and why can the last-iterate convergence
of OMWU be arbitrarily slow in zero-sum games?

1.1  Our Contributions and Techniques

Our main contributions provide sharp, quantitative
answers to this question.

New Analysis Framework via Dual Energy Dissipation.

For zero-sum games with
a unique and interior Nash equilibrium,
we develop a new framework for analyzing
the last-iterate convergence of OMWU.
We analyze the
dual iterates

of OMWU in the unconstrained space of payoff vectors,
where we show the algorithm follows an

optimistic skew-gradient descent

with respect to the
log-sum-exp energy function
.
This energy function is the convex conjugate of
the negative entropy regularizer,
and we show that minimizing energy over the
dual iterates corresponds to minimizing KL divergence
from Nash equilibrium
over the primal iterates
(Proposition
3.2

and Proposition
D.11
).

Under this dual perspective,
we prove that
energy is dissipative

over the iterates of OMWU,
which is in stark contrast to the behavior of the
standard MWU variant.
For OMWU, we obtain
tight
upper and
lower bounds on the one-step dissipation
in energy, the strength of which is
quantified in the local geometry of the
energy function Hessian
(Lemma
4.1
).
Specifically, we relate the magnitude of dual energy dissipation
to the primal KL divergence from Nash
by proving a novel, general

skew-gradient domination
inequality
(Proposition
4.4
)
that may be of independent interest.
Together, these results give a new and more
clear description of the geometric properties
that lead to the algorithm’s slow behavior:
when the primal iterate is near the simplex boundary,
the dual iterate moves in directions
over which the energy function is extremely flat,
thus leading to vanishingly small magnitudes
of energy dissipation.
As the decrease in energy is equivalent to
decrease in KL divergence from Nash,
a geometric bottleneck arises when the
primal iterates spend many steps near the boundary
of the simplex, a property that emerges
when the game’s equilibrium is
also close to the boundary.

Tight Universal Last-Iterate Convergence Rates.

Our results on energy dissipation further
translate into a new
and optimal
linear last-iterate convergence rate

in KL divergence for zero-sum games with
a unique and interior Nash equilibrium
(Theorem
4.7
).
This new universal rate has a significantly sharper
dependence on
\delta

(the minimum non-zero Nash equilibrium coordinate)
compared to the prior rate of
Wei et al., 2021
.
In particular, it improves by a leading multiplicative factor of

\exp(\frac{1}{\delta})
, a term that is unbounded as
\delta\to 0
.
While our new bound can still decay
slowly as
\delta
grows small, we prove a lower bound
on the last-iterate convergence rate
with a matching dependence on
\delta
(Theorem
4.10
), establishing in general that our new rate and analysis is tight.
Note also that upper bounds on convergence rates
in KL divergence also imply converges rates in duality gap
(see expression (
2
)).

.

,

setting.

New Separations on Uniform Convergence Guarantees.

Our geometric insights additionally yield new
contrasting bounds on
uniform convergence rates

for OMWU.
Uniform rates hold simultaneously over all instances
of a fixed dimension and
have no dependence on game-specific
constants like
\delta
.
Importantly, a potentially slow
universal

last-iterate convergence rate does not preclude
the possibility of obtaining

uniform
rates under
weaker notions of convergence.
While the result of
Cai et al., 2024

confirms that no uniform
last-iterate

rates are attainable, their followup work proved for
the
2\times 2
setting that OMWU
does have
a uniform
O(T^{-1/6})

best-iterate
rate in duality gap

(
Cai et al., 2025
)
.
Note that upper bounds on KL divergence imply upper bounds
in duality gap (see the relationships of
expression (
2
)),
but the reverse direction does not necessarily hold.
Thus, given our improved geometric understanding
and new universal last-iterate analysis
in KL, it is natural to ask:

Can we obtain any stronger uniform convergence
guarantees for OMWU?

In particular, can we strengthen the guarantee of a
uniform best-iterate convergence rate
to also hold
in
KL
divergence or total variation (
TV
) distance
from Nash?
And, can we obtain any
faster
rates than
the current
O(T^{-1/6})
bound?

Our work additionally gives answers to these questions.
On the negative side, we prove a new
separation
between
the uniform guarantees attainable in
duality gap versus
KL
divergence and
TV
distance.
Specifically, we prove that OMWU has

no uniform best-iterate convergence rate

in
KL
or
TV
(Theorem
5.1
),
meaning that on certain hard instances,
all
iterates
of OMWU over a finite time horizon remain far
from Nash under these measures.
On the other hand, in duality gap, we prove a new

\widetilde{O}(T^{-1/2})
best-iterate
convergence rate for the
2\times 2
setting
(Theorem
5.2
), a bound that
substantially improves over the prior rate
of
Cai et al., 2025
for this same regime.
Together, these results indicate that uniform
convergence guarantees do not
translate between different measures of distance to Nash,
a distinction that arises due to the inherent relationship
between OMWU and
KL
divergence,
and the fundamental geometric differences between
the level sets of
KL
divergence,
TV
distance, and
duality gap over the simplex
(see Section
I.4.4

for a visual comparison).

Ultimately, our new techniques
and results open the door to
a broader, quantitative
study of last-iterate learning in games
that is both physics-informed
and geometry-aware.
We summarize our new rates in Table
1
,
and we defer a more detailed discussion
of other related work to Section
A
.

2  Preliminaries

Notations.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
[k]=\{1,\dots,k\}
.
Let
\Delta_{k}
denote the probability simplex in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
and let
\textnormal{ri}(\Delta_{k})=\{p\in\Delta_{k}:0<p(i)<1\}

denote its relative interior.
For
p\in\textnormal{ri}(\Delta_{k})
, we say that

p
is
interior
or
fully mixed
.
For
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, we write

\langle u,v\rangle=u^{\top}v=\sum_{i=1}^{k}u(i)\cdot v(i)

to denote the
\ell_{2}
inner product.
We write
\mathbf{1}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
to denote the
vector of all ones.

Online learning in zero-sum games.

We study the setting where
two players repeatedly play a zero-sum game with
payoff matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, which yields the min-max optimization problem:

\textstyle\min_{p\in\Delta_{m}}\,\max_{q\in\Delta_{n}}\,\langle p,Aq\rangle\;.

At each step
t\geq 0
the players choose strategies
p_{t}\in\Delta_{m}

and
q_{t}\in\Delta_{n}
, incur losses
\langle p_{t},Aq_{t}\rangle

and
-\langle q_{t},A^{\top}p_{t}\rangle
, and then
observe gradient feedback payoff vectors
Aq_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}

and
-A^{\top}p_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, respectively.
The players seek to converge to a Nash equilibrium (NE) of
A
.
For this, let
\mathcal{W}:=\Delta_{m}\times\Delta_{n}
.
For
\epsilon\geq 0
, an (
\epsilon
-approximate) Nash equilibrium

w=(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}
of
A
satisfies

\textstyle\max_{q^{\prime}\in\Delta_{m}}\,\langle q^{\prime},A^{\top}p\rangle-\epsilon\leq\langle p,Aq\rangle\leq\min_{p^{\prime}\in\Delta_{n}}\,\langle p^{\prime},Aq\rangle+\epsilon.

(1)

Every payoff matrix
A
has at least one
(
\epsilon=0
) Nash equilibrium

(
von Neumann, 1928
)
.

Distances to Nash equilibrium.

This work focuses on games with an
interior
Nash equilibrium.
Let
\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W}):=\textnormal{ri}(\Delta_{m})\times\textnormal{ri}(\Delta_{n})
.
For an interior NE
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})

of
A
, we measure convergence to
w^{\star}
under several different
distances. For any
w=(p,q)\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
, we define:

•

Duality gap (DG)
:

\textnormal{DG}(w)=\max_{q^{\prime}\in\Delta_{m}}\langle q^{\prime},A^{\top}p\rangle-\min_{p^{\prime}\in\Delta_{n}}\langle p^{\prime},Aq\rangle
.

•

Kullback-Leibler divergence (KL)
:
Define the components

\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}p^{\star}(i)\log\big(p^{\star}(i)/p(i)\big)
and

\textnormal{KL}(q^{\star},q)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}q^{\star}(j)\log\big(q^{\star}(j)/q(j)\big)
.
Then
\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)=\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p)+\textnormal{KL}(q^{\star},q)
.

•

Total variation distance (TV)
:
Define the components
\textnormal{TV}(p^{\star},p)=\frac{1}{2}\|p^{\star}-p\|_{1}

and
\textnormal{TV}(q^{\star},q)=\frac{1}{2}\|q^{\star}-q\|_{1}
.
Then
\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w)=\textnormal{TV}(p^{\star},p)+\textnormal{TV}(q^{\star},q)
.

If
\textnormal{DG}(w)\leq\epsilon
, then
w
is an
\epsilon
-approximate NE.
Moreover, letting
a_{\max}:=\max_{(i,j)\in[m]\times[n]}|A(i,j)|

denote the largest absolute entry of
A
, the following
relationships hold:

\textstyle\frac{1}{(2a_{\max})^{2}}\cdot\textnormal{DG}(w)^{2}\leq\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w)^{2}\leq\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)\;.

(2)

Thus, upper bounds on
\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)
or
\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w)

also correspond to approximate Nash equilibria
in the sense of (
1
).
We give the proof of (
2
)
and additional preliminaries in
Section
B
.

Additional preliminaries.

For a symmetric matrix
M=M^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
,
we write
M\succeq 0
when
M
is positive semi-definite (PSD)
and
M\succ 0
when
M
is positive definite (PD).
For a (not-necessarily-symmetric) matrix
M
,
let
\sigma_{\max}(M):=\max_{x\neq 0}\|Mx\|_{2}/\|x\|_{2}

denote its spectral norm.
For a linear subspace
\mathcal{V}
, let
\Pi_{\mathcal{V}}(\cdot)

denote the orthogonal projection operator onto
\mathcal{V}
,
and for a matrix
M
, let

\sigma_{\min}(M,\mathcal{V})=\inf_{v\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\{0\}}\|\Pi_{\mathcal{V}}(Mv)\|_{2}/\|v\|_{2}

denote its minimum singular value restricted to
\mathcal{V}
.
A matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
is
skew-symmetric

when
M^{\top}=-M
, which implies

\langle v,Mv\rangle=0
for all
v\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.
For an integer
k\geq 2
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
we write
\textnormal{softmax}_{k}(x)\in\textnormal{ri}(\Delta_{k})
to denote
the distribution with coordinates

\textnormal{softmax}_{k}(x)(i)=\exp(x(i))/(\sum_{j=1}^{k}\exp(x(j)))

for all
i\in[k]
.
For readability, we further use the notation

\textnormal{softmax}_{k}(x)(i)\propto\exp(x(i))
for
i\in[k]
.
For a distribution
p\in\textnormal{ri}(\Delta_{k})
, the discrete entropy of
p

is given by
\textnormal{Ent}_{k}(p)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}p(i)\log(1/p(i))
.
Finally, for continuously differentiable function
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
,
recall the Bregman divergence

D_{f}:\mathcal{X}\times\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathbb{R}

is given by

D_{f}(x^{\prime},x)=f(x^{\prime})-f(x)-\langle\nabla f(x),x^{\prime}-x\rangle

for all
x\in\mathcal{X},x^{\prime}\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{X})
.

3  OMWU as Optimistic Skew-Gradient Descent

In this section, we show that the dual iterates of
OMWU can be viewed as optimistic skew-gradient descent.
We start with preliminaries on OMWU and
defer a review of standard MWU to Section
C
.

Primal OMWU update rule.

The algorithm is initialized from an arbitrary

w_{0}=(p_{0},q_{0})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
Then for
t\geq 1
, using a fixed stepsize
\eta>0
,
the iterate
w_{t}=(p_{t},q_{t})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
has coordinates

\displaystyle p_{t}(i)

\displaystyle\propto\;p_{t-1}(i)\cdot\exp\big(-\eta\big(2Aq_{t-1}-Aq_{t-2}\big)(i)\big)

\displaystyle\text{$\forall i\in[m]$}\,,

(OMWU)

\displaystyle q_{t}(j)

\displaystyle\propto\;q_{t-1}(j)\cdot\exp\big(\eta\big(2A^{\top}p_{t-1}-A^{\top}p_{t-2}\big)(j)\big)

\displaystyle\text{$\forall j\in[n]$}\;.

For notational convenience, we assume
that
w_{-1}=w_{0}\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.

Dual iterates and OMWU as Optimistic FTRL.

OMWU can also be derived as an instantiation
of the Optimistic Follow-the-Regularized-Leader (OFTRL)
family using the negative entropy regularizer

(
Rakhlin and Sridharan, 2013
;
Syrgkanis et al., 2015
)
.
Specifically, under OFTRL, the primal iterates are chosen
by applying a
regularized
best-response map
to a sequence of
dual iterates
.
In the zero-sum game setting,
these primal and dual iterates
have a compact representation
that we describe here:

First, given
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
,
let
J\in\mathbb{R}^{(m+n)\times(m+n)}
be the
block skew-symmetric matrix

J=\begin{pmatrix}0&A\\
-A^{\top}&0\end{pmatrix}=-J^{\top}\,.

(3)

Under OFTRL, the primal
iterates
\{w_{t}\}
are initialized at

w_{0}=(p_{0},q_{0})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
For the dual iterates,
initialize
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}

and
y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
and let
z_{0}=z_{-1}=(x_{0},y_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
.
At time
t=1
, let
z_{1}=z_{0}-\eta Jw_{0}
.
Then at time
t+1\geq 2
, the dual iterate

z_{t+1}=(x_{t+1},y_{t+1})\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}

is

\textstyle z_{t+1}=z_{0}-\eta\big(\sum_{k=0}^{t}Jw_{k}+Jw_{t}\big)\;,

(4)

which is a sum that depends only on

w_{0},\dots,w_{t}
.
The primal iterate
at time
t+1
is defined as follows:
first, let
R_{m}:\Delta_{m}\to\mathbb{R}
and
R_{n}:\Delta_{n}\to\mathbb{R}

be a pair of strictly convex regularizers, and let
R
be the joint,
separable regularizer defined by
R(w)=R_{m}(p)+R_{n}(q)

for
w=(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}
.
Then OFTRL sets

\textstyle w_{t+1}=\mathop{\textnormal{argmin}}_{w=(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}}\,\big\{\langle w,z_{t+1}\rangle+R(w)\big\}\;.

(5)

For OMWU, the regularizer
R
is
instantiated by the

negative entropy
functions

R_{m}=-\textnormal{Ent}_{m}
and
R_{n}=-\textnormal{Ent}_{n}
.
Then, the first-order optimality
conditions of (
5
)
under this setting of
R

yield the closed-form expression

w_{t}=(p_{t},q_{t})=(\textnormal{softmax}_{m}(x_{t}),\textnormal{softmax}_{n}(y_{t}))\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
for all
t\geq 1
.
This recovers the update rule of (
OMWU
).
We review the full derivation of this equivalence
in Section
C
.

3.1  Dual OMWU Iterates as Optimistic Skew-Gradient Descent

Our analysis of OMWU is based on
viewing the dual iterates
\{z_{t}\}

as an
optimistic skew-gradient descent

with respect to an energy function
F
.
We derive this connection here:

Energy function.

Let
\textnormal{LSE}_{m}
and
\textnormal{LSE}_{n}
denote
the
m
and
n
-dimensional
log-sum-exp
functions.
For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\textnormal{LSE}_{m}(x)=\log(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\exp(x(i)))

and

\textnormal{LSE}_{n}(y)=\log(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\exp(y(j)))
.
Together, these functions define the convex,
continuously differentiable, and separable
energy function

F:\mathbb{R}^{m+n}\to\mathbb{R}
.
For
z=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
, we let

\textstyle F(z)=\textnormal{LSE}_{m}(x)+\textnormal{LSE}_{n}(y)\;.

(6)

Specifically,
F
is the
dual function

(convex conjugate) of the negative
entropy regularizer
R
,
and the gradient of
F
maps
from the dual to the primal space.
For
z=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
, define

\nabla F(z)=(\nabla_{x}F(z),\nabla_{y}F(z))
.
Then for
z\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}

and
w=(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}
such
that
p=\textnormal{softmax}_{m}(x)
and
q=\textnormal{softmax}_{n}(y)
,
we have
\nabla F(z)=w
.
The central consequence of this property
is that the dual iterates of (
4
)
can be written as a modified
skew-gradient descent

with respect to
F
. Formally:

Proposition 3.1

(Optimistic Skew-Gradient Descent)
.

Let
\{w_{t}\}
be the iterates of (
OMWU
) with
stepsize
\eta>0
initialized from
w_{0}\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
Let
\{z_{t}\}
be the dual iterates
of (
4
) initialized from
z_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}

such that
\nabla F(z_{0})=w_{0}
.
Then for all
t\geq 0
,
it holds that
w_{t}=\nabla F(z_{t})
and

z_{t+1}=\underbrace{z_{t}-\eta J\nabla F(z_{t})}_{\text{
skew-gradient descent}}-\underbrace{\eta(J\nabla F(z_{t})-J\nabla F(z_{t-1}))}_{\text{optimistic skew-gradient correction}}\;.\hskip-15.00002pt

(OMWU Dual)

Proposition
3.1

(proof in Section
C.2
)
shows that (
OMWU Dual
) consists of
a
skew-gradient descent
step with
an additional
optimistic corrective term
.
As shown by
Wibisono et al., 2022
,
the dual iterates following skew-gradient descent correspond
to the standard MWU algorithm,
which is a forward Euler discretization of
the continuous-time skew-gradient flow

\dot{z}(t)=-J\nabla F(z(t))
.
Under skew-gradient flow, energy
F
is conserved along
the trajectory, while under the forward discretization,
energy is non-decreasing
(see Section
C.2.2

for a review).
In contrast, for OMWU, we prove the correction term
leads to strict
energy dissipation
,
which will yield last-iterate convergence
to NE.

3.2  Primal Convergence
in KL via Dual Dissipation of Energy

In this section, we show that
the change in energy over the dual iterates
is exactly the change in
\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})

over the primal iterates when
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
has an interior NE
w^{\star}\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
This equivalence is due to a more general
relationship between
F
and

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},\cdot)

that holds over the
effective
space of dual iterates.
To state this relationship, we first require
introducing the linear subspaces

\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S}^{\bot}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m+n}

and several additional preliminaries. First, we
define the subspaces:

\mathcal{Z}=\textnormal{Span}(J\mathcal{W})\,,\;\mathcal{S}=\textnormal{Span}\big(\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\mathbf{1}_{m}\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\big),\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
\mathbf{1}_{n}\end{smallmatrix}\big)\big)\,,\;\text{and}\;\mathcal{S}^{\bot}=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}:\text{$\forall s\in\mathcal{S}$, }\langle v,s\rangle=0\}.

(7)

We refer to
\mathcal{Z}
as the
effective dual space
,
as, by (
4
), the dual
iterates satisfy
z_{t}\in\mathcal{Z}
for all
t\geq 1
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{S}
is the subspace of constant shift directions,
and
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
is its orthogonal complement.

Geometry of the effective dual space.

A key property is that
\mathcal{Z}
is
orthogonal
to
interior Nash equilibria of
A
.
This is because for any interior NE
w^{\star}\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
,
Ap^{\star}
and
A^{\top}q^{\star}

are the constant vectors

Ap^{\star}=c\mathbf{1}_{m}
and
A^{\top}q^{\star}=-c\mathbf{1}_{n}

for some
c\in\mathbb{R}

(see Proposition
B.1
).
Together with the skew-symmetry of
J
,
this implies
\langle z,w^{\star}\rangle=0
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
.
See Proposition
D.6
.

Primal-dual coupling despite
lack of strict convexity.

The energy function is not globally strictly
convex, as
\nabla F
is invariant under
shifts
s\in\mathcal{S}

(see Proposition
D.3
).
However, the dual relationship of

R
and
F
and the structure of the
gradient map
\nabla R

still allows for establishing the following key relationship:
for
z\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
and
w=\nabla F(z)
, it holds that

\textnormal{KL}(\widetilde{w},w)=R(\widetilde{w})+F(z)-\langle z,\widetilde{w}\rangle
for
all
\widetilde{w}\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
We state and prove this formally
in Proposition
D.2
.

Together, these properties lead to the following
relationship beween
\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},\cdot)

and
F
over
\mathcal{Z}
:

Proposition 3.2

(Equivalence between energy and KL differences)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
have an interior NE

w^{\star}\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
Fix
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}
,
and let
w=\nabla F(z)\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})

and
w^{\prime}=\nabla F(z^{\prime})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
Then it holds that

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w^{\prime})-\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)=F(z^{\prime})-F(z)\;.

The proof of the proposition is in
Section
D.2
.
As an immediate corollary,
note that as the OMWU iterates satisfy

z_{t}\in\mathcal{Z}

and
w_{t}=\nabla F(z_{t})
for all
t\geq 1
, it holds that

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t+1})-\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})=F(z_{t+1})-F(z_{t})

for all such
t
.
Moreover, we prove in
Section
D.3

that the map
\nabla F:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}^{m+n}

is
surjective

(Proposition
D.9
), and that

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)=F(z)-\min_{z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}}F(z^{\prime})

for all

z\in\mathcal{Z}
and
w=\nabla F(z)

when
w^{\star}
is unique and interior
(Proposition
D.11
).
Taken together, these propositions establish
an exact equivalence between energy dissipation
in the dual space and minimizing
KL
divergence to
NE in the primal space.

4  Tight Bounds on Energy Dissipation

In this section, we establish tight bounds on
energy dissipation under (
OMWU Dual
).
Due to the relationships discussed in
Section
3.2
,
this leads to a sharp last-iterate convergence
rate in
KL
.

4.1  One-Step Change in Energy under OMWU

We show a dissipative one-step change
in energy under (
OMWU Dual
)
that is measured using the local norm
induced by the Hessian of
F
.
For this, we first introduce the following notation:

Local norms induced by energy Hessian.

The energy function
F

is convex and continuously differentiable,
and thus for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
its
Hessian matrix
\nabla^{2}F(z)
is PSD
and induces an inner product and norm.
Specifically, we define

\langle u,v\rangle_{z}:=\langle u,\nabla^{2}F(z)v\rangle
and

\|v\|_{z}:=\sqrt{\langle v,\nabla^{2}F(z)v\rangle}

for
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
.
We refer to
\|\cdot\|_{z}
as a
local norm
,
as the spectrum of
\nabla^{2}F(z)

is dependent on the state
z
.
We give further preliminaries on the
energy Hessian in Section
E
.

Under the following constant stepsize condition,
we prove our key energy dissipation lemma.

Assumption 1

(Stepsize)
.

0<\eta\leq\frac{1}{4(54\sigma_{\max}+9)}
,
where
\sigma_{\max}=\|A\|_{2}
.

Lemma 4.1

(Energy Dissipation)
.

For (
OMWU Dual
) with

\eta
satisfying Assumption
1
,
for all
t\geq 1
:

\textstyle-\frac{5}{4}\cdot\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\;\leq\;F(z_{t+1})-F(z_{t})\;\leq\;-\frac{1}{20}\cdot\eta^{2}{}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\;.

The significance of Lemma
4.1

is due to its
exact
characterization of
the one-step change in energy,
with matching upper and lower bounds.
We make several further remarks on
its interpretation:

Dependence on local geometry.

The one-step change in energy is always
negative and proportional
to a
dissipation term

\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}
.
This term measures the magnitude of the skew-gradient
payoff vector under the
local geometry

of
\nabla^{2}F(z_{t})
.
When the energy Hessian has low curvature,
the dissipation term is small
and leads to a slow one-step change in energy.
Note that the
flatness
of
F
is dual to the

steepness
of the entropy regularizer
R
,
and thus dissipation will grow small
when
w_{t}=\nabla F(z_{t})
approaches the boundary
of the simplex, where
R
is steepest.

Primal characterization of dissipation.

The dissipation term can also be expressed as the

variance
of the payoff vector
Jw_{t}
under
w_{t}=(p_{t},q_{t})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
Specifically,
\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}=\textnormal{Var}_{p_{t}}(Aq_{t})+\textnormal{Var}_{q_{t}}(A^{\top}p_{t})
.
This characterization stems from the structure of
\nabla^{2}F
and
the relation
\nabla F(z_{t})=w_{t}
.
See Section
E.1
.

Asymptotic last-iterate convergence in KL.

Together with Proposition
3.2
,
the negative upper bound in Lemma
4.1

also immediately implies that OMWU has
asymptotic

last-iterate convergence to NE.
Formally, we have the following result
(see Section
H
for the short proof):

Theorem 4.2

(Asymptotic Last-Iterate Convergence)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
have a unique and interior
NE
w^{\star}
,
and let
\{w_{t}\}
be the iterates of (
OMWU
)
on
A
with
\eta
satisfying Assumption
1
.
Then the sequence of iterates
\{w_{t}\}
converges, and

\lim_{t\to\infty}w_{t}=w^{\star}
.

4.2  Proof Overview of Lemma
4.1

We now give the main intuitions
for proving the lemma.
The full proof is developed in Section
F
.

Leading dissipation term.

To see how the term
\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}

arises, recall from (
OMWU Dual
) that

z_{t+1}-z_{t}=-\eta J\nabla F(z_{t})-\eta J(\nabla F(z_{t})-\nabla F(z_{t-1})).

Then using a first-order Taylor expansion, the difference of
energy gradients in the corrective term can be
expressed exactly as

\textstyle\nabla F(z_{t})-\nabla F(z_{t-1})=\nabla^{2}F(z_{t})(z_{t}-z_{t-1})+G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\;,

(8)

where
G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})
is a
remainder defined precisely in
Proposition
E.4
.
By using the dual update rule at time
t
and repeating
a similar expansion, we can further express

z_{t}-z_{t-1}=-\eta J\nabla F(z_{t})-Q_{t}

for an error term
Q_{t}

(defined precisely in Proposition
F.3
).
Substituting this difference into (
8
),
and substituting (
8
) back
into the dual update rule at time
t+1
, we can ultimately write

\textstyle z_{t+1}-z_{t}=-\eta J\nabla F(z_{t})+\eta^{2}J\nabla^{2}F(z_{t})J\nabla F(z_{t})+\eta W_{t}\;,

(9)

where
W_{t}=J\nabla^{2}F(z_{t})Q_{t}-JG_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})
.
Then, using the definition of the Bregman
divergence
D_{F}
,
we substitute (
9
) to quantify the one-step change in energy:

\displaystyle F(z_{t+1})-F(z_{t})

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla F(z_{t}),z_{t+1}-z_{t}\rangle+D_{F}(z_{t+1},z_{t})

\displaystyle=-\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}+\eta\langle\nabla F(z_{t}),W_{t}\rangle+D_{F}(z_{t+1},z_{t})\;.

(10)

Here, the final equality follows
by the skew-symmetry of
J=-J^{\top}

and by definition of
\|\cdot\|_{z_{t}}
.

Controlling the error terms.

The expansion of
F(z_{t+1})-F(z_{t})

in (
10
) is exact,
and thus the remaining technical task is
to control the magnitude of the latter two terms.
In Propositions
F.7
,
F.8
,
and
F.9
,
we prove that

\eta|\langle\nabla F(z_{t}),W_{t}\rangle+D_{F}(z_{t+1},z_{t})|\leq C\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}

for an absolute constant
C<1
when

\eta
satisfies Assumption
1
.
This allows the leading dissipation term to absorb the
error terms, resulting in the statement of the lemma.
Our analysis for controlling these error terms
relies on the following

local Hessian stability
(LHS) property of
the energy function
F
:

Proposition 4.3

(LHS Property)
.

For any
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}

and
\alpha>0
,
if
\|z-z^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\alpha
, then:

\exp(-2\alpha)\cdot\nabla^{2}F(z)\preceq\nabla^{2}F(z^{\prime})\preceq\exp(2\alpha)\cdot\nabla^{2}F(z)\;.

(LHS)

Roughly speaking, the (
LHS
) property
is a local and relative notion of third-order smoothness.
The property states that the spectra of
\nabla^{2}F(z^{\prime})

and
\nabla^{2}F(z)
are
similar, up to a multiplicative factor depending
on the closeness of
z,z^{\prime}
.
The dual OMWU iterates all satisfy

\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{\infty}\leq 3\eta\sigma_{\max}

(see Proposition
F.4
), so
for a small enough constant stepsize
\eta
,
the (
LHS
) property
allows for establishing the bounds on
the error terms above.
The (
LHS
) property is also
related to
self-concordance
,
with the former often derived as a consequence of the latter.
Note that a recent work of
Freund et al., 2026

established self-concordance of the log-sum-exp function
(which implies Proposition
4.3

by definition of
F
).
For completeness, we give a short direct proof of
Proposition
4.3
in
Section
E.1
.

4.3  Universal Linear Last-Iterate Convergence Rate

In addition to the asymptotic last-iterate convergence of
Theorem
4.2
,
our new dual energy-based analysis leads to a sharp
linear last-iterate convergence rate.
This relies on a structural inequality relating

\|J\nabla F(z)\|^{2}_{z}
to
\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)
.
For this, let

\sigma_{\min}:=\sigma_{\min}(J,\mathcal{S}^{\bot})=\inf_{v\in\mathcal{S}^{\bot}\setminus\{0\}}\|\Pi_{\mathcal{S}^{\bot}}(Jv)\|_{2}/\|v\|_{2}

denote the minimum singular value of
J

restricted to
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
.
When
A
has a unique and interior NE,
then
\sigma_{\min}>0
(see Proposition
G.9
).
We then prove the following general inequality:

Proposition 4.4

(Non-uniform skew-gradient domination)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
have a unique and interior NE

w^{\star}\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
Fix
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, and let

w=(p,q)=\nabla F(z)\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
Let
p_{\min}=\min_{i\in[m]}p(i)

and
q_{\min}=\min_{j\in[n]}q(j)
,
and define
w_{\min}:=\min\{p_{\min},q_{\min}\}
.
Then
\sigma_{\min}>0
, and moreover

\|J\nabla F(z)\|^{2}_{z}\geq\sigma_{\min}^{2}\cdot w_{\min}^{2}\cdot\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)\;.

The proof of the inequality is given in
Section
G
.
Here, we make several remarks:

Remark 4.5

(Skew-gradient domination)
.

As

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)=F(z)-\min_{z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}}F(z^{\star})

(Proposition
D.11
),
note that Proposition
4.4

establishes a structural property akin to

gradient domination
in optimization,
but with key differences arising
from the non-Euclidean geometry
induced by
\|\cdot\|_{z}

and the concern of
skew
-gradients.
In particular, the relationship is constrained by
a non-uniform and state-dependent factor
w^{2}_{\min}
, a term that captures a bottleneck arising from the local geometry of
F
.
Specifically,

w_{\min}
is exactly the smallest positive
eigenvalue of
\nabla^{2}F(z)
,
and thus the curvature of
F

flattens when the corresponding primal variable
is near the simplex boundary.
In these regions, the term
\|J\nabla F(z)\|^{2}_{z}

is small even when
w
is far from Nash in
KL
.
In Proposition
J.1
,
we give an
upper bound
on
\|J\nabla F(z)\|^{2}_{z}

showing that this non-uniform dependence on
w_{\min}

is necessary.

Remark 4.6

(Independence of bottlenecks)
.

A second bottleneck arises when

J
is ill-conditioned and has a small
restricted singular value
\sigma_{\min}
.
Intuitively, this means that large primal perturbations

w-w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
lead to only small relative changes in

J(w-w^{\prime})
, which makes OMWU
less reactive to meaningful signal from payoff vectors.
Importantly,
\sigma_{\min}
is a property of
J

that is completely independent from the geometry
of
F
and the proximity of the Nash
w^{\star}
to
the simplex boundary: we show in
Propositions
G.15

and
G.16

that there are families of matrices
where (i)
w^{\star}
is uniform but
\sigma_{\min}
is arbitrarily small,
and (ii) where
\sigma_{\min}=\tfrac{1}{2}
,
but
w^{\star}
is arbitrarily close to a vertex.
See Section
G.5
.

Recall by Proposition
3.2

that
\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t+1})-\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})=F(z_{t+1})-F(z_{t})
along the OMWU iterates.
Then combining the energy dissipation
bound of Lemma
4.1

and the skew-gradient domination property of
Proposition
4.4
,
we obtain a new, linear last-iterate
convergence rate in
KL
:

Theorem 4.7

(Last-Iterate Convergence Rate in KL)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
have
a unique and interior NE
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})
.
Let
\{w_{t}\}
denote the iterates of (
OMWU
) on
A

with
\eta
satisfying Assumption
1
, initialized from
w_{0}\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
Let
\delta_{p}=\min_{i\in[m]}p^{\star}(i)
,

\delta_{q}=\min_{j\in[n]}q^{\star}(j)
, and

\delta:=\min\{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}\}
.
For every
t
,
let
p_{t,\min}=\min_{i\in[m]}p_{t}(i)
,

q_{t,\min}=\min_{j\in[n]}q_{t}(j)
, and

w_{t,\min}:=\min\{p_{t,\min},q_{t,\min}\}
.
Let

\Lambda:=\textnormal{CrossEnt}(w^{\star},w_{0})=\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{0})-R(w^{\star})
.
Then for all
t\geq 1
,
the following hold:

\displaystyle\text{(1)}\quad\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t+1})\leq\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})\cdot\left(1-\tfrac{1}{20}\cdot\eta^{2}\sigma_{\min}^{2}\cdot w_{t,\min}^{2}\right)\;,

\displaystyle\text{(2)}\quad\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t+1})\leq 2\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{0})\cdot\exp\left(-\tfrac{1}{20}\cdot\eta^{2}\sigma^{2}_{\min}\cdot\exp\left(\tfrac{-2\Lambda}{\delta}\right)\cdot t\right)\;.

The proof of the theorem is given
in Section
H
.
Here, we make the following remarks:

Remark 4.8

(Trajectory-dependence)
.

The dependence on
w_{t,\min}
in Part (1)
of the theorem highlights the influence of the
iterates’ trajectory on the rate of convergence.
The trajectory depends on the initialization
w_{0}
,
and more importantly on the location of the NE.
When
w^{\star}
is close to the simplex boundary
(meaning
\delta
is small),
the iterates
\{w_{t}\}
are also more frequently
near the boundary.
In these regions
w_{t,\min}
is small,
meaning the one-step contraction in
KL
is also small.
This compounds in slower overall convergence.
Note that the sequence
\{w_{t,\min}\}
is not monotonic.
However,
by establishing a
uniform lower bound
of

w_{t,\min}\geq\exp(\frac{-2\Lambda}{\delta})

(Lemma
H.2
)
we obtain the rate in Part (2).

Remark 4.9

(Comparison with
Wei et al., 2021
)
.

The linear rate of Wei et al. (2021, Theorem 3)
also depends on
\exp(\frac{-\Lambda}{\delta})
.
However, explicitly tracking this dependence in their proof
yields the much slower bound of

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})\leq O(\exp(\frac{\Lambda}{\delta})\cdot\exp(-\eta^{2}\exp(\frac{-\Lambda}{\delta})\cdot t))
.
Part (2) of Theorem
4.7

is thus sharper by at least a multiplicative
\exp(\frac{1}{\delta})

factor, a term that grows unbounded as
\delta\to 0
.
Moreover, our new energy-based proof technique
offers a more transparent and quantitative understanding
of the geometric bottlenecks that contribute to slow convergence.
See Section
H.4

for further comparisons.

Matching Last-Iterate Lower Bound in KL.

We further prove that the rate in
Theorem
4.7
is
optimal
.
In particular, we prove a
lower bound
on
the last-iterate convergence rate in
KL

with a matching dependence on
\delta
,
establishing that our new analysis is tight.
Formally:

Theorem 4.10

(Lower bound in KL)
.

Assume the definitions of
Theorem
4.7
.
Fix any
T\geq 3
.
Then there exists
A\in[-1,1]^{2\times 2}

with an interior NE

w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})

with
\delta:=\delta_{p}=\delta_{q}

such that, from a positive measure set of initializations
w_{0}
,
each with
\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{0})\leq 6
,
the OMWU iterates
\{w_{t}\}

with
\eta
as in Assumption
1
satisfy

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})\geq\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{0})\cdot\exp(-40\eta^{2}\exp(\tfrac{-1}{\delta})\cdot t)\;\;\;\text{for all $t\in[T]$.}

Together with Theorem
4.7
,
this lower bound indicates that the most severe
geometric bottleneck in convergence arises when

w^{\star}
is near a
vertex
of the simplex.
We give the proof in Section
J
.

5  New Bounds on Uniform Convergence Rates

We use the geometric insights
developed in Theorems
4.7
and
4.10

to additionally
prove new bounds on
uniform
convergence rates under OMWU.
For concreteness, fix the dimensions
m
and
n
,
and recall that an algorithm has a
uniform last-iterate
or

uniform best-iterate
convergence
rate of order
f(T)

under a distance measure
D
if the following holds:

for all
T\geq 1
and
for all

A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
:

\displaystyle\text{(uniform last-iterate)}:\;\;

\displaystyle D(w_{T})

\displaystyle\leq O(f(T))

or

\displaystyle\text{(uniform best-iterate)}:\;\;\min\nolimits_{t\in[T]}

\displaystyle D(w_{t})

\displaystyle\leq O(f(T))\;.

Here, we emphasize that
f
must be a function only
of the time horizon
T
and the dimensions

m
and
n
, but with
no dependence
on the
minimum coordinate
\delta
of the NE in a particular instance.
Proving such uniform rates thus allows
quantitative guarantees to hold in an instance-agnostic manner
(see also
Cai et al., 2025
for further discussion on
these different notions).

While
Cai et al., 2024
proved for OMWU that no
uniform last-iterate convergence rates are attainable,
the follow-up of
Cai et al., 2025
proved for
the class of
2\times 2
games with an interior NE,
that, when initialized at the
uniform distribution, OMWU does have a

O(T^{-1/6})

uniform best-iterate convergence rate

in
duality gap
.
Unfortunately, we prove that similar uniform best-iterate
rates are
not
attainable under
the stronger measures of
TV
distance and
KL
divergence.

Lower Bounds on Best-Iterate in KL and TV.

We prove the following
constant lower bounds
:

Theorem 5.1

(Uniform Best-Iterate Lower Bounds)
.

For every
T\geq 2
,
there exists
A\in[-1,1]^{2\times 2}

with an interior Nash equilibrium
w^{\star}
such that,
for the iterates
\{w_{t}\}
of (
OMWU
)
on
A
with
\eta
satisfying Assumption
1
,
from a positive measure set of initializations that includes
the joint uniform distributions, the following hold:

\min\nolimits_{t\in[T]}\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w_{t})\geq\tfrac{1}{3}\;\;\text{and}\;\;\min\nolimits_{t\in[T]}\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})\geq\tfrac{1}{9}\;.

Note that these lower bounds in best-iterate also
clearly imply a lower bound on last-iterate.
We give the proof and a full discussion in
Section
K
,
where we also establish an additional uniform best-iterate
lower bound in
DG
when the initialization
is not the uniform distribution
(see Theorem
K.1
).

Fast Best-Iterate Convergence Rate
in Duality Gap for 2x2 Games.

In contrast, for
2\times 2
games,
we obtain in
duality gap

a new upper bound on
uniform best-iterate convergence rate

that substantially improves over the prior
O(T^{-1/6})

rate of
Cai et al., 2025
that was proven for the same setting:

Theorem 5.2

(Uniform Best-Iterate Convergence Rate in DG)
.

Let
A\in[-1,1]^{2\times 2}
be a zero-sum game
with an interior Nash equilibrium
w^{\star}
, and let
\{w_{t}\}

denote the iterates of running (
OMWU
)
on
A
with constant stepsize
\eta
satisfying
Assumption
1
, initialized at the
uniform distributions.
Then for any
T\geq 1
, it holds that

\min\nolimits_{t\in[T]}\textnormal{DG}(w_{t})\leq O(T^{-1/2}\cdot\sqrt{\log T})\;.

See Section
L

for the proof of the theorem and a full discussion.
Notably, our proof relies on the new, universal last-iterate
analysis in KL divergence from Theorem
4.7
,
along with an exact control of the OMWU trajectory
in the
2\times 2
setting (see Section
I
).
While we prove the result only for this low-dimensional regime,
we conjecture that the same bound holds in higher dimensions.
We expect our techniques will be useful for obtaining such
a result, but we leave this for future work.

6  Conclusion

Our work obtains tight new guarantees
on the iterate-to-iterate behavior of OMWU
in two-player zero-sum games,
and it offers sharp geometric explanations
for the algorithm’s potentially slow convergence.
We believe our new energy-based analysis framework
can be naturally extended to study the
last-iterate convergence properties of other instantiations
of Optimistic FTRL,
in other settings (such as under bandit feedback),
and for other more general classes of games.
We leave these as directions for future work.

Appendix A
Other Related Work

In this section, we give additional discussion on other related works.

Optimistic algorithms for min-max optimization and
variational inequalities.

The OMWU algorithm is more broadly related to
optimistic and extra-gradient methods
in monotone
variational inequalities
(
Facchinei and Pang, 2003
)
.
Such algorithms date back to the 1970s and
80s
(
Korpelevich, 1976
;
Popov, 1980
)
,
and within the past decade have received growing theoretical interest,
in part due to their applications in machine learning settings
(see, e.g.,
Nemirovski, 2004
,
Nesterov, 2007
,

Daskalakis et al., 2018
,

Gidel et al., 2019
,
Mertikopoulos et al., 2019
,

Liang and Stokes, 2019
,

Mokhtari et al., 2020
,

Golowich et al., 2020
,
Gorbunov et al., 2022
,

Cai et al., 2022
, and references therein).

Optimistic MWU for online learning and learning in games.

The Optimistic Mirror Descent and Optimistic Follow-the-Regularized-Leader
families for adversarial online learning,
of which OMWU is the canonical instantiation with negative
entropy regularizer, were introduced by
Rakhlin and Sridharan, 2013
.
Since then, OMWU has emerged as a premier algorithm
for online learning in games, and it has been proven
to achieve optimal or near-optimal regret bounds
(which correspond to time-average convergence guarantees)
in many classes of games, including
zero-sum and general-sum normal-form games
(see, e.g.,
Syrgkanis et al., 2015
;
Chen and Peng, 2020
;
Daskalakis et al., 2021
;
Anagnostides et al., 2022a
;
Soleymani et al., 2025
,
and references therein).

Last-iterate Convergence of OMWU in zero-sum games.

The asymptotic last-iterate convergence of OMWU in two-player
bilinear zero-sum games was first established
by
Daskalakis and Panageas, 2019
on games with a unique Nash equilibrium,
and
Lei et al., 2021
generalized this result to hold
also for convex-concave zero-sum games.

Wei et al., 2021
later obtained a quantitative (universal) linear
last-iterate convergence rate in the bilinear case.
Unlike Optimstic Gradient-Descent-Ascent (OGDA),
which has a uniform
O(T^{-1/2})
last-iterate convergence
rate in this setting
(
Gorbunov et al., 2022
;
Cai et al., 2022
)
,
OMWU was shown by
Cai et al., 2024
to have no such
uniform last-iterate convergence rate in duality gap
(i.e., that does not depend on any game-dependent
constant such as the minimum non-zero Nash equilibrium mass).

Cai et al., 2024
attributed this behavior of OMWU
to a notion they call
forgetfulness
, as the algorithm’s update rule
(as is the case for all OFTRL instantiations) inherently depends
on the accumulation of all previous gradients.
Our paper reinforces the main takeaways of their result:
the last-iterate of OMWU (and, under some measures of distance to Nash,
the best-iterate convergence,
c.f. Theorem
5.1
)
can be arbitrarily slow. However, our results also give
new and more precise quantitative explanations for this slow
convergence that are related to geometric properties
of the algorithm.

Energy-based perspective for learning in games.

Several prior works have also relied on using
a dual or energy-based perspective for analyzing
the convergence of various algorithms for learning in games.
See, e.g.,
Mertikopoulos et al., 2018
,
Bailey and Piliouras, 2018
,

Bailey and Piliouras, 2019a
,
Bailey and Piliouras, 2019b
,
Bailey et al., 2020
,

Abernethy et al., 2021
,
Wibisono et al., 2022
,

Azizian et al., 2024
,

Legacci et al., 2024
,
Ota and Fujimoto, 2025
,
Lazarsfeld et al., 2025b
,

Lazarsfeld et al., 2025a

Katona et al., 2026
,
and the references therein. However, our work is the
first to prove quantitative last-iterate
convergence rates for the
bilinear zero-sum setting by proving bounds
on
strictly dissipating
energy.

Other related works on learning in games.

The extra-gradient variant of MWU has been studied in
bilinear zero-sum games and proven to
have asymptotic last-iterate convergence
(
Fasoulakis et al., 2022
)
.
In the unconstained min-max setting, optimistic
and extra-gradient methods have also been studied
in time-varying and periodic games, where
Feng et al., 2024

established a separation between the convergence guarantees
of the extra-gradient and optimistic variants.

Lee et al., 2021
proved last-iterate convergence
for a variant of OMWU in extensive-form games.

Anagnostides et al., 2022b
proved a
O(T^{-1/2})
best-iterage convergence
rate in duality gap using Optimistic Mirror Descent on games with non-negative
sums of regret (which is satisfied by zero-sum games).
However, this result assumes a smoothness condition
on the regularizer that is not satisfied by the negative entropy function.
Thus, the
\widetilde{O}(T^{-1/2})
best-iterate convergence
rate in duality gap that we prove in
Theorem
5.2
for the
2\times 2
setting
is the fastest-known best-iterate result for OMWU.
Moreover, this result also adds to a growing body of work
that has established various convergence results for algorithms
in the setting of
2\times 2
games, which often still requires
intricate proof techniques despite the low dimension

(
Bailey and Piliouras, 2019a
;
Chen and Peng, 2020
;
Cai et al., 2024
;
Lazarsfeld et al., 2025a
;
Cai et al., 2025
;
Wang et al., 2026
)
.

Appendix B
Preliminaries on Zero-Sum Games

This Section gives additional details on
the preliminaries of zero-sum games introduced
in Section
2
.

Oragnization of Section.

This section is organized as follows:

•

Section
B.1

recalls several important properties
of zero-sum games with an interior NE.

•

Section
B.2

derives the relationships between
distances to Nash introduced in expression (
2
).

•

Section
B.3

states and proves two useful properties related
to the spectrum of the payoff matrix
A
.

The claims of this section are mostly standard
but included for completeness.

B.1  Properties of Zero-Sum Games with Interior Equilibria

Recall that for
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
and
\epsilon\geq 0
,
an
\epsilon
-approximate Nash equilibrium (NE)

w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})\in\mathcal{W}
for the
min-max game
\min_{p\in\Delta_{m}}\max_{q\in\Delta_{n}}\langle p,Aq\rangle

satisfies the inequalities (see also (
1
))

\textstyle\max_{q\in\Delta_{m}}\,\langle q,A^{\top}p^{\star}\rangle-\epsilon\leq\langle p^{\star},Aq^{\star}\rangle\leq\min_{p\in\Delta_{n}}\,\langle p,Aq^{\star}\rangle+\epsilon\;.

(11)

When
\epsilon=0
, we say
w^{\star}
is a Nash equlibrium.

If a game has an interior Nash equilibrium,
then the following key properties hold:

Proposition B.1
.

Fix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
. Then the following properties hold:

(i)

Suppose
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})

is an interior NE of
A
with

\langle p^{\star},Aq^{\star}\rangle=c\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then

Aq^{\star}=c\mathbf{1}_{m}

and
A^{\top}p^{\star}=c\mathbf{1}_{n}
.

(ii)

Fix
w=(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}
.
Suppose
Aq=d\mathbf{1}_{m}

and
A^{\top}p=d^{\prime}\mathbf{1}_{n}
for
d,d^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then
d=d^{\prime}
and

w
is a Nash equilibrium of
A
.

Proof.

We prove the two claims separately:

Claim (i)
:
By the definition of an NE from (
11
),
and since
\langle p^{\star},Aq^{\star}\rangle=c
,
we must have

\textstyle c=\langle p^{\star},Aq^{\star}\rangle\leq\min_{p\in\Delta_{m}}\,\langle p,Aq^{\star}\rangle\;.

(12)

We will first show that
both
(Aq^{\star})(i)\geq c
and
(Aq^{\star})(i)\leq c
for all
i\in[m]
.
For the first direction, suppose by way of contradiction that
there exists
j\in[m]
such that
(Aq^{\star})(j)<c
.
Then construct
p^{\prime}\in\Delta_{m}
as follows:
set
p^{\prime}(j)=1
, and
p^{\prime}(i)=0
for all other
i\neq j\in[m]
.
Then observe that

\textstyle\min_{p\in\Delta_{m}}\langle p,Aq^{\star}\rangle\leq\langle p^{\prime},Aq^{\star}\rangle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}p^{\prime}(k)\cdot(Aq^{\star})(k)=(Aq^{\star})(j)<c=\langle p^{\star},Aq^{\star}\rangle\;.

However, this contradicts the fact that

\langle p^{\star},Aq^{\star}\rangle\leq\min_{p\in\Delta_{m}}\langle p,Aq^{\star}\rangle

from (
12
).
Thus we must have
(Aq^{\star})(i)\geq c

for all coordinates
i\in[m]
.

For the other direction, suppose by contradiction
that there exists
j\in[m]
where

(Aq^{\star})(j)>c
. Note that if
(Aq^{\star})(j)>c

for
all
coordinates
j\in[m]
,
then clearly
\langle p^{\star},Aq^{\star}\rangle>c
,
which contradicts (
12
).
Thus suppose instead there exists coordinate

i\neq j\in[m]
with
(Aq^{\star})(i)\leq c
.
Now construct
p^{\prime}\in\Delta_{m}
as follows:
set
p^{\prime}(k)=p^{\star}(k)
for
all
k\neq i\neq j
,
set
p^{\prime}(j)=0
, and set
p^{\prime}(i)=p^{\star}(i)+p^{\star}(j)
.
Observe by construction that this implies

\textstyle p^{\prime}(j)\big((Aq^{\star})(j)\big)+p^{\prime}(i)\big((Aq^{\star})(i)\big)<p^{\star}(j)\big(Aq^{\star}(j)\big)+p^{\star}(i)\big(Aq^{\star})(i)\big)\;.

Then this further implies by construction that

\textstyle\min_{p\in\Delta_{m}}\langle p,Aq^{\star}\rangle\leq\langle p^{\prime},Aq^{\star}\rangle<\langle p^{\star},Aq^{\star}\rangle=c\;,

which contradicts (
12
).
Thus we also have
(Aq^{\star})(i)\leq c

for all coordinates
i\in[m]
,
and thus
Aq^{\star}=c\mathbf{1}_{m}
.
Repeating identical arguments, we also have

A^{\top}p^{\star}=c\mathbf{1}_{n}
, which completes the claim.

Claim (ii).

By the assumptions of the claim that

Aq=d\mathbf{1}_{m}
and
A^{\top}p=d^{\prime}\mathbf{1}_{n}
,
observe for any
u\in\Delta_{m}
that

\langle u,Aq\rangle=\langle u,d\mathbf{1}_{m}\rangle=d
,
and for any
v\in\Delta_{n}
that

\langle v,A^{\top}p\rangle=\langle v,d^{\prime}\mathbf{1}_{n}\rangle=d^{\prime}
.
Then since
\langle p,Aq\rangle=\langle q,A^{\top}p\rangle

and both
p\in\Delta_{m}
and
q\in\Delta_{n}
,
we must have
d=d^{\prime}=\langle p,Aq\rangle
.
To further show that
(p,q)
is a Nash equilibrium,
observe that since
Aq=d\mathbf{1}_{m}

and
A^{\top}p=d\mathbf{1}_{n}
, then

\textstyle\max_{q^{\prime}\in\Delta_{n}}\langle q^{\prime},A^{\top}p\rangle=\langle q,Ap\rangle=\min_{p^{\prime}\in\Delta_{m}}\langle p^{\prime},Aq\rangle\;.

Thus by definition in (
11
),

w=(p,q)
is a Nash equilibrium of
A
.
∎

B.2  Distances to Nash Equilibrium

Recall from Section
2
that we
consider several measures of distance to Nash;
duality gap (
DG
), total variation distance (
TV
),
and KL divergence (
KL
).
We first restate these definitions.
Let
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})

be a Nash equilibrium of
A
.
Then for any
w=(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}
, we define:

•

Duality gap (DG)
:

\textnormal{DG}(w)=\max_{q^{\prime}\in\Delta_{m}}\langle q^{\prime},A^{\top}p\rangle-\min_{p^{\prime}\in\Delta_{n}}\langle p^{\prime},Aq\rangle
.

•

Kullback-Leibler divergence (KL)
:
Define the components

\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}p^{\star}(i)\log\big(p^{\star}(i)/p(i)\big)
and

\textnormal{KL}(q^{\star},q)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}q^{\star}(j)\log\big(q^{\star}(j)/q(j)\big)
.
Then
\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)=\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p)+\textnormal{KL}(q^{\star},q)
.

•

Total variation distance (TV)
:
Define the components
\textnormal{TV}(p^{\star},p)=\frac{1}{2}\|p^{\star}-p\|_{1}

and
\textnormal{TV}(q^{\star},q)=\frac{1}{2}\|q^{\star}-q\|_{1}
.
Then
\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w)=\textnormal{TV}(p^{\star},p)+\textnormal{TV}(q^{\star},q)
.

We also define the chi-squared divergence

\chi^{2}(w^{\star},w)
as follows:

•

Chi-squared divergence:

First define the components

\chi^{2}(p^{\star},p)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{(p^{\star}(i)-p(i))^{2}}{p(i)}

and

\chi^{2}(q^{\star},q)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{(q^{\star}(j)-q(j))^{2}}{q(j)}
.
Then
\chi^{2}(w^{\star},w)=\chi^{2}(p^{\star},p)+\chi^{2}(q^{\star},q)
.

B.2.1  Bounded Duality Gap Implies Approximate Nash Equilibrium

A standard fact is that a bounded duality gap implies
an approximate Nash equilibrium. Formally:

Proposition B.2

(Small DG implies Nash)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be a zero-sum game.
Fix
w=(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}
and
\epsilon\geq 0
.
If
DG(w)\leq\epsilon
, then
w
is an
\epsilon
-approximate Nash
equilibrium of
A
.

Proof.

Using the definition of duality gap and adding
and subtracting

\langle p,Aq\rangle=\langle q,A^{\top}p\rangle
,
we can write and expand

\displaystyle\textnormal{DG}(w)

\displaystyle=\max\nolimits_{q^{\prime}\in\Delta_{m}}\langle q^{\prime},A^{\top}p\rangle-\min\nolimits_{p^{\prime}\in\Delta_{n}}\langle p^{\prime},Aq\rangle

\displaystyle=\big(\max\nolimits_{q^{\prime}\in\Delta_{m}}\langle q^{\prime},A^{\top}p\rangle-\langle q,A^{\top}p\rangle\big)+\big(\langle p,Aq\rangle-\min\nolimits_{p^{\prime}\in\Delta_{n}}\langle p^{\prime},Aq\rangle\big)\;.

Observe that both terms in the above expression
are non-negative. Thus, using the assumption that

\textnormal{DG}(w)\leq\epsilon
, it follows by rearranging that

\max\nolimits_{q^{\prime}\in\Delta_{m}}\langle q^{\prime},A^{\top}p\rangle-\epsilon\leq\langle q,A^{\top}p\rangle\;\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\;\langle p,Aq\rangle\leq\min\nolimits_{p^{\prime}\in\Delta_{n}}\langle p^{\prime},Aq\rangle+\epsilon\;.

Thus
w=(p,q)
satisfies the inequalities
in (
11
) and is therefore an
\epsilon
-approximate NE
of
A
.
∎

B.2.2  Relationships between Duality Gap, KL, and TV

In this section, we prove a series of inequalities
relating the distances
DG
,
TV
,
KL
, and
\chi^{2}
.
These relationships were introduced
in expression (
2
))
from Section
2
.
Here, we give a proof of each component separately,
most of which are standard, but included for completness.
Futher below, we restate the full sequence of
inequalities from (
2
)
in Corollary (
B.6
).

Relating Duality Gap and TV Distance.

Proposition B.3

(Duality-Gap vs. TV distance)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be a zero-sum game
with Nash equilibrium
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})\in\mathcal{W}
.
Let
a_{\max}=\max_{(i,j)\in[m]\times[n]}|A(i,j)|
denote
the maximum absolute entry of
A
. Then for any

w=(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}
, it holds that:

\textnormal{DG}(w)\leq 2a_{\max}\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w)\;.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{w}=(\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q})\in\mathcal{W}
be a
pair of distributions such that

\max_{q^{\prime}\in\Delta_{m}}\langle q^{\prime},A^{\top}p\rangle=\langle\widetilde{q},A^{\top}p\rangle

and

\min_{p^{\prime}\in\Delta_{n}}\langle p^{\prime},Aq\rangle=\langle\widetilde{p},Aq\rangle
.
Then by definition of a Nash equilibrium
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})

from (
11
), it follows that

\textstyle\langle\widetilde{q},A^{\top}p^{\star}\rangle-\langle q^{\star},A^{\top}p^{\star}\rangle\leq 0\;\text{and}\;\langle\widetilde{p},Aq^{\star}\rangle-\langle p^{\star},Aq^{\star}\rangle\geq 0\;.

Let
c=\langle p^{\star},Aq^{\star}\rangle
,
and thus the above inequalities imply

\langle\widetilde{q},A^{\top}p^{\star}\rangle-c\leq 0

and

\langle\widetilde{p},Aq^{\star}\rangle-c\geq 0
.
Then it follows by definition of

\textnormal{DG}(w)
, that we can write and further simplify

\displaystyle\textnormal{DG}(w)

\displaystyle=\max\nolimits_{q^{\prime}\in\Delta_{m}}\langle q^{\prime},A^{\top}p\rangle-\min\nolimits_{p^{\prime}\in\Delta_{n}}\langle p^{\prime},Aq\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\widetilde{q},A^{\top}p\rangle-\langle\widetilde{p},Aq\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\langle\widetilde{q},A^{\top}p\rangle-(\langle\widetilde{q},A^{\top}p^{\star}\rangle-c)-\langle\widetilde{p},Aq\rangle+(\langle\widetilde{p},Aq^{\star}\rangle-c)

\displaystyle=\langle\widetilde{q},A^{\top}(p-p^{\star})\rangle+\langle\widetilde{p},A(q^{\star}-q)\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\|A\widetilde{q}\|_{\infty}\cdot\|p-p^{\star}\|_{1}+\|A^{\top}\widetilde{p}\|_{\infty}\cdot\|q-q^{\star}\|_{1}\;,

(13)

where the final inequality comes from
Hölder’s inequality.
As
(\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q})\in\mathcal{W}
, it follows that both

\|A\widetilde{q}\|_{\infty},\|A^{\top}\widetilde{p}\|_{\infty}\leq a_{\max}
.
Moreover, by definition of
\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w)
,
we conclude from (
13
) that

\textnormal{DG}(w)\leq a_{\max}(\|p^{\star}-p\|_{1}+\|q^{\star}-q\|_{1})=2a_{\max}\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w)\;,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Relating TV Distance and KL Divergence.

Proposition B.4

(Pinsker-like Inequality for TV and KL)
.

Fix any
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})\in\mathcal{W}

and any
w=(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}
. Then it holds that

\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w)^{2}\leq\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)
.

Proof.

Recall by our definition of
TV
and
KL
that

\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w)=\textnormal{TV}(p^{\star},p)+\textnormal{TV}(q^{\star},q)

and

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)=\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p)+\textnormal{KL}(q^{\star},q)
.
By Pinsker’s inequality, we have that for each component that

2\cdot\textnormal{TV}(p^{\star},p)^{2}\leq\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p)\;\text{and}\;2\cdot\textnormal{TV}(q^{\star},q)^{2}\leq\textnormal{KL}(q^{\star},q)\;.

Thus together this implies

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)\geq 2\cdot\big(\textnormal{TV}(p^{\star},p)^{2}+\textnormal{TV}(q^{\star},q)^{2}\big)\geq\big(\textnormal{TV}(p^{\star},p)+\textnormal{TV}(q^{\star},q))^{2}=\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w)^{2}\;.

Here, the second inequality comes from the fact
that
\sqrt{(a^{2}+b^{2})/2}\geq(a+b)/2

and thus also
2(a^{2}+b^{2})\geq a+b
for all
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
.
∎

Relating KL Divergence and Chi-Squared Divergence.

Proposition B.5

(KL Divergence vs.
\chi^{2}
Divergence)
.

Fix
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})\in\mathcal{W}

and
w=(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}
. Then it holds that

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)\leq\chi^{2}(w^{\star},w)
.

Proof.

We have by definition of
\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p)
that

\textstyle\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}p^{\star}(i)\cdot\log\big(\tfrac{p^{\star}(i)}{p(i)}\big)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}p^{\star}(i)\cdot\big(\tfrac{p^{\star}(i)}{p(i)}-1\big)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\tfrac{p^{\star}(i)^{2}}{p(i)}-1\;,

where the inequality comes from the
fact that
\log(u)\leq u-1
for all
u>0
.
On the other hand, we have by definition of
\chi^{2}(w^{\star},w)

that

\textstyle\chi^{2}(p^{\star},p)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{(p^{\star}(i)-p(i))^{2}}{p(i)}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{p^{\star}(i)^{2}}{p(i)}+\frac{p(i)^{2}}{p(i)}-\frac{2p(i)p^{\star}(i)}{p(i)}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{p^{\star}(i)^{2}}{p(i)}-1\;.

Combining the two expressions then yields

\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p)\leq\chi^{2}(p^{\star},p)
.
An identical calculation also gives

\textnormal{KL}(q^{\star},q)\leq\chi^{2}(q^{\star},q)
,
which then implies by definition that

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)\leq\chi^{2}(w^{\star},w)
.
∎

Full Sequence of Relationships.

The following corollary summarizes the relationships
between the measures of distance to Nash
from Propositions
B.3
,

B.4
, and
B.5
:

Corollary B.6

(Relationships of Distances to Nash)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be a zero-sum game
with a Nash equilibrium
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})\in\mathcal{W}
.
Let
a_{\max}=\max_{(i,j)\in[m]\times[n]}|A(i,j)|
denote
the maximum absolute entry of
A
. Then for any

w=(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}
, it holds that:

\tfrac{1}{(2a_{\max})^{2}}\cdot\textnormal{DG}(w)^{2}\leq\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w)^{2}\leq\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)\leq\chi^{2}(w^{\star},w)\;.

Note that, due to Proposition
B.2
,
Corollary
B.6
implies
that upper bounds on
TV
,
KL
, and
\chi^{2}

each also imply an approximate Nash
in the sense of (
11
).

B.3  Properties of Spectrum of Payoff Matrix

Here, we state and prove two useful properties
related to the spectrum of the payoff matrix
A
.

Proposition B.7

(Relationship
between max entry and
max singular value)
.

Fix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
.
Let

a_{\max}=\max_{(i,j)\in[m]\times[n]}|A(i,j)|

and let
\sigma_{\max}=\|A\|_{2}
.
Then:

a_{\max}\leq\sigma_{\max}\leq\sqrt{mn}\cdot a_{\max}\;.

Proof.

For the first inequality, let
e_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,

e_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
i\in[m]
,
j\in[n]

denote the standard
m
and
n
-dimensional
basis vectors.
Then by Cauchy-Schwarz and the definition
of
\sigma_{\max}
, we have

\textstyle\max_{i,j}\,|A(i,j)|=\max_{i,j}\,|\langle e_{i},Ae_{j}\rangle|\leq\|e_{i}\|_{2}\cdot\|Ae_{j}\|_{2}\leq\sigma_{\max}\;.

For the second inequality,
recall that the Frobenius norm of
A
is given by

\|A\|_{F}=(\sum_{(i,j)\in[m]\times[n]}A(i,j))^{1/2}
,
and that
\|A\|_{2}\leq\|A\|_{F}
.
Then we can further bound

\textstyle\|A\|^{2}_{2}\leq\|A\|^{2}_{F}=\sum_{(i,j)\in[m]\times[n]}A(i,j)^{2}\leq mn\cdot a_{\max}\;.

Taking square roots yields the desired inequality.
∎

In the next proposition, we show that

\|J\|_{2}=\|A\|_{2}
, where
J
is the
skew-symmetric matrix from (
3
).

Proposition B.8

(Spectral norms of
J
and
A
)
.

Fix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
,
and let
J=((0,A),(-A^{\top},0))\in\mathbb{R}^{(m+n)\times(m+n)}

be the block skew-symmetric matrix from (
3
).
Then
\|J\|_{2}=\|A\|_{2}
.

Proof.

For a symmetric matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
,
let
\lambda_{\max}(M)
denote the largest eigenvalue of
M
.
Then recall by definition of the matrix spectral norm that,
for a matrix
P
, we have

\sigma_{\max}(P)=\|P\|_{2}=\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(P^{\top}P)}
.
For the skew-symmetric
J
, we can compute

J^{\top}J=\begin{pmatrix}0&A\\
-A^{\top}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&-A\\
A^{\top}&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}AA^{\top}&0\\
0&A^{\top}A\end{pmatrix}\;.

Given the block structure of
J^{\top}J
,
it follows that

\lambda_{\max}(J^{\top}J)=\max(\lambda_{\max}(AA^{\top}),\lambda_{\max}(A^{\top}A))
.
However,
\lambda_{\max}(AA^{\top})=\lambda_{\max}(A^{\top}A)
,
and thus

\|J\|_{2}=\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(J^{\top}J)}=\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(A^{\top}A)}=\|A\|_{2}\;,

which yields the desired claim.
∎

Appendix C
Details on Optimistic MWU Algorithm

In this section, we review preliminaries on the OMWU algorithm
that were introduced in Section
3
.

Organization of Section.

This section is organized as follows:

•

Section
C.1

reviews the derivation of the primal and dual
OMWU iterates (and also reviews of the standard MWU algorithm).
In particular, Section
C.1.2

gives details on OMWU as the instantiation of
Optimistic FTRL using the negative entropy regularizer.

•

Section
C.2

gives the proof of Proposition
3.1
,
which establishes the perspective
of the dual OMWU iterates as an optimistic skew-gradient
descent on the log-sum-exp energy function.

C.1  Primal and Dual OMWU Iterates

C.1.1  Primal Update Rule

Standard MWU.

We first recall the primal update rule of the standard
Multiplicative Weights Update algorithm (MWU)
(see, e.g.,
Freund and Schapire, 1999
,

Arora et al., 2012
),
which is the basis of the optimistic variant.
The MWU iterates are initialized
from
w_{0}=(p_{0},q_{0})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
Then using a fixed stepsize
\eta>0
,
for
t\geq 1
, the MWU iterates
w_{t}=(p_{t},q_{t})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})

are given by

\displaystyle p_{t}(i)

\displaystyle\propto p_{t-1}(i)\cdot\exp\big(-\eta(Aq_{t-1})(i)\big)\;\;\text{for all $i\in[m]$}

(MWU)

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad q_{t}(j)

\displaystyle\propto q_{t-1}(j)\cdot\exp\big(\eta(A^{\top}p_{t-1})(j)\big)\;\;\text{for all $j\in[n]$}\;.

Optimistic MWU.

The Optimistic MWU update rule is similar
to the standard (
MWU
) update,
but with an additional recency bias.
This leads to increases
in the weights of coordinates corresponding
to smaller magnitude losses in the most recent gradient vector.
Specifically, recall from (
OMWU
) that
the primal iterates
\{w_{t}\}
are initialized
from
w_{0}=(p_{0},q_{0})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
Then using a fixed stepsize
\eta>0
,
for
t+1\geq 1
, the iterates
w_{t}=(p_{t},q_{t})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})

are given by

\displaystyle p_{t+1}(i)

\displaystyle\propto p_{t}(i)\cdot\exp\big(-\eta(2Aq_{t}-Aq_{t-1})(i)\big)

\displaystyle\text{for all $i\in[m]$}

(14)

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad q_{t+1}(j)

\displaystyle\propto q_{t}(j)\cdot\exp\big(\eta(2A^{\top}p_{t}-A^{\top}p_{t-1})(j)\big)

\displaystyle\text{for all $j\in[n]$}\;.

We assume for notational convenience that

w_{-1}=(p_{-1},q_{-1})=w_{0}\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
This means at time
t=1
that

p_{1}(i)\propto p_{0}(i)\cdot\exp(-\eta(Aq_{0})(i))

for
i\in[m]

and
q_{1}(j)\propto q_{0}(j)\cdot\exp(\eta(A^{\top}p_{0})(j))

for
j\in[n]
.

C.1.2  Dual Iterates and Optimistic FTRL Instantiation

Component-Wise Dual Iterates.

We start by introducing the player-wise components
of the dual iterates
\{z_{t}\}
from (
4
).
For this, let
x_{0}=x_{-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and

y_{0}=y_{-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be
initial dual vectors.
Let
\{w_{t}\}
denote the sequence of
primal (
OMWU
) iterates,
where each
w_{t}=(p_{t},q_{t})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
Then for stepsize
\eta>0
,
the dual iterates
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,

y_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
are defined for all
t\geq 1
as follows:
first, at time
t=1
, let

x_{1}=x_{0}-\eta Aq_{0}

and
y_{1}=y_{0}+\eta A^{\top}p_{0}
.
Then for all
t\geq 2
, we define

\displaystyle\begin{cases}x_{t}=x_{0}-\eta\cdot\big(\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}Aq_{k}+Aq_{t-1}\big)\\
y_{t}=y_{0}+\eta\cdot\big(\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}A^{\top}p_{k}+A^{\top}p_{t-1}\big)\;.\end{cases}

(15)

Optimistic FTRL Using Negative-Entropy Regularizer.

As introduced in Section
3
,
the primal OMWU iterates can also be derived as
an instantiation of Optimstic FTRL (OFTRL) using
negative entropy regularization

(
Rakhlin and Sridharan, 2013
;
Syrgkanis et al., 2015
)
.
For this, recall that we define
the strictly convex regularizers
R_{m}:\Delta_{m}\to\mathbb{R}

and
R_{n}:\Delta_{n}\to\mathbb{R}
as
the discrete negative entropy functions:

\displaystyle R_{n}(p)

\displaystyle:=-\textnormal{Ent}_{m}(p)=\mathop{\textstyle{\sum}}\nolimits_{i=1}^{m}p(i)\log(p(i))

(16)

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad R_{m}(q)

\displaystyle:=-\textnormal{Ent}_{m}(q)=\mathop{\textstyle{\sum}}\nolimits_{j=1}^{n}q(i)\log(q(i))\;.

Here and throughout, we use the standard convention
that
0\log 0=0
.

Under Optimistic FTRL, the primal iterates

p_{t}\in\Delta_{m}
,
q_{t}\in\Delta_{n}
at time
t\geq 1

are chosen via a regularized best-response map
applied to the dual iterates
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and

y_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, respectively.
Specifically,
let the OFTRL primal iterates be initialized
at
p_{0}\in\textnormal{ri}(\Delta_{m})
and
q_{0}\in\textnormal{ri}(\Delta_{n})
.
At time
t+1\geq 1
, suppose

x_{t+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
y_{t+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

are the dual iterates from (
15
)
defined in terms of
\{q_{0},\dots,q_{t}\}

and
\{p_{0},\dots,p_{t}\}
, respectively.
Then at time
t+1
, the primal iterates of
OFTRL instantiated with the regularizers
R_{m}
and
R_{n}

are given by the objectives

\displaystyle p_{t+1}

\displaystyle:=\mathop{\textnormal{argmin}}\nolimits_{p\in\Delta_{m}}\big\{\langle p,x_{t+1}\rangle+R_{m}(p)\big\}

(17)

\displaystyle q_{t+1}

\displaystyle:=\mathop{\textnormal{argmin}}\nolimits_{q\in\Delta_{n}}\,\big\{\langle q,y_{t+1}\rangle+R_{n}(q)\big\}\;.

Here, observe that the dual variables
(x_{t+1},y_{t+1})

from (
15
) are
already scaled by the stepsize
\eta>0
.
Thus, smaller values of
\eta
correspond
to more weight on the regularization terms
in (
17
), and vice-versa.
Moreover, under the negative entropy regularizers,
the variables
(p_{t+1},q_{t+1})
have the following
closed-form solution:

Proposition C.1

(Softmax update rule)
.

For
t\geq 1
, let
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
y_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
and let
p_{t}\in\Delta_{m}
and
q_{t}\in\Delta_{n}

be defined as in (
17
).
Then
p_{t}=\textnormal{softmax}_{m}(x_{t})
and
q_{t}=\textnormal{softmax}_{n}(y_{t})
.

This result is standard and is based on the
first-order optimality conditions of the objective
in (
17
),
and thus we omit the proof;
See, e.g.,
Shalev-Shwartz et al., 2012
, Section 2.6.

Now, as previously mentioned,
the update rules of (
17
)
correspond exactly to the (
OMWU
) primal iterates.
Due to Proposition
C.1
, this will
follow as an immediate corollary of
Proposition
3.1
from Section
3
.
We restate and give the proof of this latter result in the next subsection,
which requires using the
concatenated

primal and dual variables
w_{t}=(p_{t},q_{t})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})

and
z_{t}=(x_{t},y_{t})\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
.
This notation was introduced in
expressions (
3
), (
4
),
and (
5
) from Section
3
,
and we briefly review these preliminaries again here:

Concatenated Primal and Dual Iterates.

Given
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, recall from expression (
3
)
that
J=-J^{\top}

is the block skew-symmetric matrix

J=((0,A),(-A^{\top},0))\in\mathbb{R}^{(m+n)\times(m+n)}
.
Then observe for any
w=(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}
that

\textstyle Jw=\begin{pmatrix}0&A\\
-A^{\top}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}p\\
q\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}Aq\\
-A^{\top}p\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}\;.

Thus, given the sequence of primal iterates

\{w_{k}\}_{k=1}^{t}
, where each
w_{k}=(p_{k},q_{k})\in\mathcal{W}
,
it follows for all
t+1\geq 2
that
the dual iterates
x_{t+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
y_{t+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

from (
15
) can
be written jointly as

z_{t+1}=(x_{t+1},y_{t+1})=z_{0}-\eta\cdot\big(\mathop{\textstyle{\sum}}\nolimits_{k=1}^{t}Jw_{k}+Jw_{t}\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}\;,

where
z_{0}=z_{-1}=(x_{0},y_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
is the dual initialization.
It follows recursively that we can write:

\begin{cases}z_{1}=z_{0}-\eta Jw_{0}&\text{at $t=1$}\\
z_{t+1}=z_{t}-2\eta Jw_{t}+\eta Jw_{t-1}&\text{at $t+1\geq 2$}\;.\end{cases}

(18)

Further recall from Section
3
that
we define the joint negative entropy
regularizer
R:\mathcal{W}\to\mathbb{R}
to be

R=R_{m}+R_{n}=-(\textnormal{Ent}_{m}+\textnormal{Ent}_{n})
,
where for
w=(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}
, we have

R(w)=R_{m}(p)+R_{n}(q)=-(\textnormal{Ent}_{m}(p)+\textnormal{Ent}_{n}(q))\;.

(19)

The separability of the regularizer
R

allows us to write the OFTRL objective
functions from (
17
)
using the concatenated dual variable

z_{t+1}=(x_{t+1},y_{t+1})\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
.
Specifically, it holds for
t+1\geq 1
that

w_{t+1}=(p_{t+1},q_{t+1})\in\mathcal{W}
is given by

w_{t+1}=\mathop{\textnormal{argmin}}_{w=(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}}\,\big\{\langle w,z_{t+1}\rangle+R(w)\big\}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathop{\textnormal{argmin}}\nolimits_{p\in\Delta_{m}}\,\big\{\langle p,x_{t+1}\rangle+R_{m}(p)\big\}\\
\mathop{\textnormal{argmin}}\nolimits_{q\in\Delta_{n}}\,\big\{\langle q,y_{t+1}\rangle+R_{n}(q)\big\}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{W}\;.

(20)

C.1.3  Energy Function and Gradient Map

Log-Sum-Exp Energy Function.

We recall the definition of the energy function

F:\mathbb{R}^{m+n}\to\mathbb{R}
from (
6
) in Section
3
.
First, we have that
\textnormal{LSE}_{m}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
and

\textnormal{LSE}_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
are the
m
and
n
-dimensional
log-sum-exp functions, where:

\textstyle\textnormal{LSE}_{m}(x)=\log\big(\sum_{i=1}^{m}x(i))\;\;\text{and}\;\;\textnormal{LSE}_{n}(y)=\log\big(\sum_{j=1}^{n}y(j))\;.

Both
\textnormal{LSE}_{m}
and
\textnormal{LSE}_{n}
are convex and
continuously differentiable.
Together, they define the convex and separable energy function

F=\textnormal{LSE}_{m}+\textnormal{LSE}_{n}
,
where for
z=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
we have

F(z)=\textnormal{LSE}_{m}(x)+\textnormal{LSE}_{n}(y)\;.

Energy Gradients.

By a straightforward differentiation,
note that
\nabla\textnormal{LSE}_{k}(v)=\textnormal{softmax}_{k}(v)
for any
k\geq 2

and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.
Thus, the gradient map
\nabla F:\mathbb{R}^{m+n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m+n}

is given by

\nabla F(z)=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{x}F(z)\\
\nabla_{y}F(z)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\textnormal{softmax}_{m}(x)\\
\textnormal{softmax}_{n}(y)\end{pmatrix}\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})\;\;\text{for all $z=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}$.}

(21)

Note also that the log-sum-exp energy function
F

and the negative entropy regularizer
R

are dual functions (convex conjugates).
We give more details and implications
of this duality in Section
D.1
.

C.2  OMWU as Optimistic Skew-Gradient Descent

The joint OMWU dual iterates
\{z_{t}\}
can
be written as an optimistic skew-gradient
descent on the energy function
F
.
This key property was stated in Proposition
3.1

in Section
3
.
Here, we restate the proposition and give the proof:

See
3.1

C.2.1  Proof of Proposition
3.1

For
t\geq 0
, let
w_{t}=(p_{t},q_{t})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})

be the primal iterates from (
OMWU
)
(equivalently, from (
14
)),
and let
z_{t}=(x_{t},y_{t})\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
be
the dual iterates from (
4
)
(equivalently, from (
18
)).
Moreover, recall from (
21
)
that
\nabla_{x}F(z)=\textnormal{softmax}_{m}(x)

and
\nabla_{x}F(z)=\textnormal{softmax}_{n}(y)

for any
z=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
.
Thus, we first prove inductively that

w_{t}=(p_{t},q_{t})=(\textnormal{softmax}_{m}(x_{t}),\textnormal{softmax}_{n}(y_{t}))

for all
t\geq 0
.

For the base case, we have by assumption of the
proposition that
z_{0}=(x_{0},y_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}

is such that
w_{0}=(p_{0},q_{0})=\nabla F(z_{0})=(\textnormal{softmax}_{m}(x_{0}),\textnormal{softmax}_{n}(y_{0}))
.
Now suppose through time
t

that
w_{t}=(\textnormal{softmax}_{m}(x_{t}),\textnormal{softmax}_{n}(y_{t}))
.
Then at time
t+1
,
we have by definition
of (
OMWU
) that
for all coordinates
i\in[n]
:

\displaystyle p_{t+1}(i)

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{t}(i)\cdot\exp(-\eta(2Aq_{t}-Aq_{t-1})(i))}{\sum_{k=1}^{m}p_{t}(k)\cdot\exp(-\eta(2Aq_{t}-Aq_{t-1})(k))}

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{\exp(x_{t}(i))}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\exp(x_{t}(\ell))}\cdot\exp(-\eta(2Aq_{t}-Aq_{t-1})(i))}{\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{\exp(x_{t}(k))}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\exp(x_{t}(\ell))}\cdot\exp(-\eta(2Aq_{t}-Aq_{t-1})(k))}

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp(x_{t}(i)-\eta(2Aq_{t}-Aq_{t-1})(i))}{\sum_{k=1}^{m}\exp(x_{t}(k)-\eta(2Aq_{t}-Aq_{t-1})(k))}

\displaystyle=\textnormal{softmax}_{m}\big(x_{t}-\eta(2Aq_{t}-Aq_{t-1})\big)(i)\;.

(22)

Here, the second equality follows by applying
the inductive hypothesis at time
t
.
By definition of
x_{t+1}
from (
15
),
we have
x_{t+1}=x_{t}-\eta(2Aq_{t}-Aq_{t-1})

for
t+1\geq 2
(and note that a
similar derivation also holds if
t=1
).
As (
22
) holds for all
coordinates
i\in[m]
, we
conclude that
p_{t+1}=\textnormal{softmax}_{m}(x_{t+1})
.
By an identical calculation,
we similarly find
q_{t+1}=\textnormal{softmax}_{n}(y_{t+1})
.
Thus it holds inductively that

w_{t}=(\textnormal{softmax}_{m}(x_{t}),\textnormal{softmax}_{n}(y_{t}))=\nabla F(z_{t})
for all
t\geq 0
.

For the second claim,
we have by definition of
\{z_{t}\}

from (
18
) that

z_{1}=z_{0}-\eta J\nabla F(z_{1})

at time
t=1
,
and at times
t+1\geq 2
:

z_{t+1}=z_{t}-2\eta J\nabla F(z_{t})+\eta J\nabla F(z_{t-1})=z_{t}-\eta J\nabla F(z_{t})-\eta J(\nabla F(z_{t})-F(z_{t-1}))\;.

This concludes the proof.

\blacksquare

Equivalence of (
OMWU
)
and the OFTRL Instantiation.

As a corollary of Proposition
3.1
,
we also have that the OFTRL iterates from (
5
)
(equivalently (
20
))
are also identical to those of (
OMWU
).
This follows from the fact that

\nabla F(z_{t})=(\textnormal{softmax}_{m}(x_{t}),\textnormal{softmax}_{n}(y_{t}))

for all
t\geq 1

(from the first part of Proposition
3.1
),
and from the characterization
of the iterates of (
5
) from
Proposition
C.1
.

C.2.2  Skew-Gradient Flow and Standard
Skew-Gradient Descent

Here, we briefly review the continuous-time

skew-gradient flow

and the standard forward discretization

skew-gradient descent
.

Skew-gradient flow.

The skew-gradient flow dynamics is the continuous-time
ODE:

\dot{z}(t)=-J\nabla F(z(t))\;.
