(Skew-gradient flow)

By the chain rule, observe that energy is

conserved
under (
Skew-gradient flow
),
as we can compute

\frac{d}{dt}F(z(t))=\langle\nabla F(z(t)),\dot{z}(t)\rangle=-\langle\nabla F(z(t)),J\nabla F(z(t))\rangle=0\;,

where the final equality is due to the skew-symmetry of

J=-J^{\top}
.
Geometrically, this means that the skew-gradient
vector
J\nabla F(z)
is
orthogonal

to the gradient
\nabla F(z)
.
Hence, the skew-gradient flow follows directions
that are tangent to the energy levelset at
the current point, thus conserving energy.

Skew-gradient descent.

The first-order forward (Euler) discretization
of (
Skew-gradient flow
) is
the forward skew-gradient descent.
This yields the
dual iterates corresponding to the standard MWU algorithm
from (
MWU
)
with stepsize
\eta>0
:

z_{t+1}=z_{t}-\eta J\nabla F(z_{t})\;.

(MWU Dual)

By convexity of
F
,
the one-step change in energy under (
MWU Dual
) is
always non-decreasing, since

F(z_{t+1})-F(z_{t})\geq\langle\nabla F(z_{t}),z_{t+1}-z_{t}\rangle=-\eta\langle\nabla F(z_{t}),J\nabla F(z_{t})\rangle=0\;,

where again the final equality is due
to
J=-J^{\top}
.

In contrast to the forward discretization,
we show for (
OMWU Dual
) that
the additional optimistic correction
term (which can be viewed as an approximation
of a backward discretization of skew-gradient flow),
leads to strictly dissipating energy
(see Lemma
4.1
).

See also
Wibisono et al., 2022

and
Katona et al., 2026

for additional background on skew-gradient flows
and their discretizations.

Appendix D
Details on Equivalence of KL Minimization and
Energy Dissipation

The energy function
F
and the regularizer
R

are closely related via duality.
Here, we specify several key aspects of this relationship in
more detail. For the OMWU iterates, this ultimately allows
for establishing an equivalence between minimizing
KL divergence in the primal space, and energy dissipation
in the effective dual space.

Organization of Section.

This section is organized as follows:

•

Section
D.1

gives more details on the dual relationship
of the log-sum-exp energy function
F
and the
negative-entropy regularizer
R
,
and their interplay over the effective dual space
\mathcal{Z}
.
This includes establishing a certain
modified Fenchel-Young identity (Proposition
D.2
)
and proving that
\mathcal{Z}
is orthogonal
to the interior NE of
A
(Proposition
D.6
).

•

Section
D.2

gives the proof of Proposition
3.2
,
which establishes an equivalence between
differences in
KL
divergence
and differences in energy
F
over the effective dual space
\mathcal{Z}
.
The proof of the proposition relies on the properties
introduced in Section
D.1
.

•

Section
D.3

establishes additional properties of the energy gradient map

\nabla F
over the effective dual space
\mathcal{Z}
.
This culminates in establishing, under the assumption of a
unique and interior NE, that the KL divergence
from Nash is exactly the energy suboptimality
gap in the effective dual space (Proposition
D.11
).

D.1  Duality of Regularizer and Energy Functions

The negative entropy regularizer
R

is the
convex (Fenchel) conjugate

F^{\star}
of the log-sum-exp energy function
F
.
Here, recall by definition that
F^{*}:\mathbb{R}^{m+n}\to\mathbb{R}
is
the convex function given by

F^{*}(w)\;:=\;\sup\nolimits_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}}\,\big\{\langle w,z\rangle-F(z)\big\}\;.

(23)

for all
w\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
.
As
F
and
R
are both separable,
the fact that
F^{*}=R

follows from standard relationships between
the log-sum-exp and negative entropy functions.
Moreover, it is also well known that
the Bregman divergence
D_{R}(w^{\prime},w)

induced by the negative entropy regularizer
R

is exactly the
KL
divergence
\textnormal{KL}(w^{\prime},w)
.
These relationships are stated
in the following proposition:

Proposition D.1
.

Let
F\colon\mathbb{R}^{m+n}\to\mathbb{R}
and
R\colon\mathcal{W}\to\mathbb{R}

be the energy and regularizer functions defined in (
6
) and (
19
),
respectively. Let
F^{*}
be the convex conjugate
of
F
as in (
23
).
Then the following two properties hold:

(i)

F^{*}=R
,
meaning
F^{*}(w)=R(w)

for
w\in\mathcal{W}
, and
F^{*}(w)=\infty
otherwise.

(ii)

For
w,w^{\prime}\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
:

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\prime},w)=D_{R}(w^{\prime},w)=R(w^{\prime})-R(w)-\langle\nabla R(w),w^{\prime}-w\rangle
.

The proof is standard and thus we omit it here
(see, e.g.,
Boyd and Vandenberghe, 2004
, Examples 3.19 and 3.25).
Moreover, by standard properties of convex conjugates,
note also that the energy
F=R^{*}
is
the conjugate function of
R
.

D.1.1  Modified Fenchel-Young Identity

Due to the dual relationship of
F
and
R
,
we additionally establish the following useful
properties that relate
KL
divergence to energy.
Specifically, we prove the following proposition:

Proposition D.2

(Modified Fenchel-Young Identity)
.

Let
F\colon\mathbb{R}^{m+n}\to\mathbb{R}
and
R\colon\mathcal{W}\to\mathbb{R}

be the energy and regularizer functions
from (
6
) and (
19
),
respectively.
Fix
z\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
, and let
w=\nabla F(z)\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
Then the following properties hold:

(i)

R(w)+F(z)=\langle w,z\rangle
.

(ii)

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\prime},w)=D_{R}(w^{\prime},w)=R(w^{\prime})+F(z)-\langle z,w^{\prime}\rangle

for any
w^{\prime}\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.

The first statement
is due to the standard Fenchel-Young inequality
(see, e.g.,
Shalev-Shwartz et al., 2012
, Section 2.7).
The second statement requires first introducing
several additional and important properties
of the energy function, the negative entropy regularizer,
and their gradients.
We first introduce these components
and then proceed with the proof of
Proposition
D.2

further below.

Energy function lacks global strict convexity.

Recall from expression (
7
) in
Section
3
that the linear subspace
\mathcal{S}

of constant shift directions is defined as

\textstyle\mathcal{S}=\textnormal{Span}\big(\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\mathbf{1}_{m}\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\big),\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
\mathbf{1}_{n}\end{smallmatrix}\big)\big)\;.

While the regularizer
R
is strictly convex over
the primal space
\mathcal{W}=\Delta_{m}\times\Delta_{n}
,
the energy function
F
is
not
globally
strictly convex over
\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
.
In particular, the gradients of
F
are invariant
to constant shifts (i.e., in directions
s\in\mathcal{S}
),
meaning that
F
is affine in those directions.
Specifically, the following properties hold:

Proposition D.3

(Energy function is not strictly convex)
.

Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{m+n}\to\mathbb{R}
be the energy function
from (
6
).
Fix any
z=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
.
Then for any
s=(d\mathbf{1}_{m},d^{\prime}\mathbf{1}_{n})\in\mathcal{S}

for some
d,d^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
:

\nabla F(z+s)=\nabla F(z)\;\;\text{and}\;\;F(z+s)=F(z)+(d+d^{\prime})\;.

Proof.

Recall from (
21
) that

\nabla F(z)=(\textnormal{softmax}_{m}(x),\textnormal{softmax}_{n}(y))
. Then using the definition of
\textnormal{softmax}_{k}(\cdot)
, we have

\displaystyle\nabla F(z+s)

\displaystyle=(\textnormal{softmax}_{m}(x+d\mathbf{1}_{m}),\textnormal{softmax}_{n}(y+d^{\prime}\mathbf{1}_{n}))

\displaystyle=(\textnormal{softmax}_{m}(x),\textnormal{softmax}_{n}(y))=\nabla F(z)\;.

Similarly, using the definition of
F=\textnormal{LSE}_{m}+\textnormal{LSE}_{n}
,
we have

F(z+s)=\textnormal{LSE}_{m}(x+d\mathbf{1}_{m})+\textnormal{LSE}_{n}(y+d^{\prime}\mathbf{1}_{n})=F(z)+(d+d^{\prime})\;,

which follows from the definition of log-sum-exp.
∎

Gradient of negative entropy regularizer.

Let
\nabla R\colon\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}

denote the gradient map

\nabla R(w)=(\nabla_{p}R(w),\nabla_{q}R(w))

for
w=(p,q)\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
By definition of
R
and the negative entropy functions
from (
19
), it follows that

\nabla R(w)=\begin{pmatrix}\log(p)+\mathbf{1}_{m}\\
\log(q)+\mathbf{1}_{n}\end{pmatrix}\;,

(24)

where
\log(p)=(\log(p(1)),\dots,\log(p(m)))\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and

\log(q)=(\log(q(1)),\dots,\log(q(n)))\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Although
R=F^{*}
is the convex conjugate of
F
,
the energy function’s lack of global strict convexity
shown in Proposition
D.3

means that
F
is not a
Legendre function

(see, e.g.,
Cesa-Bianchi and Lugosi, 2006
, Section 11.2 for a definition).
The main consequence of this fact is that
the regularizer gradient
\nabla R=\nabla F^{*}

is not

the inverse map of
\nabla F

(since, by Proposition
D.3
,

\nabla F\colon\mathbb{R}^{m+n}\to\mathbb{R}
is not injective).
However, the map
\nabla R\colon\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})\to\mathbb{R}^{m+n}

does serve as an inverse to
\nabla F
over
the quotient space
\mathbb{R}^{m+n}/\mathcal{S}
,
as formalized in the following proposition:

Proposition D.4

(Gradient map of regularizer)
.

Let
R\colon\mathcal{W}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the joint regularizer
from (
19
).
Fix
z=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
, and let

w=(p,q)=\nabla F(z)
.
Then there exists
s\in\mathcal{S}
such that

\nabla R(w)=z+s\;.

Specifically,
s=(c_{x}\mathbf{1}_{m},c_{y}\mathbf{1}_{n})
,
where
c_{x}=1-\textnormal{LSE}_{m}(x)\in\mathbb{R}

and
c_{y}=1-\textnormal{LSE}_{n}(y)\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

By definition of
F
and the assumption that

w=(p,q)=\nabla F(z)
,
we have
p=\textnormal{softmax}_{m}(x)

and
q=\textnormal{softmax}_{n}(y)
.
Using the definition of
\textnormal{softmax}_{m}

and
\textnormal{softmax}_{n}
, it follows that

\log(p)+\mathbf{1}_{m}=\begin{pmatrix}\log\Big(\tfrac{\exp(x(1))}{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\exp(x(j))}\Big)+1\\
\vdots\\
\log\Big(\tfrac{\exp(x(m))}{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\exp(x(j))}\Big)+1\\
\end{pmatrix}=x+(1-\textnormal{LSE}_{m}(x))\cdot\mathbf{1}_{m}\;.

(25)

By a similar calculation, we also have

\log(q)+\mathbf{1}_{n}=y+(1-\textnormal{LSE}_{n}(y))\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}
.
Substituting these simplifications into the
definition of
\nabla R(w)
from (
24
)
yields the stated claim.
∎

In contrast to Proposition
D.4
,
due to the strict convexity of the joint negative
entropy regularizer
R
, the energy gradient
\nabla F

does
serve as an inverse to
\nabla R
.
We state and prove this in the following proposition,
which is used in later subsections.

Proposition D.5

(Energy gradient is inverse of entropy gradient)
.

Let
F
and
R
be the functions from (
6
)
and (
19
), respectively.
Fix any
w=(p,q)\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
Then
\nabla F(\nabla R(w))=w
.

Proof.

Let
v=(v_{x},v_{y})=\nabla R(w)\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
.
By definition of
\nabla R

from (
24
), this means that

v_{x}=(\log_{m}(p)+\mathbf{1}_{m})

and
v_{y}=(\log_{n}(q)+\mathbf{1}_{n})
.
By a straightforward calculation, observe that

\displaystyle\nabla\textnormal{LSE}_{m}(v_{x})

\displaystyle=\textnormal{softmax}_{m}(v_{x})=\textnormal{softmax}_{m}(\log_{m}(p)+\mathbf{1}_{m})=p

\displaystyle\text{and}\;\nabla\textnormal{LSE}_{n}(v_{y})

\displaystyle=\textnormal{softmax}_{n}(v_{y})=\textnormal{softmax}_{n}(\log_{n}(q)+\mathbf{1}_{n})=q\;.

As
\nabla F(v)=(\textnormal{softmax}_{m}(v_{x}),\textnormal{softmax}_{n}(v_{y}))
,
we conclude that
\nabla F(\nabla R(w))=\nabla F(v)=w
.
∎

We are now equipped to give the proof of the “modified”
Fenchel-Young identity of Proposition
D.2
:

Proof of Proposition
D.2
.

For part (i), note that as
F
is convex and differentiable,
the standard Fenchel-Young inequality
(see, e.g.,
Boyd and Vandenberghe, 2004
, Section 3.3.2) says that,
for any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
and
w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}
:

F(z)+F^{*}(w^{\prime})\geq\langle w^{\prime},z\rangle\;,

where equality holds if and only if
w^{\prime}=\nabla F(z)
.
Thus part (i) follows from the assumption that

w=\nabla F(z)
and that
R=F^{*}
(as established in
the first claim of Proposition
D.1
).

For part (ii), recall from the second claim of
Proposition
D.1
that

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\prime},w)=D_{R}(w^{\prime},w)
.
Using the definition of
D_{R}(w^{\prime},w)
, we can then write

\displaystyle\textnormal{KL}(w^{\prime},w)=D_{R}(w^{\prime},w)

\displaystyle=R(w^{\prime})-R(w)-\langle\nabla R(w),w^{\prime}-w\rangle

\displaystyle=R(w^{\prime})+F(z)-\langle w,z\rangle-\langle\nabla R(w),w^{\prime}-w\rangle\;,

(26)

where the second equality follows from rewriting
R(w)
using
part (i) of the proposition.
Moreover, by the assumption that
w=\nabla F(z)
,
it follows from Proposition
D.4

that
\nabla R(w)=z+s
for some
s\in\mathcal{S}
.
Thus we can further simplify
the final term of (
26
) and write

\langle\nabla R(w),w^{\prime}-w\rangle=\langle z+s,w^{\prime}-w\rangle=\langle z,w^{\prime}-w\rangle\;.

(27)

Here, observe that the term
\langle s,w^{\prime}-w\rangle=0

by definition of
s\in\mathcal{S}
and
w,w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}=\Delta_{m}\times\Delta_{n}
.
Then substituting (
27
) into (
26
), we find

\displaystyle\textnormal{KL}(w^{\prime},w)

\displaystyle=R(w^{\prime})+F(z)-\langle w,z\rangle-\langle z,w^{\prime}-w\rangle=R(w^{\prime})+F(z)-\langle z,w^{\prime}\rangle\;,

which concludes the proof.

\blacksquare

D.1.2  Effective Dual Space

Recall from Section
3.2

that we define the linear subspace
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
by

\textstyle\mathcal{Z}:=\textnormal{Span}(J\mathcal{W})=\big\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\tau_{i}\cdot Jw_{i}\,\,\text{for}\,\,k\in\mathbb{N}\,,\tau_{i}\in\mathbb{R}\,,w_{i}\in\mathcal{W}\big\}.

(28)

We refer to
\mathcal{Z}
as the
effective dual space
,
as by (
4
),
all dual OMWU iterates
\{z_{t}\}
lie in
\mathcal{Z}
.

Effective Dual Space is Orthogonal to Interior NE.

The defining structural property of
\mathcal{Z}
is that,
when
A
has an interior Nash equilibrium
w^{\star}
,
every vector
z\in\mathcal{Z}
is orthogonal to
w^{\star}
.
Formally:

Proposition D.6

(Effective dual space is orthogonal to interior Nash)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
have an interior Nash
equilibrium
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
Then
\langle z,w^{\star}\rangle=0

for every
z\in\mathcal{Z}
.

Proof.

First, let
c=\langle p^{\star},Aq^{\star}\rangle
.
By the definition of
J
from (
3
) and
using part (i) of Proposition
B.1
, observe
that

\textstyle Jw^{\star}=\begin{pmatrix}0&A\\
-A^{\top}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}p^{\star}\\
q^{\star}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}Aq^{\star}\\
-A^{\top}p^{\star}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}c\mathbf{1}_{m}\\
-c\mathbf{1}_{n}\end{pmatrix}\;.

(29)

Now fix
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, and observe
by definition that we can write

z=J\big(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\tau_{i}w_{i}\big)

for some
k\geq 1
, where all
\tau_{i}\in\mathbb{R}

and
w_{i}=(p_{i},q_{i})\in\mathcal{W}=\Delta_{m}\times\Delta_{n}
.
Together with the skew-symmetry of
J=-J^{\top}
,
it then follows that

\textstyle\langle z,w^{\star}\rangle=\Big\langle J\big(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\tau_{i}w_{i}\big),w^{\star}\Big\rangle=-\Big\langle\big(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\tau_{i}w_{i}\big),Jw^{\star}\Big\rangle=-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\tau_{i}\langle w_{i},Jw^{\star}\rangle\;.

(30)

Using (
29
), observe for each
i\in[k]
that

\langle w_{i},Jw^{\star}\rangle=\Big\langle\begin{pmatrix}p_{i}\\
q_{i}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}c\mathbf{1}_{m}\\
-c\mathbf{1}_{n}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle=c\cdot\big(\langle p_{i},\mathbf{1}_{m}\rangle-\langle q_{i},\mathbf{1}_{n}\rangle\big)=0\;,

where the final equality comes from the fact that

p_{i}\in\Delta_{m}
and
q_{i}\in\Delta_{n}

are probability distributions.
Thus each term of (
30
) vanishes,
and it follows that
\langle z,w^{\star}\rangle=0
.
∎

D.2  Proof of Proposition
3.2

– Change in KL is Change in Energy

Using the modified Fenchel-Young identity
of Proposition
D.2

and the orthogonality of the effective dual space

\mathcal{Z}
to an interior Nash equilibrium
from Proposition
3.2
,
we can now prove the the key structural
result of Proposition
3.2
.
Restated here:

See
3.2

Proof.

As
w=\nabla H(z)
and
w^{\prime}=\nabla H(z^{\prime})
by assumption, we have
by Proposition
D.2
that

\displaystyle\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w^{\prime})

\displaystyle=R(w^{\star})+F(z^{\prime})-\langle z^{\prime},w^{\star}\rangle\;\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\;\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)=R(w^{\star})+F(z)-\langle z,w^{\star}\rangle\;.

Taking their difference, we find

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w^{\prime})-\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)=F(z^{\prime})-F(z)-\langle z^{\prime}-z,w^{\star}\rangle\;.

(31)

As both
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}
and
w^{\star}\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
,
we have from
Proposition
D.6
that
both
\langle z^{\prime},w^{\star}\rangle=0

and
\langle z,w^{\star}\rangle=0
.
Thus the final term in (
31
) vanishes,
which yields the desired statement.
∎

Change in KL is Change in Energy for OMWU iterates.

By definition in (
4
), we
have for the OMWU dual iterates
\{z_{t}\}

that
z_{t}\in\mathcal{Z}
for all
t\geq 1
.
Thus as an immediate corollary of
Proposition
3.2
,
we have the following:

Corollary D.7

(Equivalence between
energy and KL differences for OMWU)
.

Let
w^{\star}
be the unique and interior NE of
A
,
and let
\{w_{t}\}
and
\{z_{t}\}
be the primal and dual iterates
of OMWU on
A
with stepsize
\eta>0
,
and initialized from
w_{0}\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})

and
z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}
such that
\nabla F(z_{0})=w_{0}
.
Then

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t+1})-\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})=F(z_{t+1})-F(z_{t})\;\;\text{for all $t\geq 0$.}

Note that Corollary
D.7

assumes that the dual initialization
z_{0}

satisfying
\nabla H(z_{0})=w_{0}

also lies in
z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}
.
In the following subsection, we establish
additional properties of the energy gradient map
\nabla F

and the effective dual space
\mathcal{Z}
that imply
such an initialization always exists when
A
has a
unique and interior NE.
Thus, this assumption on
z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}
holds
without loss of generality.

D.3  KL Divergence is Energy Suboptimality Gap
Over Effective Dual Space

In this section, we further establish the following properties
about the energy gradient map
when its domain is restricted to the effective dual space
\mathcal{Z}
.
Specifically, when
A
has a unique and interior NE
w^{\star}
,
we establish in this section the following properties:

•

In Section
D.3.1
,
we prove that
\nabla F:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
is
surjective

(Proposition
D.9
)
and also
injective

when the value of the game is centered at zero
(Proposition
D.10
).

•

In Section
D.3.2
,
we prove for
z\in\mathcal{Z}
and
w=\nabla F(z)\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})

that
\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)
is
exactly the energy suboptimality gap

F(z)-\min_{z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}}F(z^{\prime})
over
the effective dual space (Proposition
D.11
).

D.3.1  Surjectivity (and Injectivity)
of Energy Gradient Map Over Effective Dual Space

Helper lemma under unique and interior Nash.

We start with the following helper lemma,
which establishes that the range of
J

and the orthogonal complement
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}

have only a trivial intersection under the
assumption of a unique and interior NE:

Lemma D.8
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
have a unique and interior NE

w^{\star}\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
Fix any
v\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
.
Suppose that
Jv\in\mathcal{S}
and
v\in\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
.
Then
v=0
.

Proof.

Since
w^{\star}
is a unique and interior
Nash equilibrium, we have by part (i)
of Proposition
B.1

that
Jw^{\star}\in\mathcal{S}
.
Now for
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
,
define
w_{\tau}=w^{\star}+\tau v
.
In other words,
w_{\tau}

is on the line through
w^{\star}
in the direction
of
v
.
It follows by linearity that

Jw_{\tau}=J(w^{\star}+\tau v)=Jw^{\star}+\tau Jv\in\mathcal{S}\;.

Here, the inclusion in
\mathcal{S}

follows from the assumption that
Jv\in\mathcal{S}
.

Now, as also
v\in\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
, we have by definition
that
\langle(\mathbf{1}_{m},\mathbf{1}_{n}),v\rangle=0
.
Thus, since
w^{\star}
is interior, there exists
a sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
such that,
for all
\tau\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
,
the point
w_{\tau}\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
is also
an interior (component-wise) probability distribution.
Here, note that the fact that
\langle(\mathbf{1}_{m},\mathbf{1}_{n}),v\rangle=0

is what ensures the components of
w_{\tau}

still have coordinates that sum to 1.

However, since
w^{\star}
is unique, and
since
Jw_{\tau}\in\mathcal{S}
, we have
by part (ii) of Proposition
B.1
that

w_{\tau}=w^{\star}+\tau v=w^{\star}
for all

\tau\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
.
Thus by the uniqueness of
w^{\star}
, we must have
v=0
.
∎

Surjectivity of energy gradient over effective dual space.

We now show that, when
A
has a unique and interior NE,
then over the effective dual space
\mathcal{Z}
,
the gradient map
\nabla F:\mathcal{Z}\to\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
is surjective.
Formally, we prove the following:

Proposition D.9

(Surjectivity of
\nabla F
over
\mathcal{Z}
)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
have a unique and interior
NE
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
Then for all
w\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
,
there exists
z\in\mathcal{Z}
such that

\nabla F(z)=w
.

Proof.

We prove the claim via the following three steps:

1. Energy gradient is globally surjective
:

First, we show that
\nabla F:\mathbb{R}^{m+n}\to\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})

is surjective over the full domain
\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
.
For this, fix
w\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
. Recalling that
R
is
the joint negative entropy regularizer, observe that
Proposition
D.4
shows that

\nabla R(w)=v
for some
v\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
.
Using the definition of
\nabla R
, it follows by
Proposition
D.5

that
\nabla F(v)=\nabla F(\nabla R(w))=w
.
Thus
\nabla F
is globally surjective.

2. Energy gradient is invariant to constant shifts
:

Now, again fix
w\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
, and let

v=\nabla R(w)\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
.
Further recall from Proposition
D.3

that
\nabla F
is invariant to constant shifts,
meaning for any
s\in\mathcal{S}
that

\nabla F(v+s)=\nabla F(v)=w
.
Thus to prove
\nabla F
is surjective over
\mathcal{Z}
,
it is sufficient to establish
v+s\in\mathcal{Z}
for some

s\in\mathcal{S}
. As
v\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
can lie in the full
space, it suffices to equivalently show that

\mathcal{Z}+\mathcal{S}=\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
.

3. Sufficient property holds under a unique and
interior NE
:

We will prove that
\mathcal{Z}+\mathcal{S}=\mathbb{R}^{m+n}

holds under the assumption of a unique and interior NE

w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
For this, first observe that the property
\mathcal{Z}+\mathcal{S}=\mathbb{R}^{m+n}

is equivalent to establishing equality between the orthogonal
complements of the two sets.
Since
\mathcal{Z}
,
\mathcal{S}
are linear subspaces, we have

(\mathcal{Z}+\mathcal{S})^{\bot}=\mathcal{Z}^{\bot}\cap\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
.
Moreover,
(\mathbb{R}^{m+n})^{\bot}=\{0\}
.
Thus our goal is to show
\mathcal{Z}^{\bot}\cap\mathcal{S}^{\bot}=\{0\}
.

For this, pick any
v=(v_{x},v_{y})\in\mathcal{Z}^{\bot}
.
By definition, this means
\langle v,z\rangle=0
for every

z\in\mathcal{Z}
.
We will show this implies
Jv\in\mathcal{S}
.
In particular, for any
w=(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}
,
let
z=Jw
. Thus clearly
z\in\mathcal{Z}
,
and thus we must have
\langle v,z\rangle=0
.
As
J=-J^{\top}
, we can further
express this condition as

\displaystyle 0=\langle v,z\rangle=\langle v,Jw\rangle=-\langle w,Jv\rangle=\big(\langle q,A^{\top}v_{x}\rangle-\langle p,Av_{y}\rangle\big)

Since this constraint must be satisfied
for all
w=(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}
simultaneously,
it follows that we must have

A^{\top}v_{x}=c_{1}\mathbf{1}_{m}
and
Av_{y}=c_{2}\mathbf{1}_{n}

for constants
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.
Thus
Jv\in\mathcal{S}
by definition of
\mathcal{S}
.

We have established that
v\in\mathcal{Z}^{\bot}
implies

Jv\in\mathcal{S}
.
Now suppose further that
v\in\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
,
meaning
v\in\mathcal{Z}^{\bot}\cap\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
.
However, since
w^{\star}
is the unique and interior NE,
we have by Lemma
D.8

that
v=0
.
Thus
\mathcal{Z}^{\bot}\cap\mathcal{S}^{\bot}=\{0\}
,
which establishes
\mathcal{Z}+\mathcal{S}=\mathbb{R}^{m+n}

under the assumptions on
w^{\star}
.
∎

Conditions for injectivity of energy gradient
over effective dual space.

We further establish that
\nabla F
is
injective over
\mathcal{Z}
when the value
of the game
A
is zero. Formally, we prove the following:

Proposition D.10

(
\nabla F
injective when game value is zero)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
have a unique and interior
NE
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
If
d=\langle p^{\star},Aq^{\star}\rangle=0
,
then
\nabla F(z)\neq\nabla F(z^{\prime})

for all
z\neq z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}
.

Proof.

First, recall from Proposition
D.3
that
\nabla F

is invariant under constant shifts. Thus it holds for
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}
that

\nabla F(z)=\nabla F(z^{\prime})
if and only if

z-z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
.
This means that
\nabla F:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
is injective over
\mathcal{Z}

if and only if
\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{S}=\{0\}
.
We will show this latter condition holds when

d=0
.

For this, pick any
z\in\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{S}
.
As
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, there exists
some
v=(v_{x},v_{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}

with
z=Jv
,
where
v=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\tau_{i}w_{i}

for
k\geq 1
and
\tau_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
,

w_{i}=(p_{i},q_{i})\in\mathcal{W}
.
Since
z\in\mathcal{S}
, it then further holds
that
z=Jv\in\mathcal{S}
.
Using the fact that
w^{\star}
is unique and interior
and applying a similar argument as in the proof of
Lemma
D.8
,

Jv\in\mathcal{S}
then further implies that
v=\alpha w^{\star}

for some constant
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
.
Together, this means that if

z\in\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{S}
, then
z\in\textnormal{Span}(Jw^{\star})
.
Moreover, as clearly
Jw^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}
,
and also since
Jw^{\star}\in\mathcal{S}
by Proposition
B.1
,
we have that

\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{S}=\textnormal{Span}(Jw^{\star})\;.

(32)

Finally, note that part (i) of Proposition
B.1

more specifically gives

Jw^{\star}=(d\mathbf{1}_{m},-d\mathbf{1}_{n})\in\mathcal{S}
,
where
d=\langle p^{\star},Aq^{\star}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}

is the value of the game. Thus if
d=0
,
then
Jw^{\star}=0
, and
\textnormal{Span}(Jw^{\star})=\{0\}
.
Thus
d=0
means

\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{S}=\{0\}
,
which implies
\nabla F:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}^{m+n}

is injective.
∎

D.3.2  KL from Nash is Energy Suboptimality Gap

Finally, we show that when
A

has a unique and interior NE
w^{\star}
,
the
KL
divergence from Nash in the primal space
is equivalent to the energy subpoptimality gap
in the effective dual space. Formally:

Proposition D.11

(KL as Energy Suboptimality Gap)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
have a unique and interior
NE
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
Let
z\in\mathcal{Z}
such that

w=\nabla F(z)
.
Then there exists
z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}

such that
\nabla F(z^{\star})=w^{\star}
, and moreover

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)=F(z)-\min_{z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}}F(z^{\prime})=F(z)-F(z^{\star})
.

Proof.
We proceed via the following steps:

1. Existence of minimizer in effective dual space
:

Recall that
F
is convex and continuously
differentiable. Thus over the linear subspace
\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
,
the first-order optimality conditions for
F
give
(see, e.g.,
Boyd and Vandenberghe, 2004
, Sec. 4.2.3):

\textstyle z\in\mathop{\textnormal{argmin}}_{z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}}F(z^{\prime})\;\iff\;\langle\nabla F(z),z^{\prime\prime}\rangle=0\;\text{for all $z^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}$}\;.

Now due to Proposition
D.9
,

\nabla F:\mathcal{Z}\to\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
is surjective over

\mathcal{Z}
. Thus, as
w^{\star}\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
,
there exists
z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}
such that

\nabla F(z^{\star})=w^{\star}
.
Due to Proposition
D.6
,
this further implies that

\textstyle\langle\nabla F(z^{\star}),z\rangle=\langle w^{\star},z\rangle=0\;\text{for all $z\in\mathcal{Z}$}\quad\implies z^{\star}\in\mathop{\textnormal{argmin}}_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}F(z)\;.

2. Minimum energy function value attained
:

Recall from expression (
32
)
in the proof of Proposition
D.10

that
\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{S}=\textnormal{Span}(Jw^{\star})
.
Thus fixing
z^{\star}\in\mathop{\textnormal{argmin}}_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}F(z)

from Step (1), then also

z_{\tau}=z^{\star}+\tau Jw^{\star}\in\mathop{\textnormal{argmin}}_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}F(z)

for any
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
.
This is because
\nabla F(z^{\star}+s)=\nabla F(z^{\star})

for any
s\in\mathcal{S}
, and since
Jw^{\star}\in\mathcal{S}
.
Thus
z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}
is in the argmin set if and only if

z^{\prime}=z_{\tau}
for some
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
.
However, since
F(z_{\tau})=F(z^{\star})
for every
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
,

\min_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}F(z)
is well-defined, and thus

F(z^{\star})=\min_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}F(z)
.

3. Apply Fenchel-Young identity
:

Now by Part (ii) of the Fenchel-Young identity
from Proposition
D.2
, we have
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
and
w=\nabla F(z)
that

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)=R(w^{\star})+F(z)-\langle w^{\star},z\rangle=R(w^{\star})+F(z)\;,

(33)

where the second equality is due to
Proposition
D.6
.
Now let
z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}
such that

\nabla F(z^{\star})=w^{\star}
.
Then it follows from (
33
) that

0=\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w^{\star})=R(w^{\star})+F(z^{\star})\implies R(w^{\star})=-F(z^{\star})
.
By Step (1), we have
z^{\star}\in\mathop{\textnormal{argmin}}_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}F(z)
,
and by Step (2), we further have

F(z^{\star})=\min_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}F(z)
.
Then substituting
R(w^{\star})=-F(z^{\star})
into (
33
),
we find for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
and
w=\nabla F(z)
that

\textstyle\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)=F(z)-\min_{z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}}F(z^{\prime})\;,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix E
Details on Energy Function Hessian Matrix

This section gives additional preliminaries
on the Hessian of the energy function
that are used in the proof of energy dissipation
under OMWU.

Oragnization of Section.

The section is organized as follows:

•

Section
E.1

gives more details on energy function Hessian that are
used throughout the sequel.

•

Section
E.2

gives more details on the
local hessian stability
(LHS)
property introduced in Section
4

and provides the proof of Proposition
4.3
.

•

Section
E.3

proves several bounds using the local norms
\|\cdot\|_{z}

that hold under the LHS property.

E.1  Properties of Energy Hessian

Our analysis of OMWU relies on
several properties of the energy function’s Hessian
\nabla^{2}F
.
Formally, for any
z=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
, the Hessian

\nabla^{2}F(z)\in\mathbb{R}^{(m+n)\times(m+n)}

is the block diagonal symmetric matrix

\nabla^{2}F(z)=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla^{2}\textnormal{LSE}_{m}(x)&0\\
0&\nabla^{2}\textnormal{LSE}_{n}(y)\end{pmatrix}\;.

(34)

Note by convexity of
F
that
\nabla^{2}F(z)\succeq 0

for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
.

In the next proposition, we state several
basic properties about the energy Hessian.
In particular, we note that

\nabla^{2}F
has a natural probabilistic
chacterization:
for
z=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
and
w=(p,q)=\nabla F(z)\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
,
the Hessian blocks
\nabla^{2}\textnormal{LSE}_{m}(x)

and
\nabla^{2}\textnormal{LSE}_{n}(y)

are the
covariance matrices

of the probability distributions
p
and
q
, respectively.
Thus, quadratic forms of these Hessian blocks
can be interpreted as variances under the
distributions
p
and
q
.
Before stating and proving these properties formally,
we first introduce the folllowing notation for discrete variances:

Notation for variances.

Fix
(v_{x},v_{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}

and
(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}
. We write
\textnormal{Var}_{p}(v_{x})\in\mathbb{R}
to denote
the variance of
v_{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
under the distribution
p\in\Delta_{m}
,
and we similarly write
\textnormal{Var}_{q}(v_{y})\in\mathbb{R}
to denote
the variance of
v_{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
under the distribution
q\in\Delta_{n}
.
Formally, we define:

\textnormal{Var}_{p}(v_{x}):=\mathop{\textstyle{\sum}}\nolimits_{i=1}^{m}p(i)\big(v_{x}(i)-\langle p,v_{x}\rangle\big)^{2}\;\;\text{and}\;\;\textnormal{Var}_{q}(v_{y}):=\mathop{\textstyle{\sum}}\nolimits_{j=1}^{n}q(j)\big(v_{y}(j)-\langle q,v_{y}\rangle\big)^{2}\,.

(35)

Then for any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
,
the matrix
\nabla^{2}F(z)
has the following properties:

Proposition E.1

(Properies of Energy Hessian)
.

Let
F
be the energy function
from (
6
).
For any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
,
let
\nabla^{2}F(z)
be the matrix in (
34
).
Let
w=(p,q)=\nabla F(z)\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
Then:

(i)

The Hessian
\nabla^{2}F(z)
is given by

\nabla^{2}F(z)=\begin{pmatrix}\big(\textnormal{Diag}(p)-pp^{\top}\big)&0\\
0&\big(\textnormal{Diag}(q)-qq^{\top}\big)\end{pmatrix}\;.

(ii)

For any
v=(v_{x},v_{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
,
it holds that

\big\langle v,\nabla^{2}F(z)v\big\rangle=\textnormal{Var}_{p}(v_{x})+\textnormal{Var}_{q}(v_{y})
.

(iii)

\textnormal{Null}\big(\nabla^{2}F(z)\big)=\mathcal{S}=\textnormal{Span}\big(\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\mathbf{1}_{m}\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\big),\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
\mathbf{1}_{n}\end{smallmatrix}\big)\big)
.

(iv)

0\preceq\nabla^{2}F(z)\preceq I
.

Proof.

We prove each of the statements separately:

Proof of (i).

This result is standard, and a proof can be found in

Boyd and Vandenberghe, 2004
, Section 3.1.5,
or
Gao and Pavel, 2017
, Proposition 2.

Proof of (ii).

Using the structure of
\nabla^{2}F(z)
from claim (i),
for
v=(v_{x},v_{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
we have

\big\langle v,\nabla^{2}F(z)v\big\rangle=\big\langle v_{x},(\textnormal{Diag}(p)-pp^{\top})v_{x}\big\rangle+\big\langle v_{y},(\textnormal{Diag}(q)-qq^{\top})v_{y}\big\rangle\;.

Observe that the matrices
(\textnormal{Diag}(p)-pp^{\top})

and
(\textnormal{Diag}(q)-qq^{\top})
are exactly the
covariance matrices of the distributions
p\in\Delta_{m}

and
q\in\Delta_{n}
.
Indeed, recalling the definitions of
\textnormal{Var}_{p}(v_{x})

and
\textnormal{Var}_{q}(v_{y})
from (
35
),
it is straightforward to check that

\displaystyle\langle v_{x},(\textnormal{Diag}(p)-pp^{\top})v_{x}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle v_{x},\textnormal{Diag}(p)v_{x}\rangle-\langle v_{x},pp^{\top}v_{x}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle v_{x},\textnormal{Diag}(p)v_{x}\rangle-\big(\langle p,v_{x}\rangle\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\textstyle{\sum}}\nolimits_{i=1}^{m}p(i)\cdot(v_{x}(i))^{2}-\big(\mathop{\textstyle{\sum}}\nolimits_{i=1}^{m}p(i)\cdot v_{x}(i)\big)^{2}=\textnormal{Var}_{p}(v_{x})\;.

Using identical calculations, it follows that also

\langle v_{y},(\textnormal{Diag}(q)-qq^{\top})v_{y}\rangle=\textnormal{Var}_{q}(v_{y})
,
which completes the proof of claim (ii).

Proof of (iii).

This property is also proven in
Proposition of
Gao and Pavel, 2017
.

Proof of (iv).

Observe that

(\textnormal{Diag}(p)-pp^{\top})\preceq I
,
which follows from the fact that all
0<p(i)\leq 1

and
pp^{\top}\succeq 0
.
Similarly,
(\textnormal{Diag}(q)-qq^{\top})\preceq I
,
and thus due to the block structure of
\nabla^{2}F(z)

from part (i) of the proposition,
it follows that
\nabla^{2}F(z)\preceq I
.
By convexity of
F
, also
0\preceq\nabla^{2}F(z)
.
∎

E.1.1  Hessian of Regularizer and Inverse Energy Hessian

We also consider the Hessian matrix of
the joint negative entropy regularizer
R:\mathcal{W}\to R

from (
19
).

Negative Entropy Hessian.

Fix any
w=(p,q)\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
Then let
\nabla^{2}R(w)\in\mathbb{R}^{(m+n)\times(m+n)}

be the Hessian matrix of
R
given by

\nabla^{2}R(w)=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla^{2}R_{m}(p)&0\\
0&\nabla^{2}R_{n}(q)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\textnormal{Diag}(1/p)&0\\
0&\textnormal{Diag}(1/q)\end{pmatrix}\;.

(36)

Here, we write
1/p=(1/p(1),\dots,1/p(m))\in\mathbb{R}^{m}

and
1/q=(1/q(1),\dots,1/q(n))\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Note that the second equality in (
36
)
follows from the definitions
of
\nabla R_{m}(p)
and
\nabla R_{n}(q)

from (
24
).

Negative Entropy Hessian as
Restricted Inverse of Energy Hessian.

Due to the duality of the energy function
and the negative entropy regularizer,

\nabla^{2}R
is the inverse of
\nabla^{2}F

when restricted to the orthogonal complement
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
:

Proposition E.2

(Energy hessian restricted inverse)
.

Fix
z\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
, and let
w=(p,q)=\nabla F(z)
.
Then
(\nabla^{2}F(z))^{-1}=\nabla^{2}R

over
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
, meaning

\nabla^{2}F(z)(\nabla^{2}R(w))v=v

for all
v\in\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
.

Proof.

Let
z=(x,y)
, and let
v=(v_{x},v_{y})
.
We first prove the claim for the blocks

\nabla^{2}\textnormal{LSE}_{m}(x)
and
\nabla^{2}R_{m}(p)
.
For this, we have by definition of

\nabla^{2}R_{m}
and
\nabla^{2}\textnormal{LSE}_{m}
that

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\textnormal{LSE}_{m}(x)R_{m}(p)v_{x}

\displaystyle=(\textnormal{Diag}(p)-pp^{\top})(\textnormal{Diag}(1/p))v_{x}

\displaystyle=\textnormal{Diag}(p)\textnormal{Diag}(1/p)v_{x}-p(p^{\top}\textnormal{Diag}(1/p))v_{x}

\displaystyle=Iv_{x}-p\cdot\langle\mathbf{1}_{m},v_{x}\rangle=v_{x}\;.

Here, the penultimate equality is
due to
p^{\top}\textnormal{Diag}(1/p)=\mathbf{1}_{m}
,
and the final equality is due to the
assumption that
v\in\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
,
and thus
\langle v_{x},\mathbf{1}_{m}\rangle=0
.
By an identical calculation, we have

\nabla^{2}\textnormal{LSE}_{n}(y)R_{n}(q)v_{y}=v_{y}
,
which proves the claim
by definition of
\nabla^{2}F
and
\nabla^{2}R
.
∎

E.1.2  Gradient Differences and Bregman Divergence in Integral Form

Throughout, we will also use the energy Hessian
\nabla^{2}F
to
express certain remainder terms when taking Taylor expansions of
F
.
First, we can express the Bregman divergence

D_{F}(\cdot,\cdot)

as follows:

Proposition E.3

(Bregman divergence in integral form)
.

Let
F
be the function in (
6
),
and let
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
.
Let
z_{s}:=z-s(z-z^{\prime})
for
s\in[0,1]
.
Then the Bregman divergence
D_{F}(z^{\prime},z)
is

D_{F}(z^{\prime},z)=\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\big\langle z-z^{\prime},\nabla^{2}F(z_{s})(z-z^{\prime})\big\rangle\,ds\;.

For
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
, we can also express
the difference of energy gradients using the energy Hessian:

Proposition E.4

(Gradient difference and Hessian remainder)
.

Let
F
be the function in (
6
),
and let
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
.
Let
z_{s}:=z-s(z-z^{\prime})
for
s\in[0,1]
.
Let
G_{F}(z,z^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
be the remainder term

G_{F}(z,z^{\prime})=\int_{0}^{1}(\nabla^{2}F(z_{s})-\nabla^{2}F(z))(z-z^{\prime})\,ds\;.

(37)

Then

\nabla F(z)-\nabla F(z^{\prime})=\nabla^{2}F(z)(z-z^{\prime})+G_{F}(z,z^{\prime})
.

Propositions
E.3

and
E.4
are both standard
consequences of Taylor’s theorem (see,
e.g.,
Nocedal and Wright, 2006
, Theorem 2.1),
and thus we omit the proofs.

E.2  Local Hessian Stability Property

In the sequel, our analysis of the energy dissipation
under OMWU relies on the following
local Hessian stability

property of the energy function,
introduced originally in Section
4
:

See
4.3

Proof.

Recalling the block structure of the energy Hessian

\nabla^{2}F(z)
from Proposition
E.1
,
it suffices to show that the property holds
for the block components
\nabla^{2}\textnormal{LSE}_{m}(x)

and
\nabla^{2}\textnormal{LSE}_{n}(y)
.
We will prove the property holds for the former,
and for the latter it will follow by identical calculations.

For this, fix any vector
u\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and let
z=(x,y)
and
z^{\prime}=(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
.
Observe that the assumption
\|z-z^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\alpha

implies
\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\alpha
.
Now for every
s\in[0,1]
, define
x_{s}=x+s(x^{\prime}-x)
,
and let
h=x^{\prime}-x
.
Moreover, let
p_{s}=\nabla\textnormal{LSE}_{m}(x_{s})=\textnormal{softmax}_{m}(x_{s})
,
meaning

p_{s}=\textnormal{softmax}_{m}(x_{s})=\textnormal{softmax}_{m}(x+sh)\;.

Further let
f_{u}(s)
be the scalar function given by

\textstyle f_{u}(s)=\big\langle u,\nabla^{2}\textnormal{LSE}_{m}(x_{s})u\big\rangle=\big\langle u,(\textnormal{Diag}(p_{s})-p_{s}p_{s}^{\top})u\big\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{s}(i)\cdot\big(u(i)-\langle p_{s},u\rangle\big)^{2}\;,

where the equalities follow from the structure of

\nabla^{2}\textnormal{LSE}_{m}
from Proposition
E.1
.

Now, differentiating
f_{u}(s)
with respect to
s

and simplifying, we find

\textstyle f_{u}^{\prime}(s)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{s}(i)\cdot\big(h(i)-\langle p_{s},h\rangle\big)\big(u(i)-\langle p_{s},u\rangle\big)^{2}\;.

By the assumption
\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{\infty}=\|h\|_{\infty}\leq\alpha
,
it follows that
|h(i)-\langle p_{s},h\rangle|\leq 2\alpha
.
Thus we can further bound

|f^{\prime}_{u}(s)|\leq 2\alpha\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{s}(i)\cdot\big(u(i)-\langle p_{s},u\rangle\big)^{2}=2\alpha\cdot f_{u}(s)\;.

Applying Grönwall’s inequality then yields

\exp(-2\alpha)\cdot f_{u}(0)\leq f_{u}(1)\leq\exp(2\alpha)\cdot f_{u}(0)\;.

Since
x_{0}=x
and
x_{1}=x^{\prime}
and

u
was arbitrary, it follows by definition of
f_{u}
that

\exp(-2\alpha)\cdot\nabla^{2}\textnormal{LSE}_{m}(x)\preceq\nabla^{2}\textnormal{LSE}_{m}(x^{\prime})\preceq\exp(2\alpha)\cdot\nabla^{2}\textnormal{LSE}_{m}(x)\;,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Local Hessian Stability and Generalized Self-Concordance.

Similar versions of this LHS property have also been exploited
in the optimization literature as a consequence
of
(generalized) self-concordance
.
See, e.g.,
Bach, 2010
,

Tran-Dinh et al., 2015

(Theorem 4.b, eq. (20)),

Sun and Tran-Dinh, 2019
(Proposition 8, eq. (16) when
\nu=2
),
or more recently
Freund et al., 2026

(Proposition 16, and Lemma 15),
and the references therein.
For the purposes of the present paper,
our proof of energy dissipation in Lemma
4.1

uses the the semidefinite ordering of (
LHS
)
via the application of several general inequalities
involving local norms under the energy Hessian.
We present these tools in the next subsection.

E.3  Consequences of Local Hessian Stability Property

E.3.1  Review of Notation for Local Norms

We first recall and expand on
the notation of the local norms introduced
in Section
4
.
Fix
z\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
.
Then for all
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
, we define

\langle u,v\rangle_{z}:=\langle u,\nabla^{2}F(z)v\rangle\;\;\text{and}\;\;\|v\|_{z}:=\sqrt{\langle v,\nabla^{2}F(z)v\rangle}\;.

We further define a local
dual
norm
\|\cdot\|_{z,*}

over the linear subspace
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m+n}

(as defined in (
7
)).
For this, when restricted to acting on vectors
v\in\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
,
we write
(\nabla^{2}F(z))^{-1}

to denote the inverse energy Hessian, where

\nabla^{2}F(z)(\nabla^{2}F(z))^{-1}u=u

for all
u\in\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
and
z\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
.
From Proposition
E.2
,
note in particular that
(\nabla^{2}F(z))^{-1}=\nabla^{2}R(w)

as defined in (
36
),
where
w=\nabla F(z)
.
Then over
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
, for any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
,
the local dual norm
\|\cdot\|_{z,*}
is given by

\|u\|_{z,*}=\sqrt{\langle u,(\nabla^{2}F(z))^{-1}u\rangle}\quad\text{for $u\in\mathcal{S}^{\bot}$.}

For
z\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
, the primal-dual pair

(\|\cdot\|_{z},\|\cdot\|_{z,*})
satisfies the
generalized Cauchy-Schwarz inequality

\big|\langle v,u\rangle\big|\leq\|v\|_{z}\cdot\|u\|_{z,*}\quad\text{for all $v\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}$ and $u\in\mathcal{S}^{\bot}$}.

E.3.2  Properties of Local Norms under LHS

We state and prove several useful properties of
the local norms under the (
LHS
) property.

Relationships between global and local norms.

The following proposition relates primal
and local norms to the Euclidean norm:

Proposition E.5
.

For all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
,

v\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
, and

u\in\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
:

\|v\|_{z}\leq\|v\|_{2}

and
\|u\|_{2}\leq\|u\|_{z,*}
.

Proof.

For the first claim, recall by part (iv) of
Proposition
E.1
that

\nabla^{2}F(z)\preceq I
. Thus for any
v\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
,
it holds that

\|v\|^{2}_{z}=\langle v,\nabla^{2}F(z)v\rangle\leq\langle v,v\rangle=\|v\|^{2}_{2}\;,

which implies
\|v\|_{z}\leq\|v\|_{2}
.
For the second claim, note that when restricted to

\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
, the ordering
\nabla^{2}F(z)\preceq I

implies
I\preceq(\nabla^{2}F(z))^{-1}
.
Then for any
u\in\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
and
z\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
,
it holds that

\|u\|^{2}_{2}=\langle u,u\rangle\leq\langle u,(\nabla^{2}F(z))^{-1}u\rangle=\|u\|^{2}_{z,*}\;,

which implies
\|u\|_{2}\leq\|u\|_{z,*}
.
∎

Local norm transfer.

For vectors
z,z^{\prime},v\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
,
a key consequence of the (
LHS
) property
is the ability to relate the
local norm
\|v\|_{z}
to the local norm
\|v\|_{z^{\prime}}

up to multiplicative factors depending on
\|z-z^{\prime}\|_{\infty}
.
Formally, we have the following relationships:

Lemma E.6
.

For
\alpha>0
, let
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}

such that
\|z-z^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\alpha
. Then
for all
v\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
:

\exp(-\alpha)\cdot\|v\|_{z}\leq\|v\|_{z^{\prime}}\leq\cdot\exp(\alpha)\|v\|_{z}\,.

Proof.

Due to the (
LHS
) property, we have for any
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}
such that
\|z-z^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\alpha
,

\exp(-2\alpha)\nabla^{2}F(z)\preceq\nabla^{2}F(z^{\prime})\preceq\exp(2\alpha)\nabla^{2}F(z)\;.

Using the definition of local norms, this implies that for any
v\in\mathcal{Z}
,

\exp(-2\alpha)\cdot\|v\|^{2}_{z}\leq\|v\|^{2}_{z^{\prime}}=\langle v,\nabla^{2}F(z^{\prime})v\rangle\leq\exp(2\alpha)\cdot\|v\|^{2}_{z}\,.

(38)

Taking the square root in the above inequality concludes the proof.
∎

Non-expansiveness of local norm.

The next lemma establishes that the energy Hessian
satisfies a certain non-expansiveness property
under its own induced local norm:

Lemma E.7
.

Let
M\in R^{(m+n)\times(m+n)}
be a matrix,
and fix
z\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
.
Then for any
v\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
:

\|M\nabla^{2}F(z)v\|_{z}\leq\|M\|_{2}\cdot\|v\|_{z}\;.

Proof.

Applying Proposition
E.5
,
we can write

\|M\nabla^{2}F(z)v\|_{z}\leq\|M\nabla^{2}F(z)v\|_{2}\leq\|M\|_{2}\cdot\|\nabla^{2}F(z)v\|_{2}\;,

(39)

where the final inequality comes from the definition
of the spectral norm
\|M\|_{2}
.
To further bound the term
\|\nabla^{2}F(z)v\|_{2}
, observe
by definition that

\|\nabla^{2}F(z)v\|^{2}_{2}=\langle v,(\nabla^{2}F(z))^{2}v\rangle\leq\langle v,\nabla^{2}F(z)v\rangle=\|v\|^{2}_{z}\;.

(40)

Here, the inequality comes from the fact that

(\nabla^{2}F(z))^{2}\preceq\nabla^{2}F(z)

since, by Proposition
E.1
,

0\preceq\nabla^{2}F(z)\preceq I
.
Then taking square roots and substituting (
40
)
into (
39
) concludes the proof.
∎

E.3.3  Bounds on Bregman Divergence
and Hessian Remainder Terms under LHS

Additional notation.

To start, we define the following two scalar functions
which will be useful in our proofs for controlling certain constant
multiplicative factors.
Specifically, let
\mu:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\nu:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}

be the functions such that, for any
x\neq 0
:

\mu(x)=\frac{\exp(x)-x-1}{x^{2}}\,,\quad\nu(x)=\frac{\exp(x)-x-1}{x}\;.

(41)

In particular we will be using the following straightforward bounds:

Lemma E.8
.

For any
\delta>0
and
|x|\leq\delta
,
it holds that

\big|\mu(x)-\tfrac{1}{2}\big|\leq\frac{\delta\exp(\delta)}{6}\quad\text{and}\quad\big|\nu(x)-\tfrac{x}{2}\big|\leq\frac{\delta^{2}\exp(\delta)}{6}\;.

Proof.

Taking a second-order Taylor expansion with Lagrange remainder,
we have:

\exp(x)=1+x+\frac{x^{2}}{2}+\frac{\exp(\xi)x^{3}}{6}\,

(42)

for some
\xi\in(0,x)
.
Rearranging, we find

\mu(x)-\frac{1}{2}=\frac{\exp(\xi)x}{6}
.
Noting that
|\exp(\xi)x|\leq\delta\exp(\delta)
for
|x|\leq\delta

then gives
|\mu(x)-\frac{1}{2}|\leq\frac{\delta\exp(\delta)}{6}
,
which yields the first claim of the lemma.

For the second claim, we can again rearrange (
42
)
to find

\nu(x)-\frac{x}{2}=\frac{\exp(\xi)x^{2}}{6}
.
Taking absolute values and again using

|\exp(\xi)x^{2}|\leq\delta^{2}\exp(\delta)
for
|x|\leq\delta

then gives
|\nu(x)-\frac{x}{2}|\leq\frac{\delta^{2}\exp(\delta)}{6}
.
∎

Bounds on Bregman divergence.

Lemma E.9
.

Fix
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}

such that
\|z-z^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\alpha

for
\alpha>0
.
Then the Bregman divergence
D_{F}(z^{\prime},z)

is bounded above and below by

\mu(-2\alpha)\cdot\|z^{\prime}-z\|^{2}_{z}\;\leq\;D_{F}(z^{\prime},z)\;\leq\;\mu(2\alpha)\cdot\|z^{\prime}-z\|^{2}_{z}\;.

Proof.

Recall from Proposition
E.3

that the Bregman divergence
D_{F}(z^{\prime},z)

can be written as

D_{F}(z^{\prime},z)=\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\big\langle z-z^{\prime},\nabla^{2}F(z_{s})(z-z^{\prime})\big\rangle\,ds\;,

(43)

where
z_{s}:=z-s(z-z^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Z}
for
s\in[0,1]
.
Fixing
s
,
observe that
\|z-z_{s}\|_{\infty}=s\|z-z^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq s\alpha
,
which follows from the assumption that
\|z-z^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\alpha
.
Thus we have by (
LHS
) that

\exp(-2s\alpha)\nabla^{2}F(z)\preceq\nabla^{2}F(z_{s})\preceq\exp(2s\alpha)\nabla^{2}F(z)\;.

(44)

For the upper bound on
D_{F}(z^{\prime},z)
, note that
the relationship in (
44
) implies

\displaystyle\big\langle z-z^{\prime},\nabla^{2}F(z_{s})(z-z^{\prime})\big\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\exp(2s\alpha)\cdot\langle z-z^{\prime},\nabla^{2}F(z)(z-z^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=\exp(2s\alpha)\cdot\|z-z^{\prime}\|_{z}^{2}\;.

Substituting back into (
43
)
and integrating, we conclude
(using the function
\mu
from (
41
)):

\displaystyle D_{F}(z^{\prime},z)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\exp(2s\alpha)\|z-z^{\prime}\|_{z}^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle=\Big(\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\exp(2s\alpha)\,ds\Big)\cdot\|z-z^{\prime}\|_{z}^{2}=\mu(2\alpha)\cdot\|z^{\prime}-z\|^{2}_{z}\;.

For the lower bound on
D_{F}(z^{\prime},z)
, we similarly have
from (
44
) that

\displaystyle\big\langle z-z^{\prime},\nabla^{2}F(z_{s})(z-z^{\prime})\big\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\exp(-2s\alpha)\cdot\langle z-z^{\prime},\nabla^{2}F(z)(z-z^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=\exp(-2s\alpha)\cdot\|z-z^{\prime}\|_{z}^{2}\;.

Thus we can again substitute back
into (
43
)
and integrate to find

\displaystyle D_{F}(z^{\prime},z)

\displaystyle\geq\Big(\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\exp(-2s\alpha)\,ds\Big)\cdot\|z-z^{\prime}\|_{z}^{2}=\mu(-2\alpha)\cdot\|z^{\prime}-z\|^{2}_{z}\;,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Bound on Hessian remainder term.

Lemma E.10
.

Fix
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
.
Then
G_{F}(z,z^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
.
Moreover, if
\|z-z^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\alpha

for some
\alpha>0
, then

\|G_{F}(z,z^{\prime})\|_{z,*}\leq\nu(2\alpha)\cdot\|z-z^{\prime}\|_{z}\,.

Proof.

Recall from (
37
) that we define
the remainder term
G_{F}(z,z^{\prime})
in integral form by

G_{F}(z,z^{\prime})=\int_{0}^{1}(\nabla^{2}F(z-sv)-\nabla^{2}F(z))v\,ds\;,

(45)

where
v=z-z^{\prime}
.
Now for the first claim of the lemma,
recall from part (iii) of Proposition
E.1
that

\textnormal{Null}(\nabla^{2}F(z^{\prime\prime}))=\mathcal{S}
,
and thus
\nabla^{2}F(z^{\prime\prime})b=0
for every
z^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
and
b\in\mathcal{S}
.
Then for all
u\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
, by symmetry of
\nabla^{2}F
,
it follows for every
b\in\mathcal{S}
that

\langle b,\nabla^{2}F(z^{\prime\prime})u\rangle=\langle u,\nabla^{2}F(z^{\prime\prime})b\rangle=0\;.

By definition, this means
\nabla^{2}F(z^{\prime\prime})u\in\mathcal{S}^{\bot}

for every
z^{\prime\prime},u\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
,
and thus also
G_{F}(z,z^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
.

For the second claim of the lemma,
taking local (dual) norms in (
45
)
and using the triangle inequality:

\|G_{F}(z,z^{\prime})\|_{z,*}\leq\int_{0}^{1}\|(\nabla^{2}F(z-sv)-\nabla^{2}F(z))v\|_{z,*}\;ds\;.

(46)

Now repeating the previous argument,
observe that each term within the norm
\|\cdot\|_{z,*}

in the right-hand-side of
(
46
) belongs to
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
.
Similar to the proof of Lemma
E.9
,
let
s\in[0,1]
and write
z_{s}:=z-sv
.
For readability, we further write
P_{z}=\nabla^{2}F(z)
,

P_{z_{s}}:=\nabla^{2}F(z_{s})
, and
B_{s}:=P_{z_{s}}-P_{z}

for
s\in[0,1]
.
Moreover, as we are working over
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
,
we write
P_{z}^{-1}=(\nabla^{2}F(z))^{-1}

to denote the energy Hessian inverse
over
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
.
With this notation, our goal is thus to control the terms

\|B_{s}v\|_{z,*}
in the integrand of (
46
).
For this, define the matrix
M:=P^{-1/2}_{z}P_{z_{s}}P^{-1/2}_{z}
,
for which it holds that

P^{-1/2}_{z}B_{s}=(M-I)P^{1/2}_{z}\;.

(47)

Then taking local (dual) norms, we have

\displaystyle\|B_{s}v\|^{2}_{z,*}=\langle B_{s}v,P^{-1}_{z}B_{s}v\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle B_{s}v,P^{-1/2}_{z}P^{-1/2}_{z}B_{s}v\rangle

\displaystyle=\|P^{-1/2}_{z}B_{s}v\|^{2}_{2}

\displaystyle=\|(M-I)P^{1/2}_{z}v\|^{2}_{2}

(using (
47
))

\displaystyle\leq\|M-I\|^{2}_{2}\cdot\|P^{1/2}_{z}v\|^{2}_{2}\;.

(48)

Now to bound the two terms of (
48
),
observe first by definition of the local norm that

\|P^{1/2}_{z}v\|^{2}_{2}=\langle P^{1/2}_{z}v,P^{1/2}_{z}v\rangle=\langle v,P^{1/2}_{z}P^{1/2}_{z}v\rangle=\langle v,P_{z}v\rangle=\|v\|_{z}^{2}\;.

(49)

To bound
\|M-I\|^{2}_{2}
, recall from the (
LHS
) property that

\exp(-2s\alpha)P_{z}\preceq P_{z_{s}}\preceq\exp(2s\alpha)P_{z}\;,

(50)

which follows from the fact that

\|z-z_{s}\|_{\infty}=s\|z-z^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq s\alpha
by assumption.
Now due to the orderings of (
50
) and using
the definition of
M
, it follows by congruence that

M=P^{-1/2}_{z}P_{z_{s}}P^{-1/2}_{z}\preceq\exp(2s\alpha)\cdot P_{z}^{-1/2}P_{z}P^{-1/2}_{z}=\exp(2s\alpha)I

(51)

and similarly

M\succeq\exp(-2s\alpha)I\;.

(52)

Thus rearranging (
51
) and (
52
)
and using
\exp(2s\alpha)-1\geq 1-\exp(-2s\alpha)
,
it follows that

-(\exp(2s\alpha)-1)I\preceq M-I\preceq(\exp(2s\alpha)-1)I\;.

Thus, we have
\|M-I\|_{2}\leq(\exp(2s\alpha)-1)

by definition of spectral norm.
Combining this with (
49
) and substituting
back into (
48
) then yields

\|B_{s}v\|_{z,*}\leq(\exp(2s\alpha)-1)\cdot\|v\|_{z}\;.

Finally, putting this bound back into (
46
)
and integrating, we find

\|G_{F}(z,z^{\prime})\|_{z,*}\leq\int_{0}^{1}\|B_{s}v\|_{z,*}\;ds\leq\Big(\int_{0}^{1}(\exp(2s\alpha)-1)\,ds\Big)\cdot\|v\|_{z}=\nu(2\alpha)\cdot\|v\|_{z}\;,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix F
Details on Energy Dissipation Under OMWU

In this section, we develop the proof of
Lemma
4.1
,
which gives matching upper and lower bounds on
the one-step change in energy under (
OMWU Dual
).
We first restate the lemma, before giving
a more detailed overview of the proof:

See
4.1

Remark F.1

(Constant Stepsize)
.

Note that, for the sake of presentation,
we do not attempt to optimize the constraint

0<\eta\leq\frac{1}{4(54\sigma_{\max}+9)}

from Assumption
1
.
Up to the leading constants,
the conclusion of
Lemma
4.1

should hold under even larger absolute
constant stepsizes.

Simplifying Notation.

For readability, we write
\sigma=\sigma_{\max}=\|J\|_{2}

to denote the spectral norm of
J
.
By Proposition
B.8
,
recall that
\|J\|_{2}=\|A\|_{2}
.

F.1  Proof of Lemma
4.1

The proof follows two steps, both using several intermediate results.
Here, we will state the conclusions of these results and
derive their consequences for proving Lemma
4.1
.
We defer their full statements and proofs
to the subsequent subsections.

1. Exact expansion of change in energy
:

The starting point is to take a first-order Taylor expansion
of
F(z_{t+1})
around
z_{t}
.
Using the structure of the (
OMWU Dual
) update,
as well as the Hessian-based approximation
of gradient differences from Proposition
E.4
,
we derive an exact expansion
of
\Delta F(z_{t})=F(z_{t+1})-F(z_{t})
,
the one-step change in energy over the dual OMWU iterates.
Specifically, we prove the following:

Proposition
F.3

(One-step expansion).

For every
t\geq 1
, there exists

\mathcal{E}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
such that

\Delta F(z_{t})=-\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}+D_{F}(z_{t+1},z_{t})+\eta\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\rangle+\eta^{2}\mathcal{E}_{t}\;.

(53)

In particular, the error term
\mathcal{E}_{t}
is comprised
of four subterms that are defined explicitly
in (
58
).
The proof of Proposition
F.3

is in Section
F.2
.

2. Controlling the error terms
:

Notice that the first term of (
53
) is exactly the
desired dissipation term
-\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}

(up to a constant factor) appearing
in the statement of the lemma.
Thus, the remaining technical challenge is to ensure the
final three terms of (
53
) also scale
at most like
O(\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}})
,
with a constant factor that can be made less than 1 via
a sufficiently small stepsize
\eta
.

For this task, we distinguish between
the middle two terms of (
53
),
which we refer to as the
quadratic error terms
,
and the final term of (
53
),
which we refer to as the
cubic error terms
.
We derive the requisite bounds
on these two sets of terms using
the local norm machinery developed
in Section
E.3
,
which hold due to the (
LHS
) property
of the energy Hessian.
In more details:

Bounds on quadratic error terms
.
Under a sufficiently small stepsize, we establish:

Proposition
F.7

(Bound on Bregman divergence term).

Fix
L\geq 21
. For
\eta\leq\tfrac{1}{L\sigma}

and all
t\geq 1
:

D_{F}(z_{t+1},z_{t})\leq\big(\tfrac{18}{25}+\phi(L)\big)\cdot\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\;,

(54)

where
\phi(L)=\tfrac{36\exp(6/L)}{25L}

is a strictly decreasing function of
L

that can be made arbitrarily small.
In particular, it holds that
\tfrac{18}{25}+\phi(L)\leq\tfrac{149}{200}
,
when
L\geq 72
.

We obtain a similar bound for
the inner product involving the Hessian remainder term:

Proposition
F.8

(Bound on inner product term).

Fix
L\geq 21
. For
\eta\leq\frac{1}{L\sigma}
and all
t\geq 1
:

\big|\eta\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\rangle\big|\leq\psi(L)\cdot\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\;,

(55)

where

\psi(L)=\tfrac{54}{5L}+\tfrac{108\exp(6/L)}{5L^{2}}

is a strictly decreasing function of
L
that can be made
arbitrarily small. In particular, it holds that

\psi(L)\leq\tfrac{31}{200}
for all
L\geq 72
.

The proofs of Propositions
F.7

and
F.8
are given in
Section
F.3.2
.

Bounds on cubic error terms
.
For the cubic error terms
\eta^{2}\mathcal{E}_{t}
,
with
\mathcal{E}_{t}
as defined in (
58
),
we prove:

Proposition
F.9

(Bound on cubic eror terms).

Let
B=\tfrac{1}{5}(54\sigma+9)
.
Then for
\eta\leq\frac{1}{21\sigma}
and
t\geq 1
:

\eta^{2}|\mathcal{E}_{t}|\leq B\cdot\eta^{3}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\;.

(56)

The proof of Proposition
F.9

is in Section
F.3.3
.

Combining the pieces for the upper bound.

For the upper bound of Lemma
4.1
,
assume the stepsize satisfies

\eta\leq\min\{\frac{1}{72\sigma},\frac{1}{20B}\}=\tfrac{1}{4(54\sigma+9)}
,
which is the exact setting of Assumption
1
.
Then applying Propositions
F.7
,
F.8

and
F.9
,
the error terms of (
53
) can be collectively bounded as

D_{F}(z_{t+1},z_{t})+\eta\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\rangle+\eta^{2}\mathcal{E}_{t}\leq\big(\tfrac{9}{10}+\eta\tfrac{1}{20}\big)\cdot\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\;.

(57)

Then together with the expansion of (
53
), we conclude
for
\Delta F(z_{t})=F(z_{t+1})-F(z_{t})
that

\displaystyle\Delta F(z_{t})

\displaystyle\leq-\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}+\big(\tfrac{9}{10}+\eta\tfrac{1}{20}\big)\cdot\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle\leq-\tfrac{1}{20}\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\;,

where the final inequality is due to
\eta\leq 1
.
This yields the upper bound statement of the lemma.

Combining the pieces for the lower bound.

For the lower bound on
\Delta F(z_{t})
,
note that
D_{F}(z_{t+1},z_{t})\geq 0

by convexity of
F
. Thus again under the setting

\eta\leq\min\{\frac{1}{72\sigma},\frac{1}{20B}\}=\tfrac{1}{4(54\sigma+9)}
,
we apply Propositions
F.8

and
F.9
to bound

-\eta|\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\rangle|-\eta^{2}|\mathcal{E}_{t}|\geq-\big(\tfrac{31}{200}+\eta\tfrac{1}{20}\big)\cdot\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\;.

Then again substituting this bound into (
53
),
we obtain the matching lower bound

\displaystyle\Delta F(z_{t})

\displaystyle\geq-\eta\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}-\eta|\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\rangle|-\eta^{2}|\mathcal{E}_{t}|

\displaystyle\geq-\big(1+\tfrac{31}{200}+\eta\tfrac{1}{20}\big)\cdot\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle\geq-\big(1+\tfrac{1}{4}\big)\cdot\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}=-\tfrac{5}{4}\cdot\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\;,

which completes the proof of the lemma.

\blacksquare

Remark F.2

(Generality of Lemma
4.1
)
.

We note that the proof of Lemma
4.1

only requires that (1)
J=-J^{\top}
is a skew-symmetric
matrix and (2) the function
F
satisfies
the local Hessian stability property (
LHS
).
While this latter property is established specifically for
the log-sum-exp energy
F
in Proposition
4.3
,
Lemma
4.1
holds
for any other function satisfying this property.

Organization of remaining subsections.

The remainder of this section is organized as follows:

•

Section
F.2

gives the full proof of the exact one-step expansion
of
F(z_{t+1})-F(z_{t})
.

•

Section
F.3

gives the proofs of Propositions
F.7
,

F.8
, and
F.9

for bounding the error terms.
This also involves first introducing several technical lemmas
related to the stability of the OMWU iterates
(see Section
F.3.1
)

•

Section
F.4

gives the proof of the core
stability property of Proposition
F.6
.

•

Section
F.5

gives a useful bound on the initial change in energy

F(z_{1})-F(z_{0})
during the first step of the algorithm
(which contains no optimistic correction term).

F.2  Expanding the One-Step Change in Energy

Proposition F.3
.

Let
\{z_{t}\}
denote the iterates of (
OMWU Dual
).
Then for any
t\geq 2
:

\displaystyle F(z_{t+1})-F(z_{t})=-\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}+D_{F}(z_{t+1},z_{t})+\eta\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\rangle+\eta^{2}\mathcal{E}_{t}\;,

where
\mathcal{E}_{t}:=\mathcal{E}_{t,1}+\mathcal{E}_{t,2}+\mathcal{E}_{t,3}+\mathcal{E}_{t,4}

for

\begin{cases}\mathcal{E}_{t,1}:=\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),J\nabla^{2}F(z_{t})(z_{t}-z_{t-1})\rangle_{z_{t}}\\
\mathcal{E}_{t,2}:=\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),JG_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\rangle_{z_{t}}\\
\mathcal{E}_{t,3}:=-\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),J\nabla^{2}F(z_{t-1})(z_{t-1}-z_{t-2})\rangle_{z_{t}}\\
\mathcal{E}_{t,4}:=-\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),JG_{F}(z_{t-1},z_{t-2})\rangle_{z_{t}}\;.\end{cases}

(58)

Proof.

Let
\Delta F(z_{t}):=F(z_{t+1})-F(z_{t})
.
By definition of the Bregman divergence
D_{F}
,
recall that:

\Delta F(z_{t})=z_{t+1}-z_{t}=\langle\nabla F(z_{t}),z_{t+1}-z_{t}\rangle+D_{F}(z_{t+1},z_{t})\;.

(59)

Thus, to obtain the statement of the proposition,
our task is to expand the first-order term

\langle\nabla F(z_{t}),z_{t+1}-z_{t}\rangle

in (
59
).
For this, by the (
OMWU Dual
) update rule,
observe that we can write

z_{t+1}-z_{t}=-\eta J\nabla F(z_{t})-\eta J\big(\nabla F(z_{t})-\nabla F(z_{t-1})\big)\;.

(60)

Now for
k\geq 2
,
let us write
\mathcal{M}_{k}:=\nabla F(z_{k})-\nabla F(z_{k-1})
.
Then observe by substituting (
60
)
into the first-order term of (
59
) that

\displaystyle\big\langle\nabla F(z_{t}),z_{t+1}-z_{t}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=-\eta\big\langle\nabla F(z_{t}),J\nabla F(z_{t})\big\rangle-\eta\big\langle\nabla F(z_{t}),J\mathcal{M}_{t}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=-\eta\big\langle\nabla F(z_{t}),J\mathcal{M}_{t}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\eta\big\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),\mathcal{M}_{t}\big\rangle\;,

(61)

where the second and third equalities are due to the
skew-symmetry of
J=-J^{\top}
.
We can then further expand the term
\mathcal{M}_{t}
using
the Hessian-based approximation of gradient differences from
Proposition
E.4
.
Specifically, applying the proposition yields

\mathcal{M}_{t}=\nabla F(z_{t})-\nabla F(z_{t-1})=\nabla^{2}F(z_{t})(z_{t}-z_{t-1})+G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\;.

(62)

We can further use the update rule of (
OMWU Dual
)
at time
t
to write

\displaystyle z_{t}-z_{t-1}

\displaystyle=-2\eta J\nabla F(z_{t-1})-\nabla F(z_{t-2})

\displaystyle=-\eta J\nabla F(z_{t})+\eta J(\nabla F(z_{t})-\nabla F(z_{t-1}))-\eta J(\nabla F(z_{t-1})-\nabla F(z_{t-2}))

\displaystyle=-\eta J\nabla F(z_{t})+\eta J\mathcal{M}_{t}-\eta J\mathcal{M}_{t-1}\;.

(63)

Similar to
\mathcal{M}_{t}
, we can apply
Proposition
E.4
at time
t-1
to obtain

\mathcal{M}_{t-1}=\nabla F(z_{t-1})-\nabla F(z_{t-2})=\nabla^{2}F(z_{t-1})(z_{t-1}-z_{t-2})+G_{F}(z_{t-1},z_{t-2})\;.

(64)

Then combining (
62
), (
63
),
and (
64
), we can write

\displaystyle z_{t}-z_{t-1}=-\eta J\nabla F(z_{t})

\displaystyle+\eta J\nabla^{2}F(z_{t})(z_{t}-z_{t-1})

\displaystyle+\eta JG_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})

\displaystyle-\eta J\nabla^{2}F(z_{t-1})(z_{t-1}-z_{t-2})

\displaystyle-\eta JG_{F}(z_{t-1},z_{t-2})\;,

from which it follows by (
62
) that

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{t}=-\eta\nabla^{2}F(z_{t})J\nabla F(z_{t})

\displaystyle+\eta\nabla^{2}F(z_{t})J\nabla^{2}F(z_{t})(z_{t}-z_{t-1})

(65)

\displaystyle+\eta\nabla^{2}F(z_{t})JG_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})

\displaystyle-\eta\nabla^{2}F(z_{t})J\nabla^{2}F(z_{t-1})(z_{t-1}-z_{t-2})

\displaystyle-\eta\nabla^{2}F(z_{t})JG_{F}(z_{t-1},z_{t-2})

\displaystyle+G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\;.

Now recall from (
59
) and (
61
) that

\Delta F(z_{t})=D_{F}(z_{t+1},z_{t})+\eta\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),\mathcal{M}_{t}\rangle
.
Then plugging in the expansion of
\mathcal{M}_{t}
from (
65
)
and using the local norm and local inner product notation

\|\cdot\|_{z_{t}}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{z_{t}}
,
we conclude:

\displaystyle\Delta F(z_{t})=-\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}+D_{F}(z_{t+1},z_{t})

\displaystyle+\eta\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\rangle

(66)

\displaystyle+\eta^{2}\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),J\nabla^{2}F(z_{t})(z_{t}-z_{t-1})\rangle_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle+\eta^{2}\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),JG_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\rangle_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle-\eta^{2}\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),J\nabla^{2}F(z_{t-1})(z_{t-1}-z_{t-2})\rangle_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle-\eta^{2}\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),JG_{F}(z_{t-1},z_{t-2})\rangle_{z_{t}}\;.

Here, up to the
\eta^{2}
factor, the final four terms of (
66
)
are the quantities
\mathcal{E}_{t,1}
,
\mathcal{E}_{t,2}
,
\mathcal{E}_{t,3}
,
and
\mathcal{E}_{t,4}
defined in the statement of the proposition,
which concludes the proof.
∎

F.3  Controlling the Error Terms

F.3.1  Helper Propositions on Stability of OMWU Iterates

Here, we establish several key properties related
to the stability of the OMWU dual iterates:
First, the following proposition gives a bound in
\ell_{\infty}

norm on the distance between consecutive iterates:

Proposition F.4
.

Let
\{z_{t}\}
be iterates of (
OMWU Dual
).
Then for all
t\geq 0
:

\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{\infty}\leq 3\eta\sigma_{\max}\;\;\text{and}\;\;\|z_{t+2}-z_{t}\|_{\infty}\leq 6\eta\sigma_{\max}\;.

Proof.

Recall from the definition of (
OMWU Dual
) that
for
t\geq 0
:

z_{t+1}-z_{t}=-2\eta J\nabla F(z_{t})+\eta J\nabla F(z_{t-1})\;.

As
\nabla F(z)\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
for
all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
,
we have by definition of
J
that

\|J\nabla F(z)\|_{\infty}\leq a_{\max}
,
where
a_{\max}=\max_{(i,j)\in[m]\times[n]}|A(i,j)|
.
By Proposition
B.7
,
a_{\max}\leq\sigma=\|J\|_{2}
,
and thus by the triangle inequality

\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{\infty}\leq 3\eta\sigma
.
The second claim for
\|z_{t+2}-z_{t}\|_{\infty}

then follows similarly.
∎

The next proposition relates the
local norm of the dual increments
z_{t+1}-z_{t}

to the magnitude of the payoff vector
J\nabla F(z_{t})

in local norm:

Proposition F.5
.

Let
\{z_{t}\}
be the iterates of (
OMWU Dual
)
with
\eta\leq\tfrac{1}{21\sigma}
.
Then for
t\geq 1
:

\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{z_{t}}\leq\tfrac{6}{5}\cdot\eta\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|_{z_{t}}\;.

The proof of Proposition
F.5
,
which is central to establishing the bounds on the error terms,
relies further on the following stability property
of the OMWU iterates:

Proposition F.6
.

Let
\{z_{t}\}
be the iterates of (
OMWU Dual
)
with
\eta\leq\tfrac{1}{21\sigma}
.
Then for all
t\geq 2
, the following inequalities hold:

\text{(i)}\;\;\|z_{t-1}-z_{t-2}\|_{z_{t}}\leq 2\cdot\|z_{t}-z_{t-1}\|_{z_{t}}\;\;\text{and}\;\;\text{(ii)}\;\;\|z_{t}-z_{t-1}\|_{z_{t}}\leq 3\cdot\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{z_{t}}\;.

We defer the proof of Proposition
F.6

to Section
F.4
,
and we assume it is true for now.
We then proceed with the proof of
Proposition
F.5
:

Proof of Proposition
F.5

First, using the OMWU update rule at time
t+1

and taking local norms, we have

\displaystyle\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle=\eta\|J\nabla F(z_{t})+J(\nabla F(z_{t})-\nabla F(z_{t-1}))\|_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle\leq\eta\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|_{z_{t}}+\eta\|J(\nabla F(z_{t})-\nabla F(z_{t-1}))\|_{z_{t}}\;.

(67)

Thus to prove the proposition, it suffices to control
the second term of (
67
) in terms of
\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|_{z_{t}}
.

Controlling the second term of (
67
).

Recall from Proposition
E.4
that

\nabla F(z_{t})-\nabla F(z_{t-1})=\nabla^{2}F(z_{t})(z_{t}-z_{t-1})+G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\;.

Thus it follows by taking norms and applying the triangle inequality that

\displaystyle\|J(\nabla F(z_{t})-\nabla F(z_{t-1}))\|_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle=\|J\nabla^{2}F(z_{t})(z_{t}-z_{t-1})+JG_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\|_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle\leq\|J\nabla^{2}F(z_{t})(z_{t}-z_{t-1})\|_{z_{t}}+\|JG_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\|_{z_{t}}\;.

(68)

To control the two terms of (
68
),
the key is to upper bound both quantities in terms of

\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{z_{t}}
.
For this, we rely on the stability property established
in Proposition
F.6
.
Specifically, for the first term of (
68
) we have

\displaystyle\|J\nabla^{2}F(z_{t})(z_{t}-z_{t-1})\|_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle\leq\|J\|_{2}\cdot\|z_{t}-z_{t-1}\|_{z_{t}}

(by Lemma
E.7
)

\displaystyle\leq 3\sigma\cdot\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle\text{(by Proposition~\ref{prop:omwu-local-stability})}\;.

(69)

For the second term of (
68
), recall by
Proposition
F.4

that
\|z_{t}-z_{t-1}\|_{\infty}\leq 3\eta\sigma
.
Then using similar calculations as in the proof of
Part
(c)
of Lemma
F.11
,
and also applying Proposition
F.6
,
we have

\displaystyle\|JG_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\|_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle\leq\|JG_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\|_{2}

(by Proposition
E.5
)

\displaystyle\leq\sigma\cdot\|G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\|_{2}

(by definition of
\|J\|_{2}
)

\displaystyle\leq\sigma\cdot\|G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\|_{z_{t},*}

(by Proposition
E.5
)

\displaystyle\leq\sigma r_{1}\cdot\|z_{t}-z_{t-1}\|_{z_{t}}

(by Lemma
E.10
)

\displaystyle\leq 3\sigma r_{1}\cdot\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle\text{(by Proposition~\ref{prop:omwu-local-stability})}\;,

(70)

where for readability we write

r_{1}:=\nu(6\eta\sigma)

(for the function
\nu
as defined in (
41
)).
Then combining (
69
) and (
70
)
and substituting back into (
68
) yields

\|J(\nabla F(z_{t})-\nabla F(z_{t-1}))\|_{z_{t}}\leq 3\sigma(1+r_{1})\cdot\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{z_{t}}\;.

(71)

Combining the pieces.

Combining (
71
) with (
67
),
we can thus write

\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{z_{t}}\leq\eta\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|_{z_{t}}+3\eta\sigma(1+r_{1})\cdot\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{z_{t}}\;.

(72)

Using the definition of
r_{1}:=6\eta\sigma
and
the upper bound on
\nu(\cdot)
from
Lemma
E.8
, it is then
straightforward to check that

3\eta\sigma(1+r_{1})\leq\tfrac{1}{6}
when

\eta\leq\tfrac{1}{21\sigma}
.
Thus rearranging (
72
), we find

\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{z_{t}}\leq\Big(\frac{1}{1-3\eta(1+r_{1})\sigma}\Big)\cdot\eta\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|_{z_{t}}\leq\frac{6}{5}\cdot\eta\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|_{z_{t}}\;,

(73)

which concludes the proof.

\blacksquare

F.3.2  Bounds on Quadratic Error Terms

We bound the Bregman divergence term
in local norm as follows:

Proposition F.7
.

Let
\{z_{t}\}
be the iterates of (
OMWU Dual
)
with
\eta\leq\tfrac{1}{21\sigma}
.
Then for
t\geq 1
:

D_{F}(z_{t+1},z_{t})\leq\tfrac{36}{25}\cdot\mu(6\eta\sigma)\cdot\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\;.

Moreover, if
\eta\leq\tfrac{1}{L\sigma}

for
L\geq 72
, then

\tfrac{36}{25}\cdot\mu(6\eta\sigma)\leq\tfrac{18}{25}+\tfrac{36\exp(6/L)}{25L}\leq\tfrac{149}{200}
.

Proof.

Recall from Proposition
F.4

that
\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{\infty}\leq 3\eta\sigma
.
Then applying the upper bound on
D_{F}(z_{t+1},z_{t})

from Lemma
E.9
, we have

D_{F}(z_{t+1},z_{t})\leq\mu(6\eta\sigma)\cdot\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\;.

(74)

Further using the bound

\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{z_{t}}\leq\frac{6}{5}\cdot\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|_{z_{t}}

from Proposition
F.5
(which holds under
the constraint
\eta\leq\frac{1}{21\sigma}
), we find

D_{F}(z_{t+1},z_{t})\leq\tfrac{36}{25}\cdot\mu(6\eta\sigma)\cdot\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\;.

Now using the definition of
\mu(\cdot)
from (
41
) and the bounds of
Lemma
E.8
, observe that if
\eta\leq 1/(L\sigma)

for some positive
L>0
, then

\tfrac{36}{25}\cdot\mu(6\eta\sigma)\leq\tfrac{18}{25}+\tfrac{36\exp(6/L)}{25L}\;.

The right hand side of this term is decreasing with
L
.
It is then straightforward to check that, for
L\geq 72
,
then
\tfrac{36\exp(6/L)}{25L}\leq\tfrac{1}{40}
.
Thus for all such
L\geq 72
,
it holds that

\tfrac{18}{25}+\tfrac{36\exp(6/L)}{25L}\leq\tfrac{149}{200}
.
∎

For the inner product involving
G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})
,
we derive a similar bound:

Proposition F.8
.

Let
\{z_{t}\}
be the iterates of (
OMWU Dual
)
with
\eta\leq\frac{1}{21\sigma}
.
Then for
t\geq 1
:

\big|\eta\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\rangle\big|\leq\tfrac{18}{5}\cdot\nu(6\eta\sigma)\cdot\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\;.

Moreover, if
\eta\leq\tfrac{1}{L\sigma}

for
L\geq 72
, then

\tfrac{18}{25}\cdot\nu(6\eta\sigma)\leq\tfrac{54}{5L}+\tfrac{108\exp(6/L)}{5L^{2}}\leq\tfrac{31}{200}
.

Proof.

Applying the generalized Cauchy-Schwarz inequality
with the pair
(\|\cdot\|_{z_{t}},\|\cdot\|_{z_{t},*})
,
we have

\big|\eta\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\rangle\big|\leq\eta\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|_{z_{t}}\cdot\|G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\|_{z_{t},*}\;.

(75)

Since by Proposition
F.4

\|z_{t}-z_{t-1}\|_{\infty}\leq 3\eta\sigma
,
we apply the dual norm bound
of Lemma
E.10

on
G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})
to further write

\displaystyle\|G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\|_{z_{t},*}\leq\nu(6\eta\sigma)\cdot\|z_{t}-z_{t-1}\|_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle\leq 3\cdot\nu(6\eta\sigma)\cdot\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{18}{5}\cdot\nu(6\eta\sigma)\cdot\eta\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|_{z_{t}}\;.

(76)

Here, the second inequality comes from applying
the stability bound of Proposition
F.6
,
and the final inequality comes from
Proposition
F.5

(which holds under the constraint
\eta\leq 1/(21\sigma)
).
Combining expressions (
76
)
and (
75
) then yields the
first claim.

For the second claim, we have from the definition of
\nu(\cdot)

from (
41
) and the bounds of
Lemma
E.8
, that, if
\eta\leq 1/(L\sigma)

for some positive
L>0
, then

\tfrac{18}{5}\cdot\nu(6\eta\sigma)\leq\tfrac{54}{5L}+\tfrac{108\exp(6/L)}{5L^{2}}\;.

The right hand side of this term is decreasing with
L
,
and it is straightforward to check that
this term is at most
\tfrac{31}{200}

for all
L\geq 72
.
∎

F.3.3  Bounds on Cubic Error Terms

Proposition F.9
.

Let
\{z_{t}\}
be the iterates of (
OMWU Dual
)
with
\eta\leq\tfrac{1}{21\sigma}
,
where
\sigma=\|J\|_{2}
.
Recall the terms
\mathcal{E}_{t,1}
,

\mathcal{E}_{t,2}
,
\mathcal{E}_{t,3}
, and
\mathcal{E}_{t,4}

from (
58
) in Proposition
F.3

are given by:

\begin{cases}\mathcal{E}_{t,1}:=\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),J\nabla^{2}F(z_{t})(z_{t}-z_{t-1})\rangle_{z_{t}}\\
\mathcal{E}_{t,2}:=\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),JG_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\rangle_{z_{t}}\\
\mathcal{E}_{t,3}:=-\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),J\nabla^{2}F(z_{t-1})(z_{t-1}-z_{t-2})\rangle_{z_{t}}\\
\mathcal{E}_{t,4}:=-\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),JG_{F}(z_{t-1},z_{t-2})\rangle_{z_{t}}\;.\end{cases}

Then the following bounds hold:

\displaystyle|\mathcal{E}_{t,1}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{18}{5}\sigma\cdot\eta\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\,,\quad|\mathcal{E}_{t,2}|\leq\frac{3}{5}\cdot\eta\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\,,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{E}_{t,3}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{36}{5}\sigma\cdot\eta\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\,,\quad|\mathcal{E}_{t,4}|\leq\frac{6}{5}\cdot\eta\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\;.

Moreover, recalling that

\mathcal{E}_{t}:=\mathcal{E}_{t,1}+\mathcal{E}_{t,2}+\mathcal{E}_{t,3}+\mathcal{E}_{t,4}
,
then

\eta^{2}|\mathcal{E}_{t}|\leq\Big(\frac{54}{5}\sigma+\frac{9}{5}\Big)\cdot\eta^{3}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}
.

Proof.

For each term, we use a combination of
(i) (generalized) Cauchy-Schwarz
(ii) the bounds from
Lemma
E.7
and
Lemma
E.10
stemming
from the (
LHS
) property
and (iii) the local stability property of OMWU
from Proposition
F.6

to show the magnitude of the term
scales at most like a constant multiple of

\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|_{z_{t}}\cdot\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{z_{t}}
.
From there, we apply to each case Proposition
F.5
,
which establishes
\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{z_{t}}=O(\eta\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|_{z_{t}})
.
In more details:

•

Term
\mathcal{E}_{t,1}
.
Using Cauchy-Schwarz, we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{E}_{t,1}|

\displaystyle=\big|\big\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),J\nabla^{2}F(z_{t})(z_{t}-z_{t-1})\big\rangle_{z_{t}}\big|

\displaystyle\leq\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|_{z_{t}}\cdot\|J\nabla^{2}F(z_{t})(z_{t}-z_{t-1})\|_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle\leq\|J\nabla HF(z_{t})\|_{z_{t}}\cdot\|J\|_{2}\cdot\|z_{t}-z_{t-1}\|_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle(\text{by Lemma~\ref{lem:hessian-local-smooth}})

\displaystyle\leq\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|_{z_{t}}\cdot\sigma\cdot 3\cdot\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle(\text{by Proposition~\ref{prop:omwu-local-stability}})

\displaystyle\leq\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|_{z_{t}}\cdot\sigma\cdot 3\cdot\tfrac{6}{5}\eta\cdot\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle(\text{by Proposition~\ref{prop:local-norm-diff}})

\displaystyle=\tfrac{18}{5}\sigma\cdot\eta\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\;.

•

Term
\mathcal{E}_{t,2}
.
Recall by Proposition
F.4

that
\|z_{t}-z_{t-1}\|_{\infty}\leq 3\eta\sigma
.
Let
r_{1}:=\nu(6\eta\sigma)

for
\nu(\cdot)
as defined in (
41
).
Using the upper bound on
\nu(\cdot)
from
Lemma
E.8
, it is straightforward
to check that
r_{1}\leq\frac{1}{6}
when
\eta\leq\frac{1}{21\sigma}
.
Then using generalized Cauchy-Schwarz, we can write

\displaystyle|\mathcal{E}_{t,2}|

\displaystyle=\big|\big\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\big\rangle_{z_{t}}\big|

\displaystyle\leq\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|_{z_{t}}\cdot\|G_{F}(z_{t},z_{t-1})\|_{z_{t},*}

\displaystyle\leq\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|_{z_{t}}\cdot r_{1}\cdot\|z_{t}-z_{t-1}\|_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle(\text{by Lemma~\ref{lem:hessian-remainder-bound}})

\displaystyle\leq\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|_{z_{t}}\cdot r_{1}\cdot 3\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle(\text{by Proposition~\ref{prop:omwu-local-stability}})

\displaystyle\leq\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|_{z_{t}}\cdot r_{1}\cdot 3\cdot\tfrac{6}{5}\cdot\eta\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle(\text{by Proposition~\ref{prop:local-norm-diff}})

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{3}{5}\cdot\eta\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle(\text{using $r_{1}\leq\tfrac{1}{6}$ when $\eta\leq\tfrac{1}{28\sigma}$})\;.

•

Term
\mathcal{E}_{t,3}
.
We repeat nearly identitical steps as for

\mathcal{E}_{t,1}
, but for the third inequality we get
from the application of Proposition
F.6

that
\|z_{t-1}-z_{t-2}\|_{z_{t}}\leq 6\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{z_{t}}
.
Then it follows that:

\displaystyle|\mathcal{E}_{t,3}|=\big|\big\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),J\nabla^{2}F(z_{t-1})(z_{t-1}-z_{t-2})\big\rangle_{z_{t}}\big|\leq 2\cdot|\mathcal{E}_{t,1}|\leq\tfrac{36}{5}\sigma\cdot\eta\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\;.

•

Term
\mathcal{E}_{t,4}
.
We repeat nearly identical steps as for

\mathcal{E}_{t,2}
, and from the application of
Proposition
F.6

we get
\|z_{t-1}-z_{t-2}\|_{z_{t}}\leq 6\|z_{t+1}-z_{t}\|_{z_{t}}
.
Thus it follows that

\displaystyle|\mathcal{E}_{t,4}|\leq\big|\big\langle J\nabla F(z_{t}),G_{F}(z_{t-1},z_{t-2})\big\rangle_{z_{t}}\big|\leq 2\cdot|\mathcal{E}_{t,2}|\leq\tfrac{6}{5}\cdot\eta\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\;.

Summing up the constant multiplicative factors, we find

|\mathcal{E}_{t}|\leq|\mathcal{E}_{t,1}|+|\mathcal{E}_{t,2}|+|\mathcal{E}_{t,3}|+|\mathcal{E}_{t,4}|\leq\big(\tfrac{54}{5}\sigma+\tfrac{9}{5}\big)\cdot\eta\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\;,

which concludes the proof.
∎

F.4  Stability of Iterates Under Local Norm

In this section, we give the proof of
Proposition (
F.6
),
which establishes the stability of
the dual OMWU iterates in local norm.
Restated here:

See
F.6

F.4.1  Proof of Proposition
F.6

We start by proving inequality (i).
Inequality (ii) will follow from (i).

Shorthand notation.

First, for readability, we define several pieces of shorthand notation
that will be used throughout:
