\langle p^{\star},A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}q^{\star}\rangle=0
.

(ii)

Fix
A\in[-1,1]^{2\times 2}
,
and suppose that
w^{\star}
is an interior NE of
A
.
Let
v=\langle p^{\star},Aq^{\star}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}
,
and let the entries of
A
be given by

A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\;.

Define
\gamma=\frac{|a-v|}{\delta_{p}\delta_{q}}
,
and assume further that
A
is not a constant matrix
of the form
A=u\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}

for some
u\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then
w^{\star}
is the unique Nash
equilibrium of
A
,
0<\gamma\leq 4
, and

A=\gamma\cdot A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}+v\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\;.

Before giving the proof of Proposition
I.2
,
we first state and prove the following useful
facts about
2\times 2
zero-sum games with
an interior equilibrium:

Proposition I.3

(Interior NE implies uniqueness in
2\times 2
games)
.

Fix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
with entries

A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\;.

Suppose
A
has an interior NE

w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})

where
p^{\star}=(1-\mu_{p},\mu_{p})

and
q^{\star}=(1-\mu_{q},\mu_{q})
for
\mu_{p},\mu_{q}\in(0,1)
.
Let
v=\langle p^{\star},Aq^{\star}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}
,
and define
\phi=a-b-(c-d)
.
Then the following properties hold:

(i)

\phi=0
if and only if
A=u\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
is a constant
matrix for some
u\in\mathbb{R}
.

(ii)

If
\phi\neq 0
, then
w^{\star}
is the unique Nash
equilibrium of
A
.

(iii)

\phi=\frac{a-v}{\mu_{p}\mu_{q}}
.

Proof.

As
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})
is an interior NE of
A
,
by Part (i) of Proposition
B.1
, we must have

Aq^{\star}=v\mathbf{1}
and
A^{\top}p^{\star}=v\mathbf{1}
for
a constant
v\in\mathbb{R}
. Using the definition of
A
and

p^{\star}=(1-\mu_{p},\mu_{p})
and

q^{\star}=(1-\mu_{q},\mu_{q})
,
this yields the constraints

\begin{cases}a-(a-b)\cdot\mu_{q}=v\\
c-(c-d)\cdot\mu_{q}=v\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad\begin{cases}a-(a-c)\cdot\mu_{p}=v\\
b-(b-d)\cdot\mu_{p}=v\;.\end{cases}

(135)

Rearranging further yields the constraints

\begin{cases}\mu_{q}\cdot(a-b-(c-d))=a-c\\
\mu_{p}\cdot(a-b-(c-d))=a-b\;.\end{cases}

(136)

For Part (i) of the proposition, recall that
\phi=a-b-(c-d)
.
Now observe that if all
a=b=c=d
,
meaning
A
is a constant matrix, then trivially
\phi=0
.
In the other direction, if

\phi=0
, then (
136
) implies

a=c
and
a=b
.
By definition of
\phi
, this further implies
a=d
.
Thus if
\phi=0
, then
A
is a constant matrix.
In this degenerate case, every

(p,q)\in\textnormal{ri}(\Delta_{2}\times\Delta_{2})

is an NE.

For Part (ii), suppose that
\phi\neq 0
.
Then (
136
) implies that

\mu_{q}=(a-c)/\phi
and

\mu_{p}=(a-b)/\phi

have unique solutions that
also uniquely define
p^{\star}=(1-\mu_{p},\mu_{p})

and
q^{\star}=(1-\mu_{q},\mu_{q})
.
This also further precludes
A
from simultaneously having
a non-interior Nash equlibrium: if such an equilibrium
w^{\prime}
exists,
then every interior
\widetilde{w}
on the line segment between

w^{\prime}
and
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})

would also be a Nash equilibrium (which follows
by Part (ii) of Proposition
B.1
).
However, this contradicts the uniqueness of the
solution of (
136
).
Thus, in the case that
\phi\neq 0
,
an interior NE of
A

is the unique NE of
A
.

For Part (iii), observe from (
135
)
that
a-v=(a-c)\cdot\mu_{p}
.
Substituting the first equality of (
136
)
further gives
a-v=\phi\mu_{q}\mu_{p}
.
Rearranging for
\phi
yields the desired statement.
∎

We now proceed with the proof of Proposition
I.2
:

Proof of Proposition
I.2
.

We prove the two parts of the proposition separately.

Proof of Part (i).

Using the definition of
A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}
and

p^{\star}=(1-\delta_{p},\delta_{p})
and
q^{\star}=(1-\delta_{q},\delta_{q})
,
observe by a direct calculation that

\textstyle A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}\begin{pmatrix}1-\delta_{q}\\
\delta_{q}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{p}\delta_{q}(1-\delta_{q})-\delta_{p}(1-\delta_{q})\delta_{q}\\
-(1-\delta_{p})\delta_{q}(1-\delta_{q})+(1-\delta_{p})(1-\delta_{q})\delta_{q}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix}\;.

By a similar calculation, we find

A^{\top}_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}p^{\star}=0
.
Thus
A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}q^{\star}
and

A^{\top}_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}p^{\star}

are both constant vectors. Thus by Part (ii) of
Proposition
B.1
it follows
that
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})

is an NE of
A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}
,
and that

\langle p^{\star},A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}q^{\star}\rangle=0
.
Now observe that since
\delta_{p},\delta_{q}\in(0,1)
, the
equilbrium
w^{\star}
is interior by definition of
p^{\star}

and
q^{\star}
.
Moreover, by Definition
I.1
,A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}
is not a constant matrix.
Thus, by Parts (i) and (ii) of Proposition
I.3
,
it follows that
w^{\star}
is also the unique NE of
A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}
.

Proof of Part (ii).

Recalling the entries of
A\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
from the
statement of the proposition,
let
\phi=a-b-(c-d)
.
Recall that we assume
A
has an interior
Nash equilibrium
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})
,
and that
A
is not a constant matrix.
Thus, by Parts (i) and (ii) of Proposition
I.3
,

\phi\neq 0
, and
w^{\star}
is the unique Nash equilibium of
A
.
Now, let
v=\langle p^{\star},Aq^{\star}\rangle
,
and suppose that
\phi>0
.
In this case, define

\textstyle A^{\prime}=A-v\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}\;.

We will show that
A^{\prime}=\gamma\cdot A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}
,
for some
0<\gamma\leq 4
,
which will imply that
A=\gamma A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}+v\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
.

For this, observe by Proposition
B.1

that since
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})

is an interior NE of
A
with
v=\langle p^{\star},Aq^{\star}\rangle
,
then
Aq^{\star}=v\mathbf{1}
and
A^{\top}p^{\star}=v\mathbf{1}
.
Thus by definition of
A^{\prime}=A-v\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
,
it follows that both
A^{\prime}q^{\star}=0
and
A^{\prime\top}p^{\star}=0
.
Using the definition of the entries of
A^{\prime}
(which depend on
the entries of
A
) it is straightforward to show that this further implies

b-v=\tfrac{-(a-v)\cdot(1-\delta_{q})}{\delta_{q}},\;d-v=\tfrac{(a-v)\cdot(1-\delta_{p})(1-\delta_{q})}{\delta_{p}\delta_{q}},\;\text{and}\,c-v=\tfrac{-(a-v)\cdot(1-\delta_{p})}{\delta_{p}}\;.

(137)

By Part (iii) of Proposition
I.3
,
recall that
\phi=(a-v)/(\delta_{p}\delta_{q})
,
and recall that we assumed
\phi>0
.
Then factoring out
\phi
from each term
of (
137
), we find exactly that

A^{\prime}=\phi\cdot A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}
.
By definition of
A^{\prime}=A-v\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
,
we conclude
A=\phi\cdot A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}+v\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
.

In the case that
\phi<0
,
we instead define
A^{\prime}=v\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}-A
.
Repeating an identical set of calculations
again gives
A^{\prime}=\phi A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}
,
and thus
A=-\phi\cdot A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}+v\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
.
In either case, set
\gamma:=|\phi|
.
By definition of
\phi=a-b-(c-d)

and
A\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
, it follows
that
|\phi|\leq 4
.
Thus we conclude

A=\gamma A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}+v\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}

for
0<\gamma\leq 4
, which yields the statement of
Part (ii) of the proposition.

\blacksquare

Remark I.4
.

We note that the Part (ii) of
Proposition
I.2

is similar to Lemma 5 of
Cai et al., 2025
.
The key difference is that the “base” matrix
in their lemma is not
A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}
,
but a different
2\times 2
payoff matrix.
The key property of
A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}
is
that the value of the game is zero
(i.e.,
\langle p^{\star},A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}q^{\star}\rangle=0
).
This allows for a
more convenient primal-dual relationship under the
OMWU iterates
(c.f., Proposition
D.10
)
that we introduce in the sequel.

Finally, we also show that
A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}

is well-conditioned in the sense that
its minimum restricted singular value

\sigma_{\min}
is always an absolute constant.

Proposition I.5

(Minimum restricted singular value of Canonical Matrix)
.

Fix any
\delta_{p},\delta_{q}\in(0,1)
.
Let
A:=A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}\in[-1,1]^{2\times 2}

be the matrix from Definition
I.1
.
Let
\sigma_{\min}
be the minimum restricted singular value
from Definition
G.8
.
Then
\sigma_{min}=\frac{1}{2}
.

We note that the proof is the generalization of
and follows similarly to that of
Part (2) of Proposition
G.16
.
There, we established for the special case

\delta=\delta_{p}=\delta_{q}
that
\sigma_{\min}=\frac{1}{2}
.
Proposition
I.5
extends
this result to hold for general
\delta_{p},\delta_{q}\in(0,1)
.

Proof.

First, let
\sigma_{\min,n}
and
\sigma_{\min,m}

be the component-wise restricted singular
values from (
110
),
and recall from Proposition
G.11

that
\sigma_{\min}=\min\{\sigma_{\min}(A,\mathbf{1}^{\bot}),\sigma_{\min}(A^{\top},\mathbf{1}^{\bot})\}
.
Using calculations similar to the proof of Part (2) of
Proposition
G.16
,
we will show that

\sigma_{\min}(A,\mathbf{1}^{\bot})=\sigma_{\min}(A^{\top},\mathbf{1}^{\bot})=\frac{1}{2}
.
For this, recall by definition that

\textstyle\sigma_{\min}(A,\mathbf{1}^{\bot})=\inf_{v\in\mathbf{1}^{\bot}\setminus\{0\}}\,\frac{\|\Pi_{\mathbf{1}^{\bot}}(Av)\|_{2}}{\|v\|_{2}}\;.

Moreover, for any
v\in\mathbf{1}^{\bot}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
it follows that
v=c\cdot(-1,1)
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then using the definition of
A=A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}
,
and recalling that

\Pi_{\mathbf{1}^{\bot}}=I-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\bot}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
,
it follows by a direct calculation that

\Pi_{\mathbf{1}^{\bot}}(Av)=c\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{p}-(\delta_{p}-(1-\delta_{p}))/2\\
-(1-\delta_{p})-(\delta_{p}-(1-\delta_{p}))/2\end{pmatrix}=\frac{c}{2}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-1\end{pmatrix}\;.

It follows that
\|\Pi_{\mathbf{1}^{\bot}}(Av)\|_{2}=|c|\sqrt{2}/2
.
Moreover, we have by definition of

v=c\cdot(1,-1)
that
\|v\|_{2}=|c|\sqrt{2}
.
Thus we conclude
\sigma_{\min}(A,\mathbf{1}^{\bot})=1/2
.
By an identical calculation, we also find

\sigma_{\min}(A^{\top},\mathbf{1}^{\bot})=1/2
,
which completes the proof.
∎

I.2  Primal and Dual OMWU Iterates

In this section, we give details on the OMWU iterates
when run on the canonical

2\times 2
game
A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}

from Definition
I.1
.
These derivations are used to prove
the lower bounds of Theorems
4.10

and Theorems
5.1
,
and also the best-iterate upper bound of Theorem
5.2
.

In this setting, note that while the dual iterates

\{z_{t}\}
all belong to
\mathbb{R}^{4}
,
the effective dual space
\mathcal{Z}
(as introduced
in Section
3.2
)
is a two-dimensional subspace.
This allows for a convenient two-dimensional
representation of both the primal and dual iterates
that facilitates obtaining precise
control of the OMWU trajectory as
a function of the Nash equilibrium coordinates

\delta_{p}
and
\delta_{q}
.

Roadmap of subsection.

Section
I.2.1

presents structural properties of
the lower-dimensional effective dual space.
Section
I.2.2

then gives simplified expressions for the
energy function and primal-dual mapping
over the effective dual space.
Section
I.2.3

states the simplified primal and dual OMWU
updates when run on
A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}
.
Throughout, we assume the following setting:

Setting I.1

(
2\times 2
Setting Using Canonical Matrix)
.

For fixed
\delta_{p},\delta_{q}\in(0,1)
,
assume that
A:=A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}

is the canonical
2\times 2
payoff matrix
from Definition
I.1
.

In this setting, we will also make use of the
following simplified notation for readability:

Simplified notation and definitions.

Under Setting
I.1
,
let
\textnormal{LSE}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
denote the two-dimensional
log-sum-exp function,
and let
R:\Delta_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
denote the
two-dimensional negative-entropy function
(as defined in Section
3
).
Moreover, we write
\textnormal{sigmoid}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}

to denote the function given by

\textnormal{sigmoid}(u)=1/(1+\exp(-u))
for
u\in\mathbb{R}
.

I.2.1  Effective Dual Space

Recall from Section
3
that

\mathcal{Z}=\textnormal{Span}(J\mathcal{W})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2+2}
,
where
J=((0,A),(-A^{\top},0))\in\mathbb{R}^{4\times 4}

and
\mathcal{W}=\Delta_{2}\times\Delta_{2}
.
In order to introduce the lower-dimensional
representation of
\mathcal{Z}
, it will be more
convenient to further work with the individual
components of the dual variables
z=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2+2}
.
Specifically, we first introduce the component-wise
effective dual spaces
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

Definition I.6

(Component-wise effective dual spaces)
.

Fix
\delta_{p},\delta_{q}\in(0,1)
and assume
Setting
I.1
.
Let
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the linear subspaces
given by

\mathcal{X}:=\textnormal{Span}(A\Delta_{2})\;\text{and}\;\mathcal{Y}:=\textnormal{Span}(A^{\top}\Delta_{2})\;.

Given the product structure of
\mathcal{W}=\Delta_{2}\times\Delta_{2}
,
and using the definition of
J
, the following characterization
of
\mathcal{Z}=\textnormal{Span}(J\mathcal{W})
is immediate:

Proposition I.7
.

Fix
\delta_{p},\delta_{q}\in(0,1)
,
and assume Setting
I.1
.
Then
\mathcal{Z}=\textnormal{Span}(J\mathcal{W})=\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
.

Now define
p^{\star}=(1-\delta_{p},\delta_{p})

and
q^{\star}=(1-\delta_{q},\delta_{q})
,
and recall from Part (i) of Proposition
I.2

that
(p^{\star},q^{\star})
is the unique and interior NE
of
A:=A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}
.
The key property of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}

is that these spaces are orthogonal
to
p^{\star}
and
q^{\star}
, respectively.
Formally, we have the following refinement of
Proposition
D.6

for the present
2\times 2
setting:

Proposition I.8

(Component-wise orthogonality to Nash)
.

Fix
\delta_{p},\delta_{q}\in(0,1)
,
and assume Setting
I.1
.
Then for any
x=(x(1),x(2))\in\mathcal{X}

and
y=(y(1),y(2))\in\mathcal{Y}
, it holds that:

\displaystyle\langle x,p^{\star}\rangle=0

\displaystyle\implies\;x(2)=x(1)\cdot(1-(1/\delta_{p}))

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\langle y,q^{\star}\rangle=0

\displaystyle\implies\;y(2)=y(1)\cdot(1-(1/\delta_{q}))\;.

Proof.

First, fix
x\in\mathcal{X}
. By definition, this means that
there exists
k\geq 1
such that

x=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\tau_{i}Ap_{i}
, where all
\tau_{i}\in\mathbb{R}

and
p_{i}\in\Delta_{2}
.
Then for each
i\in[k]
, observe that

\langle\tau_{i}Ap_{i},p^{\star}\rangle=\tau_{i}\langle p_{i},A^{\top}p^{\star}\rangle=\tau_{i}\langle p_{i},0\rangle=0\;.

Here, the final equality is due to the fact
that
(p^{\star},q^{\star})
is an interior Nash equilibrium
of
A=A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}

with
\langle p^{\star},Aq^{\star}\rangle=0

(Part (i) of Proposition
I.2
),
which implies that

A^{\top}p_{\star}=0
is the zero vector
(Part (i) of Proposition
B.1
).
Thus
\langle x,p^{\star}\rangle=0

by definition of
x
.
Now, as
p^{\star}=(1-\delta_{p},\delta_{p})
,
a direct calculation and rearrangement then finds

\langle x,p^{\star}\rangle=x(1)\cdot(1-\delta_{p})+x(2)\cdot\delta_{p}=0\quad\implies\quad x(2)=x(1)\cdot(1-(1/\delta_{p}))\;.

This proves the first statement of the proposition.
The second statement follows using
an identical argument for
y\in\mathcal{Y}
.
∎

Before proceeding, we make the following two remarks:

Remark I.9

(Comparison with Proposition
D.6
)
.

For a matrix
A
with interior NE

w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})
,
Proposition
D.6

establishes for the general case that

\langle z,w^{\star}\rangle=0
for all
z=(x,y)\in\mathcal{Z}
.
In particular, this implies by definition of
J
that

\langle x,p^{\star}\rangle+\langle y,q^{\star}\rangle=0
.
Proposition
I.8

establishes under Setting
I.1

the slightly stronger
component-wise
property that
both
\langle x,p^{\star}\rangle=0

and
\langle y,q^{\star}\rangle=0
.
As seen in the proof
of Proposition
I.8
,
this property is a consequence of the fact that

\langle p^{\star},A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}q^{\star}\rangle=0
,
as proven in Part (i) of Proposition
I.2
.
Indeed, it is then straightforward to see that this
stronger property also holds for any other (general-dimension)

A
whose value of the game is zero.

Remark I.10

(Main consequence is lower-dimensional
effective dual space)
.

The key consequence of
Proposition
I.8

is that, under Setting
I.1
,
the dual variable components
x\in\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}

and
y\in\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
each lie on one-dimensional
subspaces. Thus, a dual variable
z=(x,y)\in\mathcal{Z}

can be fully specified by the first coordinates

(x(1),y(1))\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
of its components.
This means that the effective dual space
\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2+2}

is a
two-dimensional subspace
.
This will allow for additional, convenient
simplifications of the energy function
F

and the primal-dual relationship over
\mathcal{Z}
.
We note that similar approaches were used in
prior works of
(
Bailey and Piliouras, 2019a
, Section 3.2)

and
(
Lazarsfeld et al., 2025a
, Proposition 4.2)
,
albeit for different base matrices.

I.2.2  Primal-Dual Mapping Over Effective Dual Space

Under Setting
I.1
,
working over the lower-dimensional structure of
\mathcal{Z}

established by Proposition
I.8

allows for simplified chacterizations of
the energy function
F
and its gradient map.
Formally, we establish the following properties:

Proposition I.11

(Energy and primal-dual map over
\mathcal{Z}
)
.

Fix
\delta_{p},\delta_{q}\in(0,1)
and
assume Setting
I.1
.
Let
z=(x,y)\in\mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
,
and let
w=(p,q)=\nabla F(z)\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})=\textnormal{ri}(\Delta_{2})\times\textnormal{ri}(\Delta_{2})
.
Then the following relationships hold:

(i)

\textnormal{LSE}(x)=x(1)+\log\big(1+\exp\big(\frac{-x(1)}{\delta_{p}}\big)\big)

and

\textnormal{LSE}(y)=y(1)+\log\big(1+\exp\big(\frac{-y(1)}{\delta_{q}}\big)\big)
.

(ii)

p(1)=\textnormal{sigmoid}\big(\frac{x(1)}{\delta_{p}}\big)

and

q(1)=\textnormal{sigmoid}\big(\frac{y(1)}{\delta_{q}}\big)
.

(iii)

x(1)=\delta_{p}\cdot\log\big(\frac{p(1)}{1-p(1)}\big)

and

y(1)=\delta_{q}\cdot\log\big(\frac{q(1)}{1-q(1)}\big)
.

Proof.

Note that as
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
y\in\mathcal{Y}
, we have
by Proposition
I.8

that
x(2)=x(1)\cdot(1-(1/\delta_{p}))

and
y(2)=y(1)\cdot(1-(1/\delta_{q}))
.
We use this relationship to prove of
each of the three parts as follows:

Proof of Part (i).

By definition of
\textnormal{LSE}(\cdot)
and using
Proposition
I.8
,
we can compute

\displaystyle\textnormal{LSE}(x)

\displaystyle=\log\big(\exp(x(1))+\exp(x(2))\big)

\displaystyle=\log\big(\exp(x(1))+\exp\big(x(1)\cdot(1-\tfrac{1}{\delta_{p}})\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\log\big(\exp(x(1))\cdot(1+\exp\big(\tfrac{-x(1)}{\delta_{p}})\big)\big)=x(1)+\log\big(1+\exp\big(\tfrac{-x(1)}{\delta_{p}}\big)\big)\;.

The statement for
\textnormal{LSE}(y)
follows by an identical calculation.

Proof of Part (ii).

Recall by definition of
F
and the assumptions
of the proposition that

\nabla F(z)=(p,q)=(\textnormal{softmax}(x),\textnormal{softmax}(y))
.
Thus be definition of
softmax
and using
Proposition
I.8
,
we can write

\displaystyle p(1)=\frac{\exp\big(x(1)\big)}{\exp\big(x(1)\big)+\exp\big(x(2)\big)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp\big(x(1)\big)}{\exp\big(x(1)\big)+\exp\big(x(1)\cdot(1-\frac{1}{\delta_{p}})\big)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp\big(x(1)\big)}{\exp\big(x(1)\big)\cdot\big(1+\exp\big(\frac{-x(1)}{\delta_{p}}\big)\big)}=\textnormal{sigmoid}\Big(\frac{x(1)}{\delta_{p}}\Big)\;.

The statement for
q(1)
follows by an identical calculation.

Proof of Part (iii).

Recall for
u\in\mathbb{R}
that

\textnormal{sigmoid}^{-1}(u)=\log\big(\frac{u}{1-u}\big)
.
Thus by Part (ii), we have

\frac{x(1)}{\delta_{p}}=\textnormal{sigmoid}^{-1}(p(1))=\log\Big(\frac{p(1)}{1-p(1)}\Big)\;,

Rearranging gives

x(1)=\delta_{p}\cdot\log\big(\frac{p(1)}{1-p(1)}\big)
.
The claim for
y(1)
follows identically.
∎

Remark I.12

(Bijectivity of energy gradient map
over
\mathcal{Z}
.)
.

Note that Parts (ii) and (iii) of
Proposition
I.11

establish that, under Setting
I.1
,

\nabla F:\mathcal{Z}\to\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
is bijective.
Indeed, note that as the zero-sum game value under

A:=A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}

is zero (Part (i) of Proposition
I.2
),
the more general statements of Propositions
D.9

and
D.10
also together imply
that
\nabla F:\mathcal{Z}\to\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
is bijective.
For the
2\times 2
setting, the closed-form expression of
Part (iii) of Proposition
I.11

thus allows for recovering the coordinates of the
dual variable
z=(x,y)\in\mathcal{Z}
solely using the
coordinates of the corresponding primal variable

w\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.

Part (iii) of Proposition
I.11

also leads to several useful corollaries.
First, it allows for defining the coordinates of the unique
“dual” Nash variable
z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}
that maps
to the primal NE of
A:=A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}

under
\nabla F
. More formally:

Corollary I.13

(Dual Nash coordinate in
\mathcal{Z}
)
.

Fix
\delta_{p},\delta_{q}\in(0,1)

and assume Setting
I.1
.
Let
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
Let
z^{\star}=(x^{\star},y^{\star})\in\mathcal{Z}
be such that

\textstyle x^{\star}(1)=\delta_{p}\cdot\log\Big(\frac{1-\delta_{p}}{\delta_{p}}\Big)\;\;\text{and}\;\;y^{\star}(1)=\delta_{q}\cdot\log\Big(\frac{1-\delta_{q}}{\delta_{q}}\Big)\;.

Then
\nabla F(z^{\star})=w^{\star}
.

As a second corollary, note that
the expressions
\delta_{p}\log(p(1)/(1-p(1)))

and
\delta_{q}\log(q(1)/(1-q(1)))
are increasing
in
p(1)
and
q(1)
, respectively.
Similarly,
\textnormal{sigmoid}(x(1)/\delta_{p})

and
\textnormal{sigmoid}(y(1)/\delta_{q})

are increasing in
x(1)
and
y(1)
, respectively.
Together this implies the following further
relationships between
(p,q)\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})

and
(x,y)\in\mathcal{Z}
:

Corollary I.14
.

Fix
\delta_{p},\delta_{q}\in(0,1)

and assume Setting
I.1
.
Let
z=(x,y)\in\mathcal{Z}
, and
let
w=(p,q)=\nabla F(z)\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
Fix any
\rho\in(0,1)
.
Then the following relationships hold:

p(1)\geq\rho\,\iff\,x(1)\geq\delta_{p}\cdot\log\Big(\frac{\rho}{1-\rho}\Big)\;\;\text{and}\;\;q(1)\geq\rho\,\iff\,y(1)\geq\delta_{q}\cdot\log\Big(\frac{\rho}{1-\rho}\Big)\;.

I.2.3  OMWU Dynamics

Under Setting
I.1
,
the primal-dual relationships over the effective
dualspace
\mathcal{Z}
lead to simplified expressions
for the primal and dual OMWU updates.
Formally, we have the following:

Proposition I.15

(OMWU update rules
on canonical
2\times 2
matrix)
.

Fix
\delta_{p},\delta_{q}\in(0,1)

and assume Setting
I.1
.
Let
\{w_{t}\}
and
\{z_{t}\}
denote the
primal and dual iterates of OMWU on

A:=A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}
with
stepsize
\eta>0

initialized from
w_{0}=(p_{0},q_{0})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
,
where each
w_{t}=(p_{t},q_{t})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})

and
z_{t}=(x_{t},y_{t})\in\mathcal{Z}
.
Then for all
t\geq 1
:

\begin{cases}\,x_{t+1}(1)=x_{t}(1)-\eta\cdot\delta_{p}\left(q_{t}(1)-(1-\delta_{q})+\big(q_{t}(1)-q_{t-1}(1)\big)\right)\\
\,y_{t+1}(1)=y_{t}(1)+\eta\cdot\delta_{q}\left(p_{t}(1)-(1-\delta_{p})+\big(p_{t}(1)-p_{t-1}(1)\big)\right)\;,\end{cases}

and moreover

\begin{cases}\,p_{t+1}(1)=\textnormal{sigmoid}\left(\frac{x_{t+1}(1)}{\delta_{p}}\right)\\
\,q_{t+1}(1)=\textnormal{sigmoid}\left(\frac{y_{t+1}(1)}{\delta_{q}}\right)\;.\end{cases}

Proof.

First, recalling the structure of
A=A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}

from Definition
I.1
, a straightforward
calculation shows that, for
any
p,q\in\Delta_{2}
:

(Aq)(1)=\big(q(1)-(1-\delta_{q})\big)\cdot\delta_{p}\;\;\text{and}\;\;(A^{\top}p)(1)=\big(p(1)-(1-\delta_{p})\big)\cdot\delta_{q}\;.

(138)

Now recall by definition of the OMWU dual iterates
\{z_{t}\}

from (
4
) that, for all
t\geq 1
:

z_{t+1}=(x_{t+1},y_{t+1})=z_{t}-\eta(Jw_{t}+J(w_{t}-w_{t-1}))\;,

which means component-wise that

\displaystyle x_{t+1}

\displaystyle=x_{t}-\eta(Aq_{t}+A(q_{t}-q_{t-1}))

(139)

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad y_{t+1}

\displaystyle=y_{t}+\eta(A^{\top}p_{t}+A^{\top}(p_{t}-p_{t-1}))\;.

Considering only the first coordinates
of the components in (
139
)
and applying the simplifications from (
138
)
yields the first statement of the proposition.

For the second statement, observe that
all
z_{t}=(x_{t},y_{t})\in\mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
by definition.
Moreover, by Proposition
3.1
,
we have
w_{t}=\nabla F(z_{t})
for all
t\geq 0
.
Then applying Part (ii) of
Proposition
I.11

yields the second statement and concludes the proof.
∎

Remark I.16

(Leading coordinates suffice
for tracking OMWU iterates)
.

Due to Proposition
I.8
,
for
z=(x,y)\in\mathcal{Z}
,
observe that
the leading coordinates
x(1)\in\mathbb{R}
and
y(1)\in\mathbb{R}

fully specify
z
.
Similarly, for
w=(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}
,
the leading coordinates
p(1)\in[0,1]
and
q(1)\in[0,1]

fully specify
w
.
Thus, the simplified expressions
in Proposition
I.15

are sufficient for fully specifying the
the OMWU iterates
\{z_{t}\}
and
\{w_{t}\}

under Setting
I.1
.

I.3  Montonicity and Cycling Properties of OMWU Iterates

Using the simplified OMWU update expressions
from Proposition
I.15
,
we prove in this section an additional set of
monotonicty properties that the OMWU iterates satisfy
under Setting
I.1
.
In the statement of the lemma, we assume without loss
of generality that
\delta_{p},\delta_{q}<\frac{1}{2}
,
and that the stepsize
\eta
is bounded by an absolute constant.

Lemma I.17
.

Fix
0<\delta_{p}\leq\delta_{q}<\frac{1}{2}

and assume Setting
I.1
.
Let
\{w_{t}\}
and
\{z_{t}\}
be the primal and dual
iterates of OMWU with stepsize
0<\eta<\frac{1}{8}
,
initialized from some
w_{0}\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
.
For each
t\geq 1
,
let
\widetilde{x}_{t}=x_{t}(1),\widetilde{y}_{t}=y_{t}(1)

and
\widetilde{p}_{t}=p_{t}(1),\widetilde{q}_{t}=q_{t}(1)
.
Fix any
k\geq 1
.
Then the following hold:

(i)

Suppose
\widetilde{q}_{k-1}\leq 1-3\delta_{q}
.
Then:

\widetilde{x}_{k+1}-\widetilde{x}_{k}\geq\frac{\eta}{2}\cdot\delta_{p}\delta_{q}
.

(ii)

Suppose
\widetilde{q}_{k-1}\geq 1-\frac{\delta_{q}}{3}
.
Then:

\widetilde{x}_{k+1}-\widetilde{x}_{k}\leq-\frac{\eta}{3}\cdot\delta_{p}\delta_{q}
.

(iii)

Suppose
\widetilde{p}_{k-1}\leq 1-3\delta_{p}
.
Then:

\widetilde{y}_{k+1}-\widetilde{y}_{k}\leq-\frac{\eta}{2}\cdot\delta_{p}\delta_{q}
.

(iv)

Suppose
\widetilde{p}_{k-1}\geq 1-\frac{\delta_{p}}{3}
.
Then:

\widetilde{y}_{k+1}-\widetilde{y}_{k}\geq\frac{\eta}{3}\delta_{p}\delta_{q}
.

Proof.

We start by proving Part (i) of the lemma,
and we note that Parts (ii), (iii), and (iv) will follow
by nearly identical calculations.
For this, using the update rule of
Proposition
I.15
,
it follows that

\textstyle\widetilde{x}_{k+1}-\widetilde{x}_{k}\geq\frac{\eta}{2}\delta_{p}\delta_{q}\iff 1-\delta_{q}-2\widetilde{q}_{k}+\widetilde{q}_{k-1}\geq\frac{\delta_{q}}{2}\;.

(140)

For this, let
\widetilde{q}_{k-1}=1-\alpha_{k-1}

for
\alpha_{k-1}\in(0,1)
.
Our goal is to derive a lower bound on
\widetilde{q}_{k}

in terms of
\widetilde{q}_{k-1}
that will facilitate
establishing the inequality in the right hand side
of (
140
).
For this, observe again by the dual update rule
of Proposition
I.15
that

\widetilde{y}_{k}-\widetilde{y}_{k-1}=\eta\delta_{q}\big(\widetilde{p}_{k}-(1-\delta_{p})+\widetilde{p}_{k}-\widetilde{p}_{k-1}\big)\leq 2\eta\delta_{q}\,

(141)

where the inequality follows from the fact that

\widetilde{p}_{k}\leq 1
, that
\widetilde{p}_{k}-\widetilde{p}_{k-1}\leq 1
,
and that
\delta_{p}>0
.
Then by definition of
\widetilde{q}_{k}=\textnormal{sigmoid}(\widetilde{y}_{k}/\delta_{q})
,
and using the fact that
1-\textnormal{sigmoid}(u)=1/(1+\exp(u))

for any
u\in\mathbb{R}
, it follows that

\displaystyle 1-\widetilde{q}_{k}=\frac{1}{1+\exp(\widetilde{y}_{k}/\delta_{q})}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{1+\exp(\widetilde{y}_{k-1}/\delta_{q})\cdot\exp(2\eta)}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{(1+\exp(\widetilde{y}_{k-1}/\delta_{q}))\cdot\exp(2\eta)}=\exp(-2\eta)(1-\widetilde{q}_{k-1})\;.

Here, the first inequality comes from applying the bound

\widetilde{y}_{k}\leq\widetilde{y}_{k-1}+2\eta\delta_{q}

from (
141
),
the second inequality comes from

\exp(2\eta)\geq 1
,
and the final equality comes from

\widetilde{q}_{k-1}=\textnormal{sigmoid}(\widetilde{y}_{k-1}/\delta_{q})
.
As
\exp(-u)\geq 1-u
for all
u\in\mathbb{R}
, we then further have

1-\widetilde{q}_{k}\geq(1-2\eta)(1-\widetilde{q}_{k-1})
.
Using the definition
1-\widetilde{q}_{k-1}=\alpha_{k-1}
,
this means that
\widetilde{q}_{k}\leq 1-(1-2\eta)\alpha_{k-1}
,
and thus we have

\displaystyle 1-\delta_{q}-2\widetilde{q}_{k}+\widetilde{q}_{k-1}

\displaystyle\geq 1-\delta_{q}-2(1-(1-2\eta))\alpha_{k-1}+1-\alpha_{k-1}=(1-4\eta)\alpha_{k-1}-\delta_{q}\;.

Now under the assumption that

\widetilde{q}_{k-1}=1-\alpha_{k-1}\leq 1-3\delta_{q}
,
we have
\alpha_{k-1}\geq 3\delta_{q}
.
Moreover as we assume
\eta\leq 1/8
, we have

1-4\eta\geq 1/2
. This allows us to further write

\textstyle 1-\delta_{q}-2\widetilde{q}_{k}+\widetilde{q}_{k-1}\geq\frac{3}{2}\delta_{q}-\delta_{q}=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{q}\;,

which exactly establishes the right-hand side
of (
140
) and thus the statment of Part (i)
of the lemma.

The proof of Part (ii) follows via nearly identical calculations
as the poof of Part (i).
In particular, we further use the identity

\exp(u)=\textnormal{sigmoid}(u)/(1-\textnormal{sigmoid}(u))

for
u\in\mathbb{R}
and the fact that
\exp(2\eta)\leq 1+4\eta

when
\eta\leq 1/8
.
The proofs of Part (iii) and Part (iv) then follow
identically to Parts (i) and (ii), respectively.
This is due to the symmetry of the dual update rules
for
\widetilde{x}_{k+1}
and
\widetilde{y}_{k+1}
in
Proposition (
I.15
),
and thus we omit these calculations.
∎

I.3.1  Maximum Width of Dual Coordinates

In the following proposition, we establish
a bound on the coordinates of a dual variable
in terms of its energy value.
This is used in the sequel to establish an upper
bound on the maximum distance between any two dual
(and also primal) OMWU iterates. Formally:

Proposition I.18

(Energy bound implies dual coordinate bound)
.

Fix
\delta_{p},\delta_{q}\in(0,1)

and assume Setting
I.1
.
Fix
z=(x,y)\in\mathcal{Z}
, and let
\alpha>0
.
If
F(z)\leq\alpha
, then the following hold:

\textstyle-\big(\frac{\delta_{p}}{1-\delta_{p}}\big)\cdot\alpha\leq x(1)\leq\alpha\quad\text{and}\;\;-\big(\frac{\delta_{q}}{1-\delta_{q}}\big)\cdot\alpha\leq y(1)\leq\alpha\;.

Proof.

Fix
z=(x,y)\in\mathcal{Z}
, and let
\widetilde{x}=x(1)\in\mathbb{R}
and

\widetilde{y}=y(1)\in\mathbb{R}
.
Recall by definition of the energy function
F

and its simplification over
\mathcal{Z}
from
Proposition
I.11
that

F(z)=\textnormal{LSE}(x)+\textnormal{LSE}(y)=\big(\widetilde{x}+\log(1+\exp(-\widetilde{x}/\delta_{p}))\big)+\big(\widetilde{y}+\log(1+\exp(-\widetilde{y}/\delta_{q}))\big)\;.

Note that over
\mathcal{Z}
, both terms

\widetilde{x}+\log(1+\exp(-\widetilde{x}/\delta_{p})

and

\widetilde{y}+\log(1+\exp(-\widetilde{y}/\delta_{q})

are convex (in particular, minimized at the coordinates

x^{\star}(1)
and
y^{\star}(1)
from
Corollary (
I.13
))
and always positive for all
\delta_{p},\delta_{q}\in(0,1)
.
Thus, for any
z=(x,y)\in\mathcal{Z}
,
both
\textnormal{LSE}(x)\geq 0
and
\textnormal{LSE}(y)\geq 0
.
Therefore,
F(z)\leq\alpha
implies that both

\textnormal{LSE}(x)\leq\widetilde{x}+\log\big(1+\exp(-\widetilde{x}/\delta_{p}))\leq\alpha\;\;\text{and}\;\;\textnormal{LSE}(y)\leq\widetilde{y}+\log\big(1+\exp(-\widetilde{y}/\delta_{q}))\leq\alpha\;.

For the
\widetilde{x}
variable, observe further that

\log(1+\exp(-\widetilde{x}/\delta_{p}))\geq 0
, and thus

\widetilde{x}\leq\alpha-\log(1+\exp(-\widetilde{x}/\delta_{p}))\leq\alpha\;.

This proves the upper bound on
x(1)=\widetilde{x}
.
For the lower bound, recall that

\log(1+\exp(-u))\geq-u
for any
u\in\mathbb{R}
.
Thus if
\textnormal{LSE}(x)\leq\alpha
, then

\alpha\geq\widetilde{x}+\log(1+\exp(-\widetilde{x}/\delta_{p}))\geq\widetilde{x}-(\widetilde{x}/\delta_{p})=\widetilde{x}((\delta_{p}-1)/\delta_{p})=-\widetilde{x}((1-\delta_{p})/\delta_{p})\;.

Then rearranging gives

\widetilde{x}\geq-(\delta_{p}/(1-\delta_{p}))\cdot\alpha\;,

which proves the lower bound on
x(1)
.
The bounds on
y(1)=\widetilde{y}
follow by identical calculations.
∎

I.4  Structural Properties of Distances to Nash in 2x2 Setting

In this section, we give simplified expressions
for duality gap, TV distance, and KL divergence
under Setting
I.1

(i.e., when
A:=A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}

is the canonical matrix from Definition
I.1
).
For this, we consider throughout the following subsections

w=(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}=\Delta_{2}\times\Delta_{2}
, and we use the
notation
\widetilde{p}=p(1)\in[0,1]
and
\widetilde{q}=q(1)\in[0,1]
for readability.
Moreover, recall by Proposition
I.2

that the unique and interior NE
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})
of

A=A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}

is given by
p^{\star}=(1-\delta_{p},\delta_{p})

and
q^{\star}=(1-\delta_{q},\delta_{q})
.

I.4.1  Duality Gap under Setting
I.1

For
w=(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}
,
recall that duality gap is defined as

\textstyle\textnormal{DG}(w)=\max_{q^{\prime}\in\Delta_{2}}\langle q^{\prime},A^{\top}p\rangle-\min_{p^{\prime}\in\Delta_{2}}\langle p^{\prime},Aq\rangle.

Then in terms of the leading coordinates
\widetilde{p}=p(1)
and
\widetilde{q}=q(1)
,
we have by definition of
A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}
:

\displaystyle\textnormal{DG}(w)

\displaystyle=\max\big\{(A^{\top}p)(1),(A^{\top}p)(2)\big\}+\max\big\{(-Aq)(1),(-Aq)(2)\big\}

\displaystyle=\max\big\{(\widetilde{p}-(1-\delta_{p}))\delta_{q},(1-\delta_{p}-\widetilde{p})(1-\delta_{q})\big\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\max\big\{(1-\delta_{q}-\widetilde{q})\delta_{p},(\widetilde{q}-(1-\delta_{q}))(1-\delta_{p})\big\}\;.

Here, we also used the fact that
p(2)=1-\widetilde{p}

and
q(2)=1-\widetilde{q}
.
Thus
\textnormal{DG}(w)
has a piecewise linear structure,
which we can summarize depending on the
location of
(\widetilde{p},\widetilde{q})
relative to

(1-\delta_{p},1-\delta_{q})

(i.e., the leading coordinates of the unique and interior NE of

A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}
).
Then
\textnormal{DG}(w)
is given by:

\textnormal{DG}(w)=\begin{cases}(\widetilde{p}-(1-\delta_{p}))\delta_{q}+(\widetilde{q}-(1-\delta_{q}))(1-\delta_{p})&\text{if $\widetilde{p}\geq 1-\delta_{p}$, \, $\widetilde{q}\geq 1-\delta_{q}$}\\
(1-\delta_{p}-\widetilde{p})(1-\delta_{q})+(\widetilde{q}-(1-\delta_{q}))(1-\delta_{p})&\text{if $\widetilde{p}\leq 1-\delta_{p}$, \, $\widetilde{q}\geq 1-\delta_{q}$}\\
(1-\delta_{p}-\widetilde{p})(1-\delta_{q})+(1-\delta_{q}-\widetilde{q})\delta_{p}&\text{if $\widetilde{p}\leq 1-\delta_{p}$, \, $\widetilde{q}\leq 1-\delta_{q}$}\\
(\widetilde{p}-(1-\delta_{p}))\delta_{q}+(1-\delta_{q}-\widetilde{q})\delta_{p}&\text{if $\widetilde{p}\geq 1-\delta_{p}$, \, $\widetilde{q}\leq 1-\delta_{q}$}\;.\end{cases}

(142)

I.4.2  TV Distance under Setting
I.1

Recall by definition that the total variation distance

\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w)
is given by

\textstyle\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w)=\textnormal{TV}(p^{\star},p)+\textnormal{TV}(q^{\star},q)=\frac{1}{2}\|p^{\star}-p\|_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\|q^{\star}-q\|_{1}\;.

As
p=(\widetilde{p},1-\widetilde{p})
and
p^{\star}=(1-\delta_{p},\delta_{p})
,
observe that

\textstyle\|p^{\star}-p\|_{1}=|1-\delta_{p}-\widetilde{p}|+|\delta_{p}-(1-\widetilde{p})|=2\cdot|1-\delta_{p}-\widetilde{p}|\;.

Similarly,
\|q^{\star}-q\|_{1}=2\cdot|1-\delta_{q}-\widetilde{q}|
.
Thus we have that

\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w)=\textnormal{TV}(p^{\star},p)+\textnormal{TV}(q^{\star},q)=|1-\delta_{p}-\widetilde{p}|+|1-\delta_{q}-\widetilde{q}|\;.

(143)

I.4.3  KL Divergence under
Setting
I.1

Recall by definition that the KL divergence

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)
is given by

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)=\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p)+\textnormal{KL}(q^{\star},q)\;.

Then under this setting, the components
\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p)

and
\textnormal{KL}(q^{\star},q)
can be simplified as:

\displaystyle\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{2}p^{\star}(i)\log\Big(\frac{p^{\star}(i)}{p(i)}\Big)=(1-\delta_{p})\cdot\log\Big(\frac{1-\delta_{p}}{\widetilde{p}}\Big)+\delta_{p}\cdot\log\Big(\frac{\delta_{p}}{1-\widetilde{p}}\Big)

(144)

\displaystyle\textnormal{KL}(q^{\star},q)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{j=1}^{2}q^{\star}(j)\log\Big(\frac{q^{\star}(j)}{q(j)}\Big)=(1-\delta_{q})\cdot\log\Big(\frac{1-\delta_{q}}{\widetilde{q}}\Big)+\delta_{p}\cdot\log\Big(\frac{\delta_{q}}{1-\widetilde{q}}\Big)\;.

I.4.4  Comparison of Levelsets Between Distances

In the following set of figures, we plot the levelsets
of
\textnormal{DG}(w)
,
\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w)
, and
\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)

for the
2\times 2
setting using the simplified
expressions from Section
I.4
.
We plot the levelsets over the leading coordinates

(p(1),q(1))

for three different settings of
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})
.
In each subplot,
w^{\star}
is denoted by the yellow star.
The figures highlight a fundamental distinction between
the geometries of these distance functions over the simplex.

Figure
4
: Uniform NE.

Here,
p^{\star}=q^{\star}=(0.5,0.5)
.

Figure 4:
\textnormal{DG}(w)
,
\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w)
, and
\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)
levelsets
in the
2\times 2
setting
for
p^{\star}=q^{\star}=(0.5,0.5)
.

Figure
5
:
Boundary NE in one component.

Here,
p^{\star}=(0.9,0.1)

and
q^{\star}=(0.5,0.5)
.

Figure 5:
\textnormal{DG}(w)
,
\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w)
, and
\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)
levelsets
in the
2\times 2
setting
for
p^{\star}=(0.9,0.1)
, and
q^{\star}=(0.5,0.5)
.

Figure
6
:
Boundary NE in both components.

Here,
p^{\star}=q^{\star}=(0.9,0.1)
.

Figure 6:
\textnormal{DG}(w)
,
\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w)
, and
\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)
levelsets
in the
2\times 2
setting
for
p^{\star}=q^{\star}=(0.9,0.1)
.

Discussion.

Observe that the geometry of the levelsets of each distance function
is highly dependent on the location of the Nash equilibrium
w^{\star}
.
In particular, when
w^{\star}
is closer to the simplex boundary
(Figure
5
and Figure
6
),
the
\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)
levelsets become
“squished” near the boundary closest to
w^{\star}
.
Note in these regions that a primal variable
w=(p,q)
can be
simultaneously close to
w^{\star}
in
TV
distance
(which is symmetric), yet still far away in
\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)
.
This property is captured in our uniform best-iterate lower
bound of Theorem
K.1
, Part (1).
Additionally, observe in Figure
6

that for
w^{\star}
close to a vertex, a primal variable

w=(p,q)
with, for example,
p(1)=0.95

and
q(1)=0.5
, can simultaneously be close in
\textnormal{DG}(w)
,
but far in
\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w)
and
\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)
.
This property is captured by the separation in uniform
best-iterate guarantees between

TV
and
KL
(via the constant lower bound of
Theorem
5.1
)
and
DG
(via the
\widetilde{O}(T^{-1/2})

best-iterate rate of Theorem
5.2
).
Finally, note that the location of the NE
w^{\star}

in the example of Figure
5

roughly corresponds to that of the hard
2\times 2

instance used in the results of
Cai et al., 2024
.

Appendix J
Details on Lower Bound for Universal Last-Iterate Convergence in KL

This section gives the proof of
Theorem
4.10
,
which gives a universal lower bound on the
convergence rate of OMWU in
KL
divergence.
We first restate the theorem:

See
4.10

Observe that in Theorem
4.10
,
the dependence on
\delta
in the lower bound
matches that in the upper bound of Theorem
4.7
,
indicating that our analysis is tight.
The proof of the theorem follows two steps that we outline here:

High-level overview of proof.

Let
\{w_{t}\}
and
\{z_{t}\}
denote the
primal and dual iterates of the OMWU algorithm.
We have by Corollary
D.7

and the lower bound on energy dissipation
from Lemma
4.1
that, for all
t\geq 0
:

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t+1})-\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})=F(z_{t+1})-F(z_{t})\geq-\tfrac{5}{4}\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\;.

Recall in Proposition
4.4

that we established a lower bound on the dissipation
term
\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}
. This translates
into the upper bound on last-iterate convergence rate in
KL

in Theorem
4.7
.
For the present lower bound of Theorem
4.10
,
we first establish in Proposition
J.1

an analogous
non-uniform upper bound
on the
dissipation term
\|J\nabla F(z)\|^{2}_{z}
.

In particular, this upper bound is a structural and general
property that we prove for the
2\times 2
setting,
and which holds over a region of the primal space
\mathcal{W}

near the simplex boundary. This leads to a much tighter
bound compared to the crude uniform upper bound of
Proposition
G.14
.
Similar to the lower bound on
\|J\nabla F(z)\|^{2}_{z}
,
the upper bound of Proposition
J.1

contains a non-uniform multiplicative factor
that depends on the minimum coordinates
p_{\min}

and
q_{\min}
of the corresponding primal variable
w=(p,q)=\nabla F(z)
.
This further indicates that the local state-dependence
in the lower bound of Proposition
4.4

is necessary.

Then, the last-iterate lower bound of Theorem
4.10

follows by constructing a
2\times 2
payoff matrix and a set of
initializations where we establish
an upper bound on
p_{t,\min}
and
q_{t,\min}

that is exponentially small in the minimum Nash coordinate

\delta
, and that holds for all iterates
t
over a fixed time horizon.
This relies on the preliminaries for OMWU
in the
2\times 2
setting that we developed in Section
I
.

Roadmap of the section.

In Section
J.1
,
we state and prove the intermediate result of
Proposition
J.1
.
The full proof of Theorem
4.10

then follows in Section
J.2
.

J.1  Non-Uniform Upper Bound on Dissipation Term

In this section, we establish a non-uniform

upper bound
on the dissipation term

\|J\nabla F(z)\|^{2}_{z}
.
For simplicity, we focus on the low-dimensional
setting where
A\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
.

Proposition J.1

(Non-uniform upper bound on dissipation term)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}

have a unique and interior Nash equilibrium

w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})
such that

p^{\star}=q^{\star}=(1-\delta,\delta)

for
\delta\in(0,0.5)
.
Let
\sigma_{\max}=\|A\|_{2}
.
Consider
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2+2}
and

w=(p,q)=\nabla F(z)

such that
p_{\min}=p(2)\leq\delta

and
q_{\min}=q(2)\leq\delta
.
Define
\widehat{w}_{\min}=\max(p_{\min},q_{\min})
.
Then:

\|J\nabla F(z)\|^{2}_{z}\leq 8\cdot\sigma^{2}_{\max}\cdot\widehat{w}_{\min}\cdot\delta\cdot\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)\;.

Proof.

To prove the lemma, we leverage the primal
perspective of the non-uniform gradient domination
result from Proposition
G.13
.
Specifically, using part (i) of Proposition
G.13
,
we have

\|J\nabla F(z)\|^{2}_{z}=\textnormal{Var}_{p}(Aq)+\textnormal{Var}_{q}(A^{\top}p)\;.

(145)

We will thus derive upper bounds on the individual
terms in (
145
) via the following steps:

1. Exact Characterization of Variance in
2\times 2
Setting.

Let
p=(p(1),p(2))
and
q=(q(1),q(2))
.
We first prove that

\displaystyle\textnormal{Var}_{p}(Aq)

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot p(1)\cdot p(2)\cdot\|A\|^{2}_{2}\cdot\|q-q^{\star}\|^{2}_{2}

(146)

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\textnormal{Var}_{q}(A^{\top}p)

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot q(1)\cdot q(2)\cdot\|A\|^{2}_{2}\cdot\|p-p^{\star}\|^{2}_{2}\;.

(147)

To prove (
146
),
let
v=Aq\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
By definition of
\textnormal{Var}_{p}(v)
from (
35
),
it is straightforward to compute in the two-dimensional case
that
\textnormal{Var}_{p}(v)=p(1)\cdot p(2)\cdot(v(1)-v(2))^{2}
.
Now, let let
\mathbf{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
denote the all-ones vector.
By Proposition
F.8
,
we have
Aq^{\star}=c\mathbf{1}
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
.
Thus, we can write:

v=Aq=A(q^{\star}+(q-q^{\star}))=c\mathbf{1}+A(q-q^{\star})\;.

Further letting
d=(1,-1)
, it follows that we can write
and simplify

\displaystyle v(1)-v(2)=\langle d,v\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle d,c\mathbf{1}+A(q-q^{\star})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle d,A(q-q^{\star})\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\|d\|_{2}\cdot\|A(q-q^{\star})\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{2}\cdot\|A\|_{2}\cdot\|q-q^{\star}\|_{2}\;.

Substituting this bound on
v(1)-v(2)
into the
definition of
\textnormal{Var}_{p}(v)
then exactly yields
the desired inequality of (
146
).
By an identical calculation, we also
find
\textnormal{Var}_{q}(A^{\top}p)\leq 2q(1)q(2)\|A\|^{2}_{2}\|p-p^{\star}\|^{2}_{2}
,
where we additionally use the fact that

\|A\|_{2}=\|A^{\top}\|_{2}
.

2. Relating KL to Euclidean Distance to Nash.

Next, we prove the following relationships between

KL
and euclidean distance to Nash:

\displaystyle\|p-p^{\star}\|^{2}_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\cdot\max(p_{\min},\delta_{p})\cdot\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p)

(148)

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\|q-q^{\star}\|^{2}_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\cdot\max(q_{\min},\delta_{q})\cdot\textnormal{KL}(q^{\star},q)\;.

(149)

We start by proving (
148
).
For this, recall from Section
3

that for
w=(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}
, we write

R(w)=R_{m}(q)+R_{n}(q)

to denote the (separable) negative entropy function,
where
R_{m}=-\textnormal{Ent}_{m}
and
R_{n}=-\textnormal{Ent}_{n}
.
Moreover,
\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)=D_{R}(w^{\star},w)
is the
Bregman divergence of
R
,
and more specifically
\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p)=D_{R_{m}}(p^{\star},p)

and
\textnormal{KL}(q^{\star},q)=D_{R_{n}}(q^{\star},q)
.
Thus, by using the integral form of Bregman divergences
(see, e.g.,
Nocedal and Wright, 2006
, Theorem 2.1),
it follows that

\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p)=D_{R_{m}}(p^{\star},p)=\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\big\langle(p-p^{\star}),\nabla^{2}R_{m}(p_{s})(p-p^{\star})\big\rangle\,ds\;,

(150)

where we write
p_{s}=p-s(p-p^{\star})
for
s\in[0,1]
.

We simplify the integrand in (
150
) as follows:
first, by definition in (
36
),
we have for all
s\in[0,1]

\textstyle\nabla^{2}R_{m}(p_{s})=\textnormal{Diag}\left(\big(\frac{1}{p_{s}(1)},\dots,\frac{1}{p_{s}(n)}\big)\right)\;.

Thus for each
s
, using the facts that
m=2
and

p^{\star}=(1-\delta_{p},\delta_{q})

we can simplify

\displaystyle\big\langle(p-p^{\star}),\nabla^{2}R_{m}(p_{s})(p-p^{\star})\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathop{\textstyle{\sum}}\nolimits_{i=1}^{2}\frac{(p(i)-p^{\star}(i))^{2}}{p_{s}(i)}

\displaystyle=\frac{(p(1)-(1-\delta_{p}))^{2}}{p_{s}(1)}+\frac{(1-p(1)-\delta_{p})^{2}}{p_{s}(2)}

\displaystyle=\big(p(1)-(1-\delta_{p})\big)^{2}\cdot\bigg(\frac{1}{p_{s}(1)}+\frac{1}{p_{s}(2)}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\|p-p^{\star}\|^{2}_{2}\cdot\frac{1}{p_{s}(1)\cdot p_{s}(2)}\;.

Here, the last line follows from
the fact that
\|p-p^{\star}\|^{2}_{2}=2\cdot(p(1)-(1-\delta_{p}))^{2}

in this two-dimensional setting.
Thus, we can further rewrite (
150
) as

\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p)=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\|p-p^{\star}\|^{2}_{2}\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(1-s)}{p_{s}(1)\cdot p_{s}(2)}\,ds\;.

(151)

To further derive a lower bound on (
151
),
we will show a uniform upper bound on

p_{s}(1)\cdot p_{s}(2)=p_{s}(2)\cdot(1-p_{s}(2))
for all
s\in[0,1]
.
For this, we recall by the assumptions of the lemma
that
p^{\star}(2)=\delta=\min(p^{\star}(1),p^{\star}(2))\leq\frac{1}{2}
,
and also
p(2)=p_{\min}=\min(p(1),p(2))\leq\frac{1}{2}
.
In particular, this also implies that
p_{s}(2)=\min(p_{s}(1),p_{s}(2))\leq\frac{1}{2}

for all
s\in[0,1]
.
Then by concavity, for all
s\in[0,1]
:

p_{s}(2)\cdot(1-p_{s}(2))\leq p_{s}(2)\leq\max(p(2),\delta_{p})=\max(p_{\min},\delta_{p})\;.

Substituting this uniform bound into (
151
),
and using the fact that
\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)ds=\frac{1}{2}
,
we find

\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p)\geq\frac{1}{4}\cdot\|p-p^{\star}\|^{2}_{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\max(p_{\min},\delta_{p})}\;.

Rearranging then yields the desired inequality from (
148
).
Using identical calculations similarly yields the inequality
from (
149
).

3. Combining the Pieces.

Combining the inequalities of (
146
) and (
147
)
from Step 1 and
expressions (
148
) and (
149
)
from Step 2, we find

\displaystyle\textnormal{Var}_{p}(Aq)

\displaystyle\leq 8\cdot\|A\|^{2}_{2}\cdot p_{\min}\cdot\max(q_{\min},\delta)\cdot\textnormal{KL}(q^{\star},q)

(152)

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\textnormal{Var}_{q}(A^{\top}p)

\displaystyle\leq 8\cdot\|A\|^{2}_{2}\cdot q_{\min}\cdot\max(p_{\min},\delta)\cdot\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p)\;.

Here, we additionally used the fact that

p(1)p(2)=(1-p(2))p(2)\leq p(2)=p_{\min}

and similarly
q(1)q(2)\leq q_{\min}
.
Then under the assumptions that
q_{\min}<\delta

and
p_{\min}<\delta
, and using the
notation
\widehat{w}_{\min}=\max(p_{\min},q_{\min})
,
substituting the bounds of (
152
)
into (
145
) and simplifying yields

\displaystyle\|J\nabla H(z)\|^{2}_{z}

\displaystyle=\textnormal{Var}_{p}(Aq)+\textnormal{Var}_{q}(A^{\top}p)

\displaystyle\leq 8\cdot\|A\|^{2}_{2}\cdot\big(\widehat{w}_{\min}\cdot\delta\cdot\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p)+\widehat{w}_{\min}\cdot\delta\cdot\textnormal{KL}(q^{\star},q)\big)

\displaystyle=8\cdot\sigma^{2}_{\max}\cdot\widehat{w}_{\min}\cdot\delta\cdot\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})\;.

This yields the statement of the lemma.
∎

J.2  Proof of Theorem
4.10

We begin the proof by giving a more detailed overview of the main steps:

(1)

We consider the payoff matrix
A\in[-1,1]^{2\times 2}

that is the instantiation of the canonical
2\times 2

matrix
A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}

from Definition
I.1
,
with
\delta_{p}=\delta_{q}=\delta\in(0,1/2)
.
Given the time horizon
T
, we set
\delta=1/T
,
which means the interior NE
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})
of
A

will be near a vertex of
\mathcal{W}
.
We also consider initializations
w_{0}\in\mathcal{W}

that are near the same vertex of
\mathcal{W}
.

(2)

Using the control of the OMWU trajectory on
the matrix
A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}
established
in Section
I.2.3
,
our setting of
\delta
and the set of initializations
allows for proving that the minimum coordinates

p_{t,\min}
and
q_{t,\min}
of the OMWU iterates
have an
upper bound

of
\exp(-1/\delta)
that holds uniformly
over all iterates
t\in[T]
.
Note that this matches (in its dependence on
\delta
)
the general uniform lower bound on

p_{t,\min}
and
q_{t,\min}
from Lemma
H.2
.

(3)

Then, using the
lower bound
on energy dissipation
from Lemma
4.1

and the
upper bound
on the dissipation term

\|J\nabla F(z)\|^{2}_{z}
from
Proposition
J.1
,
we obtain the final rate.

We proceed to give the details of these steps below:

Setup of payoff matrix and set of initializations.

Given
T\geq 3
,
let
\delta=1/T<1/2
.
Let
A:=A_{\delta,\delta}\in[-1,1]^{2\times 2}

be the instantiation of the matrix
A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}

from Definition
I.1

with
\delta_{p}=\delta_{q}=\delta
.
Thus
A
is the matrix

A=A_{\delta,\delta}=\begin{pmatrix}\delta^{2}&-\delta(1-\delta)\\
-\delta(1-\delta)&(1-\delta)^{2}\end{pmatrix}\;.

Moreover, recall from Proposition
I.2

that
A
has the unique and interior NE

w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})

with
p^{\star}=q^{\star}=(1-\delta,\delta)\in\textnormal{ri}(\Delta_{2})
.
By Proposition
B.7
,
we further have
\sigma_{\max}:=\|A\|_{2}\leq 2
.

Now, for
w=(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}
with
p=(p(1),p(2))

and
q=(q(1),q(2))
, we define the following
regions
\mathcal{C}_{\delta},\mathcal{P}_{\delta,0},\mathcal{Q}_{\delta,0}\subset\textnormal{ri}(\Delta_{2})
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{\delta,0}

\displaystyle:=\big\{(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}\;:\;\exp(-3/\delta)\leq p(2),q(2)\leq\exp(-2/\delta)\big\}

(153)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\delta}

\displaystyle:=\big\{(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}\;:\;p(2),q(2)\leq\exp(-1/\delta)\big\}\;.

Observe by definition that
\mathcal{W}_{\delta,0}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
,
and that
\mathcal{W}_{\delta,0}
has positive Lebesgue measure
for any
\delta>0
.
Now let
\{w_{t}\}
be the primal iterates of OMWU initialized
from
w_{0}\in\mathcal{W}_{\delta,0}
,
and let
\{z_{t}\}
denote the corresponding dual OMWU iterates.
For all
t\geq 0
,
let
w_{t}=(p_{t},q_{t})\in\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
and
z_{t}=(x_{t},y_{t})\in\mathcal{Z}
.

Tracking the OMWU trajectory.

We show for all
t\in[T]
that

w_{t}\in\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
.
By definition of
\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
,
this implies that the minimum coordinate of each primal iterate

p_{t}
and
q_{t}
remains bounded by
\exp(-1/\delta)

for all times
t\in[T]
.

First, using the bounds on the trajectory of the dual iterates
from Lemma
I.17
,
observe that if ever
w_{t-1},w_{t}\in\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
,
then
x_{t+1}(1)-x_{t}(1)<0
and
y_{t+1}(1)-y_{t}(1)>0
.
By the primal-dual relationships of Proposition
I.15
,
this further means that

p_{t+1}(1)-p_{t}(1)<0
and
q_{t+1}(1)-q_{t}(1)>0
.
By definition of
\mathcal{W}_{\delta,0}
, this latter bound implies
that
q_{t}(1)\geq 1-\exp(-2/\delta)\geq 1-\exp(-1/\delta)
for all
t\in[T]
.
Thus, in order to show that
w_{t}\in\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
for all
t\in[T]
,
it suffices to establish for all iterates that
p_{t}(1)\geq 1-\exp(-1/\delta)
.

For this, using the primal-dual relationship of
Corollary
I.14
, observe
for any
t\geq 0
that

\textstyle p_{t}(1)\geq 1-\exp(-1/\delta)\;\iff\;x_{t}(1)\geq\delta\cdot\log\big(\frac{1-\exp(-1/\delta)}{\exp(-1/\delta)})\;.

(154)

Moreover, as
w_{0}\in\mathcal{W}_{\delta,0}
, it additionally holds
by definition of
p_{0}(1)=1-p_{0}(2)
and
by Corollary
I.14
that

\textstyle p_{0}(2)\leq\exp(-2/\delta)\;\implies\;p_{0}(1)\geq 1-\exp(-2/\delta)\;\implies\;x_{0}(1)\geq\delta\cdot\log\big(\frac{1-\exp(-2/\delta)}{\exp(-2/\delta)})\;.

(155)

Now, observe further from the dual update rule of
Proposition
I.15
that we can crudely bound

x_{t+1}(1)-x_{t}(1)=-\eta\delta\cdot\big(q_{t}(1)-(1-\delta)\big)-\eta\delta\big(q_{t}(1)-q_{t-1}(1)\big)\geq-2\eta\delta\;.

(156)

Here, the inequality is due to the fact
that
\delta,q_{t}(1),q_{t-1}(1)\in(0,1)
.
Thus, for all
t\in[T]
it holds
by (
155
)
and (
156
) that

x_{t}(1)\geq x_{0}(1)-t\cdot 2\eta\delta\geq x_{0}(1)-t\delta
,
where the last inequality is due to the assumption
\eta\leq 1/2
.
Now recalling that
\delta=1/T
,
this further gives for all
t\in[T]
that

\displaystyle x_{t}(1)\geq x_{0}(1)-T\delta

\displaystyle\geq\delta\cdot\log\big(\tfrac{1-\exp(-2/\delta)}{\exp(-2/\delta)})-1

\displaystyle=\delta\cdot\log\big(\tfrac{1-\exp(-2/\delta)}{\exp(-2/\delta)})-\delta\cdot\log\big(\exp(\tfrac{1}{\delta}))

\displaystyle=\delta\cdot\log\big(\tfrac{1-\exp(-2/\delta)}{\exp(1/\delta)\cdot\exp(-2/\delta)}\big)

\displaystyle=\delta\cdot\log\big(\tfrac{1-\exp(-2/\delta)}{\exp(-1/\delta)}\big)\geq\delta\cdot\log\big(\tfrac{1-\exp(-1/\delta)}{\exp(-1/\delta)}\big)\;.

Thus
x_{t}(1)
satisfies the bound
in (
154
) for all
t\in[T]
,
which implies that
also
p_{t}(1)\geq 1-\exp(-1/\delta)

for all
t\in[T]
.
Together with the previously established fact that
all
q_{t}(1)\geq 1-\exp(-1/\delta)
,
we then have by definition
that
w_{t}\in\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
for all
t\in[T]
.

Bound on change in KL divergence
.

Since
w_{t}\in C_{\delta}
for all
t\in[T]
,
it follows by definition of
C_{\delta}
for all
t\geq 0
that

p_{t,\min}=\min(p_{t}(1),p_{t}(2))\leq\exp(-1/\delta)\;\;\text{and}\;\;q_{t,\min}=\min(q_{t}(1),q_{t}(2))\leq\exp(-1/\delta)\;.

Letting
\widehat{w}_{t,\min}=\max(p_{t,\min},q_{t,\min})
,
we then also have
\widehat{w}_{t,\min}\leq\exp(-1/\delta)

for all
t\geq 0
.
It follows that each iterate
w_{t}

satisfies the preconditions of Proposition
J.1
.
This allows us to bound

\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}\leq 8\cdot\sigma^{2}_{\max}\cdot{\widehat{w}_{t,\min}}\cdot\delta\cdot\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})\leq 16\cdot\exp(-1/\delta)\cdot\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})\;.

(157)

Here, we use the fact that
\sigma_{\max}\leq 2
and
\delta\leq 1/2

by definition of
A
.
Moreover, recall from Corollary
D.7

that the primal and dual OMWU iterates satisfy

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t+1})-\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})=F(z_{t+1})-F(z_{t})

for all times
t\geq 0
. Then applying the

lower bound
on energy dissipation from
Lemma
4.1
, we can further bound

\displaystyle\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t+1})-\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})

\displaystyle=F(z_{t+1})-F(z_{t})

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{5}{4}\cdot\eta^{2}\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}

\displaystyle\geq-20\cdot\eta^{2}\exp(-1/\delta)\cdot\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})\;.

Here, the final inequality comes from the
upper bound on
\|J\nabla F(z_{t})\|^{2}_{z_{t}}

from expression (
157
).
Rearranging, we find

\displaystyle\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t+1})

\displaystyle\geq\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})\cdot(1-20\eta^{2}\cdot\exp(-1/\delta))

\displaystyle\geq\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})\cdot\exp(-40\eta^{2}\cdot\exp(-1/\delta))\;.

(158)

The final inequality follows from the
fact that
20\eta^{2}\cdot\exp(-1/\delta)\leq 1/2

under the constraint on
\eta
in Assumption
1
,
and from the bound
1-u\geq\exp(-2u)
for all
u\in[0,0.5]
.
As the bound in (
158
) holds uniformly
over all
t\in[T]
, we conclude for all
t\geq 0
that

\displaystyle\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})

\displaystyle\geq\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{0})\cdot\exp(-40\eta^{2}\cdot\exp(-1/\delta)\cdot t)\;.

(159)

Initial bound on KL divergence
.

It remains to show a constant upper bound on
\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{0})

under initializations
w_{0}=(p_{0},q_{0})\in\mathcal{W}_{\delta,0}
.
For this, using the definition of

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{0})=\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p_{0})+\textnormal{KL}(q^{\star},q_{0})

from (
144
), we have

\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p_{0})=(1-\delta)\cdot\log\big(\tfrac{1-\delta}{p_{0}(1)}\big)+\delta\cdot\log\big(\tfrac{\delta}{p_{0}(2)}\big)\leq\delta\cdot\log\big(\tfrac{\delta}{p_{0}(2)}\big)\;.

Here, the inequality comes from the fact that

p_{0}(1)>1-\delta

for all
(p_{0},q_{0})\in\mathcal{W}_{\delta,0}
.
Additionally using the fact that
p_{0}(2)>\exp(\frac{-3}{\delta})

for all
(p_{0},q_{0})\in\mathcal{W}_{\delta,0}
,
we can further derive the bound

\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p_{0})\leq\delta\cdot\log\big(\tfrac{\delta}{\exp(-3/\delta)}\big)=\delta\log\delta-\delta\big(\tfrac{-3}{\delta}\big)\leq 3\;.

The final inequality is due
to
\delta\log\delta\leq 0
for
\delta\leq 1
.

By an identical calculation,
we also find
\textnormal{KL}(q^{\star},q_{0})\leq 3

for all
w_{0}=(p_{0},q_{0})\in\mathcal{W}_{\delta,0}
.
Thus it follows for all such
w_{0}
that

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{0})\leq 6
, which concludes the proof.

\blacksquare

Appendix K
Details on Uniform Convergence Rate Lower Bounds

In this section, we develop the proof of the
uniform best-iterate convergence rate lower bounds
of Theorem
5.1
that
were introduced in Section
5
.
To restate the theorem:

See
5.1

Theorem
5.1

establishes a separation between

TV
distance and
KL
divergence
and duality gap in terms of
uniform best-iterate convergence guarantees for OMWU:
while a quantitative uniform best-iterate convergence
rate is attainable in duality gap
under the uniform initialization
(due to
Cai et al., 2025
,
and also Theorem
5.2
),
no such rate is possible under the stronger
notions of
TV
and
KL
divergence.

Using the intuition for the geometric bottlenecks
developed in Section
4
,
the hard instances in the proof of the theorem have a
(symmetric) NE close to a simplex vertex, and with

\delta
scaling inversely with
T
. In this regime,
we rely on the primal-dual relationship of the OMWU iterates
to exactly track the OMWU dynamics in the
2\times 2
setting
(see Section
I
for these preliminaries).
Subsequently, this allows for establishing on these instances
that
all
T
iterates
of OMWU remain a bounded
distance away from equilibrium
in
TV
and
KL
.

Additional Lower Bounds in KL and DG
under Non-Uniform Initializations.

In addition to Theorem
5.1
,
we also prove a second set of uniform best-iterate convergence
rate lower bounds that further highlight the
different geometries of the distance functions
and their interplay with the OMWU iterates.
Specifically, when initialized from some positive measure
subsets of non-uniform distributions
(that may be near the boundary of the simplex),
we prove two additional lower bounds:
(i) all iterates of OMWU can be a constant distance away
from Nash in
KL
, but convergent in
TV
and
DG
,
and (ii) all iterates of OMWU can also be a constant
distance away from Nash in
DG
.
Formally:

Theorem K.1

(Additional Uniform Best-Iterate Lower Bounds)
.

For every
T\geq 3
, it holds that:

(1)

There exists
A\in[-1,1]^{2\times 2}

with interior NE
w^{\star}
and a positive
measure set of initializations, not including the
joint uniform distributions, such that,
for the iterates
\{w_{t}\}
of (
OMWU
)
on
A
with stepsize
\eta
satisfying Assumption
1
:

\text{for all $t\in[T]$, both}\begin{cases}\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w_{t})\leq O(T^{-1})\\
\textnormal{DG}(w_{t})\leq O(T^{-1})\end{cases}\text{while also}\;\min\nolimits_{t\in[T]}\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})\geq\tfrac{1}{6}\;.

(2)

There exists
A\in[-1,1]^{2\times 2}

with interior NE
w^{\star}
and a positive
measure set of initializations, not including the
joint uniform distributions, such that,
for the iterates
\{w_{t}\}
of (
OMWU
)
on
A
with stepsize
\eta
satisfying Assumption
1
:

\min\nolimits_{t\in[T]}\textnormal{DG}(w_{t})\geq\tfrac{1}{6}\;.

Part (1) of Theorem
K.1

is based on the
universal
last-iterate lower bound in

KL
from Theorem
4.10
.
Note that this result gives a separation in uniform
best-iterate convergence guarantees between

TV
and
KL

(which is not established by Theorem
5.1
).

Moreover, Part (2) of Theorem
K.1

further shows that, when initialized near the simplex boundary,
no best-iterate convergence guarantee is possible
in duality gap.
Note that this does not contradict the uniform best-iterate
convergence rate upper bounds of
Theorem
5.2

and of
Cai et al., 2025
, both of which hold under
the assumption of joint uniform initializations.

In the following subsections, we proceed
to give the proofs of Theorem
5.1

and Theorem
K.1
,
both of which use the preliminaries for OMWU
on the canonical
2\times 2
instance
A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}

from Section
I
.

K.1  Proof of Theorem
5.1

To prove the theorem, we instantiate the payoff matrix
A

and the set of intializations as follows:

•

Set
A:=A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}\in[-1,1]^{2\times 2}

from Definition
I.1

with
\delta:=\delta_{p}=\delta_{q}=\frac{1}{6\exp(T)}
.

•

Initialize
w_{0}=(p_{0},q_{0})\in\mathcal{P}_{0}\times\mathcal{Q}_{0}
,
where:

\mathcal{P}_{0}=\{p\in\textnormal{ri}(\Delta_{2})\,:\,p(1)\leq 1/2\}\;\text{and}\;\mathcal{Q}_{0}=\{q\in\textnormal{ri}(\Delta_{2})\,:\,q(1)\leq 1/2\}\;.

Let
\mathcal{W}_{0}=\mathcal{P}_{0}\times\mathcal{Q}_{0}\subset\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
,
and observe that
\mathcal{W}_{0}
has positive Lebesgue measure,
and that
\mathcal{P}_{0}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{0}

both contain the uniform distributions

p_{0}=(1/2,1/2)
and
q_{0}=(1/2,1/2)
.
Moreover, by the setting of
A
, we have
by Corollary
I.14

that the corresponding dual initializations

(x_{0},y_{0})\in\mathcal{Z}

under OMWU satisfy
x_{0}(1)\leq 0

and
y_{0}(1)\leq 0
for all
(p_{0},q_{0})\in\mathcal{W}_{0}
.

The proof then proceeds by (1) tracking the trajectory
of the dual OMWU iterates over the time horizon
T
,
which (2) leads to a lower bound on the
TV distance and KL divergence from Nash
that holds uniformly over all iterates.
We proceed to give the details of these two steps:

Bounds on Trajectory.

For every
t
, let
w_{t}=(p_{t},q_{t})\in\textnormal{ri}(\Delta_{2})

and
z_{t}=(x_{t},y_{t})\in\mathcal{Z}
denote the primal
and dual iterate of OMWU.
Moreover, let
\widetilde{p}_{t}=p_{t}(1)\in(0,1)
,
\widetilde{q}_{t}=q_{t}(1)\in(0,1)

and
\widetilde{x}_{t}=x_{t}(1)\in\mathbb{R}
,
\widetilde{y}_{t}=y_{t}(1)\in\mathbb{R}

denote the leading coordinate of each variable.
Under
A:=A_{\delta,\delta}
, Proposition
I.15

then gives for
t+1\geq 1
the simplified OMWU dual update rule of

\textstyle\widetilde{x}_{t+1}-\widetilde{x}_{t}=\eta\delta\cdot\big(1-\delta-\widetilde{q}_{t}+\widetilde{q}_{t-1}-\widetilde{q}_{t}\big)<2\eta\delta\leq\delta\;.

Here, the inequality comes from
0<\widetilde{q}_{t},\widetilde{q}_{t-1}<1
and

\delta>0
, and the assumption that
\eta<1/2
.
It follows that, under the initialization
\widetilde{p}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
:

\textstyle\widetilde{x}_{t}\leq\widetilde{x}_{0}+\delta\cdot t\leq\delta T\leq\delta\log\big(\frac{1}{6\delta}\big)\leq\delta\log\big(\frac{1-3\delta}{3\delta}\big)\;\text{for all $t\in[T]$}\;.

Here, the first inequality comes from
\widetilde{x}_{0}\leq 0
, the second inequality comes from the
setting of
\delta=1/(6\exp(T))
,
and the third inequality holds when
\delta\leq 1/6
,
which is true for all
T\geq 2
.
By Corollary
I.14
, it then follows that

\textstyle\widetilde{x}_{t}\leq\delta\log\big(\frac{1-3\delta}{3\delta}\big)\;\iff\;\widetilde{p}_{t}\leq 1-3\delta\;\;\text{for all $t\in[T]$.}

(160)

By Part (iii) of Lemma
I.17
,
this further implies that

\widetilde{y}_{t+1}-\widetilde{y}_{t}\leq 0
for all
t\in[T]
.
Under the initializations
\widetilde{q}_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}

and again using Corollary
I.14
,
it then holds that

\textstyle\widetilde{y}_{t}\leq\widetilde{y}_{0}\leq 0\;\iff\;\widetilde{q}_{t}\leq\frac{1}{2}\;\;\text{for all $t\in[T]$.}

(161)

Bounds on Distance to Nash.

Recall for
A=A_{\delta,\delta}
that
the unique NE
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})
of
A
is given
by
p^{\star}=q^{\star}=(1-\delta,\delta)
.
Then by the definition of
\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w_{t})

from (
143
),
and using the bound
\widetilde{q}_{t}\leq 1/2
from (
161
),
we have for all
t\in[T]
that

\textstyle\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w_{t})=|1-\delta-\widetilde{p}_{t}|+|1-\delta-\widetilde{q}_{t}|\geq|1-\delta-\widetilde{q}_{t}|\geq|\frac{1}{2}-\delta|\;.

(162)

As
\delta\leq 1/6
for all
T\geq 2
,
it then follows that

\textstyle\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w_{t})\geq\frac{1}{3}\;\;\text{for all $t\in[T]$.}

Finally, by the relationship

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})\geq\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w_{t})^{2}

from Corollary
B.6
,
we also have

\textstyle\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})\geq\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w_{t})^{2}\geq\frac{1}{9}\;\;\text{for all $t\in[T]$},

which concludes the proof.
\blacksquare

K.2  Proof of Theorem
K.1

In this section we give the proof of
Theorem
K.1
.
We prove the two parts separately:

K.2.1  Proof of Part 1

The proof follows as a corollary
of the
universal
last-iterate lower bound
in
KL
from Theorem
4.10
.
There, recall from the proof that
for all
T\geq 3
, the game instance
is
A=A_{\delta,\delta}
with

\delta=1/T
.
We start by recalling the properties established
in the proof of Theorem
4.10
:

Conclusions from
Proof of Theorem
4.10
.

Recall from (
153
) that
we define the set of initializations
\mathcal{W}_{\delta,0}\subset\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})

and the set
\mathcal{C}_{\delta}\subset\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{\delta,0}

\displaystyle:=\big\{(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}\;:\;\exp(-3/\delta)\leq p(2),q(2)\leq\exp(-2/\delta)\big\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\delta}

\displaystyle:=\big\{(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}\;:\;p(2),q(2)\leq\exp(-1/\delta)\big\}\;.

The proof of Theorem
4.10
established
the following two conclusions:
for all initial
w_{0}\in\mathcal{W}_{\delta,0}
and
for all times
t\in[T]
:

w_{t}\in\mathcal{C}_{\delta}\;\text{and}\;\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})\geq\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{0})\cdot\exp(-40\eta^{2}\cdot\exp(-1/\delta)\cdot t)\;.

(163)

We now show further implications of these conclusions:

All iterates are convergent in TV and Duality Gap.

By definition of the set
\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
,
the fact that
w_{t}\in\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
for all
t\in[T]
implies that

1-\exp(-1/\delta)\leq p_{t}(1),q_{t}(1)\leq 1\;\;\text{for all $t\in[T]$}.

(164)

By the definition of
\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w)
from (
143
),
it then follows for all
t\in[T]
that:

\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w_{t})=|p_{t}(1)-1-\delta|+|q_{t}(1)-1-\delta|\leq 2\delta=\tfrac{2}{T}\;.

Here, the final equality is to due to the setting
\delta=1/T
.
Moreover, using the relationship between

\textnormal{DG}(w_{t})
and
\textnormal{TV}(w_{t})
from Corollary
B.6
,
and using the fact that
A_{\delta,\delta}\in[-1,1]^{2\times 2}
,
we further conclude for all
t\in[T]

that
\textnormal{DG}(w_{t})\leq\sqrt{2}\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w_{t})\leq 2\sqrt{2}/T
.
Thus it holds on this instance that

\textnormal{DG}(w_{t})\leq O\big(T^{-1}\big)\;\text{and}\;\;\textnormal{TV}(w^{\star},w_{t})\leq O\big(T^{-1}\big)\;\text{for all $t\in[T]$}.

All iterates remain far in KL.

We now show that the conclusions from (
159
)
imply that all iterates
\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})
remain
bounded below by a constant.
First, we show a constant lower bound on

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{0})
for all initializations
w_{0}\in\mathcal{W}_{\delta_{0}}
.
For this, observe that
\mathcal{W}_{\delta,0}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
,
and thus the bounds in (
164
)
also apply for any initialization
w_{0}=(p_{0},q_{0})\in\mathcal{W}_{\delta}
.
Then, using the definition of

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w)=\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p_{0})+\textnormal{KL}(q^{\star},q_{0})

from (
144
), we have

\displaystyle\textnormal{KL}(p^{\star},p_{0})

\displaystyle=(1-\delta)\cdot\log\big(\tfrac{1-\delta}{p_{0}(1)}\big)+\delta\cdot\log\big(\tfrac{\delta}{p_{0}(2)}\big)

\displaystyle\geq(1-\delta)\cdot\log\big(\tfrac{1-\delta}{p_{0}(1)}\big)\geq(1-\delta)\cdot\log\big(\tfrac{1-\delta}{1-\exp(-1/\delta)}\big)\geq\tfrac{2}{3}\;.

Here, the final inequality holds
when
\delta\leq 1/2
,
which is true by the setting of
\delta=1/T

for
T\geq 2
.
An identical calculation
also gives
\textnormal{KL}(q^{\star},q_{0})\geq 2/3
,
and thus by definition

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{0})\geq 4/3
for all
initializations
w_{0}\in\mathcal{W}_{\delta,0}
.
Further using that
T=1/\delta
,
it follows from (
159
) that
for all
t\in[T]
:

\displaystyle\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})

\displaystyle\geq\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{0})\cdot\exp(-40\eta^{2}\cdot\exp(-1/\delta)\cdot T)

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{4}{3}\cdot\exp(-40\eta^{2}T/\exp(T))

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{4}{3}\cdot\tfrac{1}{\exp(2)}\geq\tfrac{1}{6}\;.

Here, the penultimate inequality comes
from the assumption that
\eta\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
,
and since
T/\exp(T)\leq\tfrac{1}{5}
for all
T\geq 3
.
Thus we find on this instance that

\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{t})\geq 1/6
.

\blacksquare

K.2.2  Proof of Part 2

The structure of the proof is similar to that
of Theorem
5.1
.
First, we instantiate the payoff matrix
A

and the set of initializations as follows:

•

Set
A:=A_{\delta_{p},\delta_{q}}\in[-1,1]^{2\times 2}

from Definition
I.1

with
\delta:=\delta_{p}=\delta_{q}=\frac{1}{\exp(T)}
.

•

Initialize
w_{0}=(p_{0},q_{0})\in\mathcal{P}_{0}\times\mathcal{Q}_{0}
,
where:

\mathcal{P}_{0}=\{p\in\textnormal{ri}(\Delta_{2})\,:\,\tfrac{1}{2}\leq p(1)\leq\tfrac{3}{4}\}\;\;\text{and}\;\;\mathcal{Q}_{0}=\{q\in\textnormal{ri}(\Delta_{2})\,:\,1-\tfrac{\delta^{2}}{3}\leq q(1)\leq 1-\tfrac{\delta^{2}}{6}\}\;.

Let
\mathcal{W}_{0}=\mathcal{P}_{0}\times\mathcal{Q}_{0}\subset\textnormal{ri}(\mathcal{W})
,
and observe that
\mathcal{W}_{0}
has positive Lebesgue measure.
Moreover, for every
T\geq 3
,
we have
\delta<1/8
, and thus
all initializations
p_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
satisfy
p_{0}<1-\delta
.
On the other hand, note that

\mathcal{W}_{0}
does not contain
the uniform initialization
p_{0}=q_{0}=(1/2,1/2)
,
which is in contrast to
the proof of Theorem
5.1
.

We now proceed to (1) track the trajectory
of the dual OMWU iterates over the time horizon
T
,
which (2) will lead to a constant lower bound on the
duality gap that holds over all iterates.
In more details:

Bounds on Trajectory.

For every
t
, let
w_{t}=(p_{t},q_{t})\in\textnormal{ri}(\Delta_{2})

and
z_{t}=(x_{t},y_{t})\in\mathcal{Z}
denote the primal
and dual iterate of OMWU, and
let
\widetilde{p}_{t}=p_{t}(1)\in(0,1)
,
\widetilde{q}_{t}=q_{t}(1)\in(0,1)

and
\widetilde{x}_{t}=x_{t}(1)\in\mathbb{R}
,
\widetilde{y}_{t}=y_{t}(1)\in\mathbb{R}
.
Now under
A:=A_{\delta,\delta}
,
we can use Proposition
I.15

to crudely bound for any
t\geq 0

\widetilde{y}_{t+1}-\widetilde{y}_{t}=-\eta\delta(1-\delta-\widetilde{p}_{t}-(\widetilde{p}_{t}-\widetilde{p}_{t-1}))\geq-2\eta\delta\geq-\delta\;.

(165)

Here, the first inequality is due to

\widetilde{p}_{t},\widetilde{p}_{t-1},\delta\in(0,1)
,
and the second is by the assumption
that
\eta<1/2
.
Now under the initialization
q_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}
,
we have
\widetilde{q}_{0}\geq 1-\delta^{2}/3
.
By Corollary
I.14
,
this implies that

\widetilde{q}_{0}\geq 1-\tfrac{\delta^{2}}{3}\;\implies\;\widetilde{y}_{0}\geq\delta\log\big(\tfrac{1-\delta^{2}/3}{\delta^{2}/3}\big)\;.

(166)

Then using (
165
), it follows for
all
t\in[T]
that we can bound

\displaystyle\widetilde{y}_{t}\geq\widetilde{y}_{0}-\delta\cdot t\geq\widetilde{y}_{0}-\delta\cdot T

\displaystyle\geq\delta\log\big(\tfrac{1-\delta^{2}/3}{\delta^{2}/3}\big)-\delta\log\big(\tfrac{1}{\delta}\big)

\displaystyle=\delta\log\big(\tfrac{1-\delta^{2}/3}{\delta/3}\big)\geq\delta\log\big(\tfrac{1-\delta/3}{\delta/3}\big)\;.

(167)

Here, the second inequality comes from
applying the lower bound on
\widetilde{y}_{t}
from (
166
)
and using the setting
\delta=1/\exp(T)\implies T=\log(1/\delta)
.
Again by Corollary
I.14
, it follows
for all
t\in[T]
that

\widetilde{y}_{t}\geq\delta\log\big(\tfrac{1-\delta/3}{\delta/3}\big)\;\implies\;\widetilde{q}_{t}\geq 1-\delta/3\;.

(168)

Moreover, using the fact that
\widetilde{p}_{0}\leq 3/4<1-\delta
for
for all
p_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
, this further implies via
Part (ii) of Lemma
I.17

that all
\widetilde{x}_{t+1}-\widetilde{x}_{t}<0\implies\widetilde{p}_{t+1}-\widetilde{p}_{t}<0
.
Thus for all
t\in[T]
it holds that

\widetilde{p}_{t}\leq\widetilde{p}_{0}\leq 3/4<1-\delta\;.

(169)

We now derive the implications of the uniform
coordinate bounds of (
168
)
and (
169
) on the duality gap
of the iterates.

Bounds on Distance to Nash.

For
A=A_{\delta,\delta}
,
the unique NE
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})
of
A
is given
by
p^{\star}=q^{\star}=(1-\delta,\delta)
.
Then by the definition of
\textnormal{DG}(w_{t})

from (
142
),
and using the bounds
\widetilde{q}_{t}\geq 1-\delta/3

and
\widetilde{p}_{t}<1-\delta
,
we have for all
t\in[T]
that

\displaystyle\textstyle\textnormal{DG}(w_{t})

\displaystyle=(1-\delta-\widetilde{p}_{t})(1-\delta)+(\widetilde{q}_{t}-(1-\delta))(1-\delta)

(170)

\displaystyle\geq(1/4-\delta)\cdot(1-\delta)

(171)

\displaystyle\geq(1/4-1/20)\cdot(19/20)\geq 1/6\;.

(172)

The first inequality comes from the fact
that the second term in (
170
) is non-negative,
and also by applying the upper bound
\widetilde{p}_{t}\leq 3/4
.
The second inequality comes
from the fact that
\delta=1/\exp(T)
,
and thus
\delta\leq 1/20
for all
T\geq 3
.
As the bound of (
172
) holds uniformly
for all
t\in[T]
, we conclude on this instance
that
\min_{t\in[T]}\textnormal{DG}(w_{t})\geq 1/6
,
which concludes the proof.

\blacksquare

Appendix L
Details on Best-Iterate Convergence Rate in Duality Gap
for 2x2 Setting

In this section, we develop the proof of
Theorem
5.2
,
which establishes a fast uniform best-iterate
convergence rate upper bound in duality gap
for the
2\times 2
setting.
To restate the theorem:

See
5.2

Note that this result improves over the prior
best-known
O(T^{-1/6})
rate of
Cai et al., 2025

for the same setting.
This improvement is due to a refined proof
technique that leverages the new
analysis of OMWU in
KL
divergence
from Theorem
4.7
.
We give a brief comparison with the analysis
of
Cai et al., 2025
and a high-level overview of our proof here:

Comparison
with
Cai et al., 2025
.

We recall that the proof of
Cai et al., 2025
uses a
two-regime approach depending
on the magnitude of the time horizon
T

with respect to the minimum Nash coordinate

\delta=\min(\delta_{p},\delta_{q})
.
They show a best-iterate bound in duality gap
scaling like

\min(\delta,T^{-1/4}\delta^{-1/2})
.
Depending on whether
T\geq\delta^{-1/6}

or
T<\delta^{-1/6}
, this term always is
at most
T^{-1/6}
, which results in their bound.
For large
T
, their analysis relies on a
universal upper bound on the random-iterate
convergence in duality gap.
For small
T
, their analysis relies on
tracking the trajectory of the OMWU iterates
in the primal space.

High-level overview of proof.

In contrast to the proof of
Cai et al., 2025
, our proof uses a three-regime approach
that allows for much tighter bounds when

T
is large with respect to
\delta
.
In the largest regime, we use our new universal
last-iterate analysis of OMWU in
KL
divergence
(Theorem
4.7
)
to establish a fast
last-iterate
bound in
DG
.
For the moderate and small
T
regimes,
we directly track the OMWU trajectory
and obtain tighter best-iterate bounds
of roughly
O(\delta^{2})
and
O(\max(1/T,\delta))
, respectively.
Together, using the definition of the three regimes,
this leads to the overall
\widetilde{O}(T^{-1/2})

best-iterate bound in duality gap.

We give more details on these regimes and their
consequences in the proof overview in the following section.
However, we first make two additional remarks regarding
the assumption of uniform initialization,
and on the possibility of generalizing
our techniques to higher-dimensional settings.

Remark L.1

(On Uniform Initialization)
.

The proof of Theorem
5.2

assumes a uniform initialization
(similar to the results of
Daskalakis and Panageas, 2019
,

Wei et al., 2021
,
Cai et al., 2024
,
Cai et al., 2025
).
However, our proof also naturally extends to
a positive measure set of initializations
and leads to the same rate,
up to the leading absolute constants.
Note that such initializations
are all interior and near the uniform distribution;
as we showed in Part (ii) of
Theorem
K.1
,
there is in general no quantitative uniform convergence rate
when initialized near the boundary of the simplex.

Remark L.2

(On Generalizing to Higher Dimensions)
.

Even in the
2\times 2
setting,
the proof for obtaining the
\widetilde{O}(T^{-1/2})

uniform best-iterate convergence rate in duality gap
is intricate and involves understanding
the interplay between (a)
the trajectory of the OMWU iterates
and (b) the non-uniform rate of energy dissipation
over the iterates.
In the
2\times 2
setting, the low-dimensional
property of the effective dual space
(as detailed in
Section
I.2.1
)
allows for obtaining fine-grained control of
the iterate trajectory.
This helps for establishing
a phase transition in the rate of energy dissipation
when
T
is sufficiently large (see
Lemma
L.5
below).
While such a transition empirically holds
for higher-dimensional settings,
establishing an analogous bound
remains a challenging and open
technical question.
Moreover, controlling the trajectory of the iterates
in the
2\times 2
setting
also allows for proving that the primal iterates always enter
a region of the simplex with a sufficiently small duality gap,
even when far from Nash under a metric like TV distance
(see Sections
I.4

and
I.4.4
).
Thus, extending the result to higher-dimensions
likely requires some additional control
of the iterates (e.g., establishing the number of
steps spent in various regions of the primal
and dual spaces, as a function of the
location of the interior NE).
We conjecture that OMWU still obtains
a
\widetilde{O}(T^{-1/2})
uniform best-iterate
convergence rate in the general-dimension
setting, and we leave this for future work.

L.1  Proof of Theorem
5.2

In this section, we give the proof of
Theorem
5.2
. The proof uses
several intermediate components and
results that we outline here,
and which we prove in the later subsections.

Assumptions on NE.

First, we assume without loss of generality
that
A\in[-1,1]^{2\times 2}
is not a constant matrix.
Then by Part (ii) of Proposition
I.3
,
since
w^{\star}=(p^{\star},q^{\star})
is an
interior NE of
A
, then
w^{\star}
is the unique NE of
A
.
Let
p^{\star}=(1-\delta_{p},\delta_{p})\in\textnormal{ri}(\Delta_{2})

and
q^{\star}=(1-\delta_{q},\delta_{q})\in\textnormal{ri}(\Delta_{2})

for
\delta_{p},\delta_{q}\in(0,1)
.
In the remainder of the proof, we will assume without
loss of generality that

0<\delta_{p}\leq\delta_{q}\leq\frac{1}{10}
.
Otherwise, if
\delta_{p},\delta_{q}\geq\frac{1}{10}
,
then the universal last-iterate bound of
Theorem
4.7
,
together with Corollary
B.6
,
would directly give
\textnormal{KL}(w^{\star},w_{T})\leq O(T^{-1/2})

for all
T\geq 1
.

Assumptions on Initialization.
